CHAPTER I
NFAMILIAR harmonious music crowded into his ears
and made them alive, but the rest of him still felt
dead. When he attempted to raise his head from
his pillow and see where the music came from, his neck was
wooden and his eyelids were heavy and numb. He tried to
lift a hand to the aid of his eyes, but it also was weighted,
and he could only let it fall ponderously back by his side.
The music persisted-high-pitched voices and golden trum-
pets in the clouds, it seemed-and very slowly the aliveness
in his ears spread over his entire body. He tried doubtfully
to turn over in bed and found that he could. Then he tried
again to lift a hand to his eyes, and found he could do that.
With slight assistance his eyes came open. He was in a
strange room. Thin slivers of light that a mid-morning sun
squeezed in past the edges of the window blinds made the
wall paper into a crazy quilt and gave to sections of the
heavy plaster molding along the ceiling's edge all the dignity
of marble. Gradually everything became straight in his
mind: He was Giles Dabney, from the hills of Wiggam's
Glory, Ohio, and he was now somewhere in Cambridge, Mas-
sachusetts. He remembered that he had noticed the color
of the wall paper and the depth of that molding when he
arrived travel-weary the night before.
There was the music again! It was across the street-and
now quite earthly. With full piano accompaniment a bari-
tone and a tenor sang words that he could just make out:
"What-from ven-geance yet restrains-me?"
They stopped abruptly, and laughed when the accompanist
ran on a few measures ahead. Three or four others joined
3
4
THE HILLIKIN
in the laughter. The singers evidently were not very seri-
ous, not over-respectful. Yet a moment later they sang on,
their voices smoothly blending; and soon they were joined
by the other three or four. It was music more ingeniously
contrived and much more civilized than any that Giles had
ever heard in the Wiggam's Glory hills. It proclaimed to him
that he was in a new world. And he leaped from bed to put
himself in readiness to go forth and undertake discoveries.
The early style of the fixtures in the bathroom diverted
him. The top of the wash basin was a slab of mottled
marble, and the long, deep, sarcophagus-like bathtub was
tin. Or maybe it was not tin; maybe it was copper. Well,
anything was better than the washtubs the coal miners
back in his hills had to use for purposes of cleaning up.
As soon as he was in his clothes he went circumspectly
down the stairway into the brown hall that smelled not
unpleasantly of varnish. Just as he was reaching for the
door knob, his landlady appeared from the other side of
some maroon portières and cheered him with a pleasant
"Good morning!" She hoped he had slept well; she thought
his room exceptionally quiet, and believed his bed extra
long. She told him how to go over to the next corner and
then down past a spreading willow tree at the edge of
Divinity Field and thereby save several steps on his way
to Harvard Square and the College Yard.
With a prestidigitator's lightning transition she was con-
tributing other information. From her house northward it
was only two blocks to Somerville. Many would consider
this an objection, but as a matter of fact she did not mind
it in the least. The houses in this region were much newer
and consequently much better than the ugly nondescript
structures with French roofs over on Oxford Street and
Massachusetts Avenue. But of course he would find all of
Cambridge pleasant-despite the way the moths were eating
the elms-and he would think Harvard an imposing and
THE HILLIKIN
5
lovely old place. Her son had gone there, her daughter had
gone to Radcliffe, and already both were safely in the em-
ploy of Lee Higginson and Company. She knew by sight
the brilliant and able man who was destined to be Harvard's
new president, and on not fewer than two separate occasions
she had spoken in person to the magnificent old one. Long
ago-before she had met her dear husband back in the
eighties she used to go about with students herself. She
was an invited guest at the Commencement Spread of the
man now President of the United States-in fact, had danced
with him.
"But you must run along and get some breakfast," she
said as if there might be doubt about the matter. "And I
must get the rest of my morning's work done. We can finish
our conversation some other time."
Outside, everything was as unfamiliar to Giles as the
music had been. The houses were more massive than the
ones he had been accustomed to see, and some of them had
barnlike gambrel roofs. The streets were hard and clean,
though they were not bricked, and the curbstones were gran-
ite a material used back in Ohio only for tombstones.
Trees overhung the streets in lofty arches, and in the cool
substantial air there was the faintest odor of something
agreeably pungent and salty.
He wanted to lift up his arms and run and shout. In
truth, he did run a little-a great healthy-looking young
buck from the provinces. But when he noticed an amused
expression on the face of a smartly dressed young woman
who walked beside a learned-looking man with a spreading
red beard, he inferred that even running was a trifle out of
the ordinary in Cambridge. So he contented himself with
walking briskly and watching dark foreign men in stout
wagons who cried with un-American tongues: "Water-
melons! Fresh watermelons!" or "Old rags! Old rags!
Old rags!" or "Halibut! Fresh halibut! Fresh halibut!"
6
THE HILLIKIN
He had never heard of a halibut, but when he passed near
the wagons he believed it must be a fish.
When he came to the spreading willow at the end of the
street he was in a small sandy field with a high board fence
and woodland on his left, and the green roofs and red walls
of college buildings in every other direction. In the middle
of the field he passed some men who were loading sand out
of a pit into a high-wheeled cart which a grim-looking gray
horse supported with a saddle-like arrangement on his back.
A cart! Why did they not get a wagon with four wheels?
But in New England they seemed to order things differently.
At Harvard Square-which he found was a triangle-he
went into a tiny restaurant labeled "Successor to the Hole
in the Ground" and ate an inexpensive breakfast. Then
he dodged across the Square to explore the Yard. Gates
of stone and brick and ironwork were everywhere gates
like the triumphal arches in his Ancient History. They bore
lofty inscriptions, too. He thought he had better walk en-
tirely round the vast enclosure and see all the gates and all
the lettering in stone.
He was pleased to learn almost as soon as he started
that Harvard had been founded to save the New World from
the devastations of illiterate ministers of the gospel. The
institution had not been absolutely thorough in the crusade,
he thought, for back in Wiggam's Glory the human spirit
still suffered from illiterate ministrations. Through an-
other gate he saw a modest sun-dial on which some poet had
inscribed the adequate words, "On this moment hangs eter-
nity." He made his way on and on, reading as he went.
Eventually he found himself again on the Avenue, but far-
ther east. Above one gate he read: "Enter to grow in
wisdom." So there had been people who had borne in mind
just such needs as his!
He walked through this mighty portal, disturbed by a
vague feeling that he ought to take off his hat, and at length
THE HILLIKIN
7
got past the last wing of an alien granite building that
possessed many pinnacles but no discoverable front. An
erect old man with a birthmark on his face came along, and
Giles asked to be directed to the office of the president of
the university. The old man said he happened to be going
over in that direction himself, and obligingly offered to act
as guide.
They entered a gray building that was almost covered
with ivy. "Something always requires paint," the old man
said as he indicated that they were to climb a sleek granite
stairway, "and just now it happens to be the corridor be-
neath the president's office. So you will have to go through
the Faculty Room."
Giles had never seen so much magnificence. One great
bespangled chandelier hung almost low enough to touch
their heads as they crossed the room, and on the walls
everywhere above the unbroken line of deep, inviting chairs
there were portraits of dignitaries whose cut of clothes sug-
gested that they ranged all the way from the present moment
back to the time of Sir Walter Raleigh.
"Right in here," said the old man when they were once
again in a corridor. He put his hat away in one corner
of the office and begged Giles to be seated and explain
what it was that he wished to bring to the attention of the
president of the university.
He smiled a lofty smile, and Giles was not too much awed
to smile in return at the thought of being personally con-
ducted to the office of the president by no less a person
than the president himself. The president smiled again,
a little less sublimely, a little more intimately.
Something within Giles whispered that he ought not to
have troubled so great a man with his own obscure enter-
prise. "Oh, I only wished to find out about entering col-
lege," he explained in great modesty.
"I suspected as much," the president replied, and smiled
8
THE HILLIKIN
again as if he understood perfectly. "Young men of char-
acter are always coming to us, though they do not always
reach the president's office as soon as they arrive. But you
must certainly be aware that you are late. The autumn en-
trance examinations are over-or virtually so. I hope you
have not come far."
Giles felt his throat parching. He tried to swallow, but
his tongue would not stir. He tried again, and succeeded
to the extent of saying at last, "From Ohio."
"Oh, how unfortunate. If you had only written we could
have saved you all the expense and trouble."
Giles felt a tiny push of courageous blood in his veins.
"I am glad I didn't write, then. I wanted to come."
The president looked at him deliberately, sympathetically.
"Where did you prepare?"
Giles had never heard his case summed up in just that
manner before, but he thought he understood.
"In the Culbertson High School."
"Where is Culbertson? What is it near?"
"Near Centerville-and only sixty or seventy miles from
Columbus."
"I believe I have heard of Centerville. And we have
graduates in Columbus. But there were no Harvard men
teaching in your high school?"
"No," Giles answered; "he was from somewhere else."
"He?” the president asked, much more interested than he
had yet been. "Had you but one teacher?"
"Yes, just one."
The president took plenty of time for everything. At
last he said: "Your coming interests me greatly. How did
you happen to travel all the way over here when Ohio is
full of colleges, and many of them excellent ones? Who
directed you?"
"Nobody directed me. But two different men spoke about
THE HILLIKIN
9
Harvard. One of them was a Mr. Leacock, from Cin-
cinnati."
"And he was not the holder of a Harvard degree?"
Giles's eyes saw the damaged figure of the vagabond Mr.
Leacock after Bucky Tharp had pitched him head first out
of the Last Chance in a Saturday night brawl over at Bread-
less south of Wiggam's Glory, and he was glad to feel sure
that Mr. Leacock was not a Harvard man.
"No, I'm sure he never attended Harvard."
"Nor the other of the two?"
"No, I don't believe he ever did either. He peddled
patent medicines."
"We never place any restrictions on our graduates' occu-
pations," the president replied in great good cheer. "But
were they good medicines?"
He relaxed slightly from his upright position in his chair.
Then he sat calmly for a moment, his hands clasped in front
of him, his thumbs in motion, his sagacious eyes much alive.
"Come with me," at last he said.
They went again through the Faculty Room. "Long-
fellow!" Giles exclaimed under his breath when he recog-
nized one of the many portraits.
"Yes. We have had many men distinguished in their
day on our faculty."
After those words there was an awesome silence until
they entered an office downstairs.
"I have brought you a young man," the president ex-
plained to a rotund, agreeable professor who jumped up
from his desk as soon as they entered, "who has come all
the way from the hills of Ohio to enter Harvard, without
knowing about our entrance examinations. He seems to be
fairly mature, and I have reason to believe that he has
strength of will. Might we not admit him as a Special
Student? I'll leave him with you."
"It ought to be possible," the dean said when the presi-
10
THE HILLIKIN
dent had gone and he had heard Giles's story. "Of course
you're twenty-one years old."
"No, I'm only nineteen."
"Nineteen! Only nineteen! Then I don't see a ghost of
a show for you. We are shutting down on Special Students
just now. If a man is twenty-one, we are disposed to give
him a chance. A man is not always mature at that age,
but we often grant him the benefit of the doubt. When
he is not that old—”
The door opened ever so slightly, and a cheery, smiling
face peeped in. "Hello, there!" this unannounced visitor
called. Then when he saw Giles: "Oh, I beg your pardon.
I did not know you had begun to keep office hours yet. I'll
see you another time. We just came up from the country
this morning."
"No! Don't go! You're just the person I wanted to see.
This young man heard of Harvard way down in the wilds
of Ohio, and he packed up and came on here without know-
ing about our methods of admission."
The visitor stepped all the way in. He was a tallish man,
with shrewd, pleasant blue eyes and a schoolboy's head of
unruly hair, and he held a bulging green book-bag awk-
wardly in one hand and his hat in the other.
"Can't you admit him as a Special Student?" he asked
without a moment of hesitation.
"He's hardly old enough."
Giles did not shrink when this visitor gave him a quick
glance as though he had long known this kind of person.
"You certainly must be twenty-one. I am sure you look
as mature as I did when I was thirty. But I—I am not
certain that that would be sound reasoning to follow."
"He has read some Latin and some German," the dean
at the desk explained.
"And I hope some English," the other replied, and let a
THE HILLIKIN
11
kindly smile interpret the quality of his remark. "Tell us
some of the things you have read."
"Books?"
"Yes, books."
Giles saw the whitewashed shelves of books in the little
bedroom off the lean-to kitchen in the house where he had
grown up. He was afraid the books would not count for
much in this great testing, but he named them modestly—
books by Scott, Thackeray, Dickens, Maria Edgeworth,
Louisa M. Alcott, Hawthorne, Anthony Trollope, James
Fenimore Cooper, Emerson, Whittier, Poe, Tennyson, Jane
Austen-"
"Was your mother a teacher?" the visitor broke in. "It
would hurt nothing if all our seniors had read those books."
And he directed a merry twinkle toward his colleague.
"Yes," Giles answered, when at last there was oppor-
tunity, "my mother was a teacher."
"Then possibly you can write a straight sentence, too."
"I believe I can."
"Then how would it do"-with another glance of under-
standing toward his colleague "if he were to make out a
very full record of all that he has done and let us act upon
it. I don't see a great chance for him-do you?—but he
might somehow crawl in under those flexible words in the
catalogue, 'mature persons who give satisfactory evidence
of their fitness.""
"Yes, Mr. Dabney," and Giles jumped at thus being
addressed as a man-"I concur in that opinion. Suppose
you bring in such an outline by to-morrow afternoon so that
we can act upon it before the rush catches us—and you
can then know without unnecessary delay whether you
are to remain."
Out under the trees where the air was not so suffocating,
those words marched through his head in endless proces-
sion: "And you will know whether you are to remain!"
12
THE HILLIKIN
"And you will know whether you are to remain!" Here,
right at hand, was everything he wanted, and now perhaps
he would be adjudged unworthy of receiving it. They had
talked as casually about his case as if they had been choos-
ing a necktie.
As he followed the criss-cross paths of the Yard in im-
patient aimlessness, he felt rebellion welling up within him.
His grievance was not specific. The three men had no
doubt treated him with unaccustomed consideration. But
had they never been in a tight place themselves? Did they
know nothing of the task of the man who has to start from
exactly nowhere? Could they not understand that he had
come a long distance, and had spent a lot of money? But,
of course, for them it would not be a lot.
How could he ever go back to the hills with nothing to
show for his absence? He had promised himself a thousand
times that he would carry something important—many
things, in fact-to his father and mother back there where
for them there was no escape. And now already he was
facing the prospect of going back empty-handed. No, he
should prefer not to go back at all.
He found himself walking out of the Yard at the gate
bearing the inscription about the illiterate ministers. He
found himself following a street past a very old churchyard.
Then he passed a little church. A tombstone of a marker
declared it to be the oldest church building in Cambridge.
It had been built two hundred and fifty years before Wig-
gam's Glory had ever been thought of!
He passed an old elm in the middle of the street where
another granite tablet recorded that here George Washing-
ton took command of the Continental Army-or at least
some part of it-at the beginning of the Revolutionary
War. A yellowing leaf fluttered down outside the wrought-
iron fence, and Giles picked it up and slipped it into his
pocket. Think of having a leaf from such a spot as that!
THE HILLIKIN
13
He would send it home. It would be a curiosity in a region
where the only spots that had been touched by drama were
those under which ten men had been buried in a cave-in at
the Sparkling Diamond coal mine, or sixty had lost their
lives when the coal dust or the fire damp had let go-and
where not even such momentous places were ever marked
with memorial tablets.
He followed the winding avenue. He passed a great
spreading yellow and white house that stood back from
the street with giant elms on its flanks. He would have
recognized that house if he had come upon it in another
world. It looked precisely as it did in his school history
above the words, "Home of Longfellow." He could scarcely
believe that he had come upon it without being directed.
Yet there it was, in front of his eyes.
His mind was swept clean of everything except the poetic
reality of what he saw. If Hiawatha and Evangeline had
come down the walk from the house, they would have con-
stituted a perfectly reasonable sight.
Then the smothering fundamental thought clutched him
again. Perhaps he could not stay.
Who said he could not stay? Did those three big men
think he intended to leave a place where one could go
on the merest stroll and pass ancient churches, historic elms,
and the habitations of poets?
Flushed with desperation, he walked swiftly on and on
along Brattle Street. Then he followed a pleasant by-street
that also was bordered with expansive lawns.
As he hurried blindly in pursuit of his destiny, he passed
one house where a woman in an attractive traveling suit
and a hat less upright, less resistant than the ones he had
seen along Brattle Street was surveying the state of neg-
lect into which her lawn and shrubs and flowers had fallen
during the summer.
He hurried to her. "Don't you want me to take care
14
THE HILLIKIN
of your yard for you?" he asked as though she might be
on the point of awarding the contract to somebody else.
She looked at him with unfeigned interest. The thought
flashed through his mind that she was not a very old woman.
"Why," she said, assuming that he had come specifically
to apply for the job, "I hardly know. You see I shall need
some one. Have you references?"
"No, but I can take care of it. I'm a student at Har-
vard-or I hope to be."
"Well, that wouldn't increase your chances in the least,"
she replied, not unpleasantly, yet positively. "I engaged
a student to take care of things this summer-he was
supposed to be a trained gardener, too—but you can see for
yourself what has resulted. He knew nothing whatsoever,
to begin with. And then he ran off and went to Europe
on a cattle-boat."
"But I'll not run off. And what's more, I know some-
thing. We have a yard back home almost as big as this,
with almost as many flowers. And I laid out one a good
deal bigger than this for an old woman who is interested
in such things."
His speech, his unrestraint, amused her.
"Where is your home?"
"Ohio."
"Ohio? Goodness, I didn't know they had flowers way
out there."
"Well, they do once in a while."
She liked his understated afterthought, and looked him
over as if he were a specimen.
"If you were to try taking care of mine, you'd have to
remember, to begin with, that we have 'lawns' and 'gar-
dens,' not 'yards.'
"Then you mean I'm to have the job?"
""
"I might give you a trial—if your rates are not too high.'
"They'll not be. I'll work awhile and let you see how
THE HILLIKIN
15
you like it, and then you can pay me whatever you think
I'm worth."
She looked at him again with interest, and Giles thought
she was only about half as old as she had seemed before.
She was just a young woman, with fair, well-controlled face,
and gray-blue eyes that seemed to see.
"Why-yes-that ought to be satisfactory. When could
you begin-to-morrow morning? And since college doesn't
open for several days yet, couldn't you work most of each
day for a time? I don't fancy living in a jungle."
Yes, he could come to-morrow morning. He could work
most of every day, too. And he hurried away lest for some
unimagined reason she should change her mind.
Down the street a distance, he had to run a little. There
was no other adequate way of expressing his elation. He
had found a source of income on his first day in town! If
now he could only feel sure that he was to be in college-
That unfinished sentence brought him to a full stop by
a young elm on the edge of the sidewalk. People who
passed smiled at his obliviousness and wondered how an
unsophisticated outlander of this particular style had ever
found his way into Cambridge. But they did not trouble
themselves deeply. And Giles, after he had stood stock
still with his large head inclined in thought until he reached
a new desperate resolve to find some means of squeezing
into Harvard, went on his way down the street as little
troubled about the people who passed as most of them were
about him.
CHAPTER II
HE next morning he was awake when only the
"Cloop-cloop" of milkmen's horses disturbed the
TH
early Cambridge quiet. He got up, dressed, tip-
toed down a relaxed stairway that creaked and snapped
at every step, slipped out into a daylight that was yet
sunless, and hurried toward Harvard Square. In the tiny
restaurant he ate deliberately while the solitary waiter on
duty yawned, arranged the boxes of cigars in the show
case, and carried away a day's catch of wailing, agonizing
flies on a yellow sheet of sticky paper.
Just as Giles had expected, nobody was in sight when he
arrived at the overgrown lawn. Nor were there evidences
of activity in the house. Well, it was better to be early
than late. It was like rising for a day of honest toil in
Wiggam's Glory thus to be out with the sun. Anyhow he
did not want Mrs. Yes, now he had thought of that
on his way home: What was her name? She had inferred
that he knew.
He moved cautiously over the wet grass as if footfalls
on the turf might disturb her morning slumber. He esti-
mated how long it would take him to cut the tall grass;
he considered what he might do with the neglected annuals
and perennials; he studied with unobscured rural eyes the
possibilities of the lawn if it were rebuilt. The driveway
came over too close to the hedge on the south side.
When might somebody be expected to stir in the house?
He glanced at windows, but saw no one. In feigned casual-
ness he walked past the front entrance with an ear alert
for sounds. He heard none. But on the door he noticed
16
THE HILLIKIN
17
a bright name plate and ventured close enough to read.
"Aldrich,” it said. Then he noticed a name on the front
of the mail box-a name snipped from a card. "Mrs. Pres-
ton MacIntyre Aldrich," it read. So he was working for
Mrs. Aldrich. He was pleased to know her.
He saw a penciled note pinned above the glistening silvery
knob of the doorbell. It was addressed to "The Gardener,"
and it informed him that he would find the lawn mower,
the sickle, the weeder, the shears, and all other implements
just inside the one unlocked door at the corner of the studio
in the rear of the garden. He was not to run the lawn
mower until after nine o'clock.
Shortly after ten Mrs. Aldrich appeared in a bright in-
formal costume which suggested breakfast and which made
her look very youthful.
"So you found my note-before you gave the front door-
bell a pull, didn't you? I left one at the service entrance,
too, but I suspected that you might go to the front door."
She smiled brightly, a little maliciously, and Giles stood
corrected. Out where he came from, people were not so
fussy about doors. But of course he was not out there
now.
At twelve o'clock when Mrs. Aldrich looked out from
an upstairs window she was sure that he was going to work
for her a long time-unless he refused to do so.
He ate luncheon in the same tiny restaurant-beside a
stout expressman who spread his elbows wide on the lunch
counter while he waited for an order of ham and eggs, and
remarked to the friendly waiter that pretty soon now "all
these God-damned trunks will be pouring into the Square-
some of them as heavy as pianos." Giles wondered why it
was necessary for anybody to bring such heavy trunks to
college when there was nothing to do but study. But that,
he supposed, was something else he should have to learn.
When he was through with a brief afternoon turn on
18
THE HILLIKIN
Mrs. Aldrich's lawn he scuttled across Cambridge toward
Somerville, tried a hot bath in the long, deep, tin bathtub,
dressed in his good clothes, and marched in freshness to
the Yard to deliver his high school diploma, his program
of work done, and such other attestations of intellectual
maturity as he had been able to bring together in one after-
noon and one evening.
The agreeable dean was just leaving his office. "I had
about decided that you had abandoned your project," he
remarked as he ushered Giles in. "But you had not?"
"Not at all!"
Giles suddenly felt the need of positiveness of attitude.
The dean seemed several degrees cooler than he had seemed
when the president took Giles to him.
"But after I was in here yesterday I went out and found
a good job, and I've been working all day."
"Fine! Fine!" the pleasant dean exclaimed. His aca-
demic conscience wanted to add: "Why don't you just
work on at that and forget college?" But instead, his good
nature spoke in full voice: "I hope it doesn't turn out to be
useless."
"Mrs. Preston Aldrich had just returned from her vaca-
tion when I walked up that way," Giles explained as if he
had long known Cambridge and the Aldriches, “and found
her grounds in pretty bad shape, and I was right on the spot
to offer to fix them up for her-before college opened."
"Fine! Capital! Why, that's just first rate. I don't
know the Aldriches very well, but they ought to have some-
thing for you to do all autumn and maybe in the spring
again if you're admitted, that is, and if you know how
to do such things."
"Oh, I know how, all right!"
Giles thought if he could not speak positively about being
admitted, he could make up by being positive about the
lawn.
THE HILLIKIN
19
The dean cast a lively glance at him. Then he unfolded
the pages of handwriting which Giles had brought, and
read a little through the lower edge of his glasses.
"Yes. Um-m. Yes! Yes! This tells us what we shall
wish to know exactly." He read on. "Why yes, this is
bully." And he crumpled the pages back into their original
fold and tossed them upon his desk.
Giles immediately forgave him that reference he had made
the day before to "the wilds of Ohio."
"I only wish I could be more encouraging-I really wish
I could."
Giles tried to look him in the eyes, but could not. He
could only back away. He had to get out where there was
fresh air. He had to walk until motor activity gave him
the impression that he was going somewhere.
Often had he felt alien in the world, but never so much
as now when he saw on every side the things he hungered
for, and yet felt the restraining hand of some one who
told him they were not for him. Why were they not?
That was what he wanted to know after he had walked
many miles through devious streets. That was what he
still wanted to know the next morning when he got up heavy-
eyed and baffled, and went again to work.
Not a sound emanated from Mrs. Aldrich's house, though
once Giles saw a maid with a touch of white on the top of
her head hurry past a wide window.
But across the hedge, on the next lawn, there was activity.
A thirteen-year-old girl in a bright sweater kicked a foot-
ball in every direction. Once the ball came over the hedge
and bumped along on the grass near where Giles worked
at a bed of asters-"fall roses" out where he came from.
In a moment the girl was through a hole in the hedge.
She picked up the ball and then for a moment watched.
Then she made a slight movement as if she might go or
might say something, according to circumstances. Giles

THE HILLIKIN
looked up.
She stood friendly and expectant, with the
football under her arm. Then she came a little nearer.
"Cleaning them up, are you?"
"Yes, a little."
"Ours need it, too. But ours are not one-half as bad-
not one-fifth as bad-as these are."
Giles worked on with his head close over the flowers
and weeds. She made the fluttering little motion again,
and when he did not look up, she disappeared through the
hole in the hedge.
At noon he hurried to University Hall. "Have you de-
cided-?" he asked after the official-looking girl in the
outer office had told him to go right in.
"Decided? Why man alive, you only filed your appli-
cation yesterday. I should say-let me see, Thursday—
I should say, about next Tuesday afternoon." He smiled
a smile that was half amusement, half sympathy, when Giles
turned to the door too disappointed for speech.
Tuesday, did he say? How long would it take him, or
any man, to read a few pages of writing? He almost read
them through while he had them in his hand the day before.
But there were other ways of doing things, evidently.
Giles almost thought he heard somebody say: "If every-
body gets back from his vacation, we may get together
some time next week and decide whether your portion is
to be life or death. Whether it will be the one or the other
will depend largely on which one of us happens to speak
up first and influence the direction the thinking of the others
is to take."
Or who knew but what that official-looking dark-eyed
girl in the outer office read all the applications and decided
matters? She acted as if she ran the college.
While Giles worked the next morning and reviewed all
these questions that had come to mind the day before, the
girl in the bright sweater kicked her football over the hedge

Two girls right across the hedge there! Either of them

CHAPTER III
E WAS glad that Mrs. Aldrich wanted no one in
work-clothes disfiguring her landscape after twelve
o'clock on Tuesday. He needed plenty of time for
making himself ready before he marched down to University
Hall to learn what the gods there had decided.
At three o'clock when he came up to the building he felt
profoundly that this was the most important moment of
his life in truth, the only important one. More often than
not, destiny lurked in episodes and in people that nobody
thought of looking upon as dramatic. In this instance,
destiny took the form of an agreeable man in an ordinary
office who sorted out lives as a matter of daily routine. He
took his duties as lightly as the men who shoveled gravel
and sand through the screen over on Divinity Field. Some
of it went through. Some of it did not. Of course! But
who worried about whether a certain individual pebble went
through? The pebble could do the worrying.
He would mount these steps-he was mounting them
now he would enter the building, and the girl at the type-
writer who seemed to run the institution would say in her
most official manner, "You are to go right in." When he
was inside the smaller office a man who talked cheerily to
some one over the telephone would at last conclude the con-
versation, hang up the receiver, turn and see him and say
"Oh, yes!" and a few other words. And those few other
words would be as fateful as Waterloo or the Marne, for
they would make clear whether Giles Dabney, Hillikin from
Wiggam's Glory, Ohio, would remain in Cambridge, Massa-
chusetts, and profit by the wisdom brought together there
26
THE HILLIKIN
27
by generations of believers, or be shunted off in pursuit of
some other dream or in pursuit of no dream at all. The
history of the world might have to be rewritten as a result of
what the dean was to say.
By the time he arrived at the door of the office he was
too much agitated to go in. He must catch his breath and
calm his fluttering heart, and thus be able to hear the judg-
ment of the gods without revealing too much of his true
state of mind. He walked outside and stood on the top
step and tried to gain command of himself. A dignified
professor with a square black beard which was beginning
to show the least bit of gray came up the steps slowly, and
Giles, in order not to seem standing purposeless, turned and
reëntered the building and read on the bulletin board in
the corridor the many announcements of "last days for
receiving" applications for all sorts of concessions, privileges,
and monies.
He took off his hat, wiped the perspiration from his brow,
self-consciously felt to make sure that his necktie was on
straight, and opened the door. No official-looking girl sat
behind the typewriter, and the door to the inner office stood
ajar. He tiptoed over and peeped in.
"Oh, hello there! Come right in," the pleasant man called
to him. "I sent a note to you not ten minutes ago. But
now that you are here, I can explain our action a trifle more
in detail."
Giles felt all his veins grow empty and parched.
"We were very sympathetic toward your application, since
you seem to be in earnest, and especially since you have
come so far without having had complete information. You
are not twenty-one years old, and that makes it difficult to
classify you as a mature person meriting the privileges of a
Special Student. And of course we could not admit you
even provisionally to the freshman class. If we should open
the back door in any such fashion, we should have enough
28
THE HILLIKIN
freshmen in a year or two to fill a dozen colleges. So you
see, I know, how difficult it has been for us to reach a de-
cision. But we finally reached one, and it is this-"
to
Giles's body seemed to lose its material character,
merge with the air about him, so that he was unable to con-
trol his own limits or his own movements. Yet he could
hear the dean talking:
"We wanted to be fair. So we decided to leave the matter
in your hands. In other words, we are going to admit you
as a Special Student and let you prove to us yourself whether
you are mature enough to justify our confidence."
Giles felt his body becoming material again; his knees
trembled in sudden relaxation.
"But there will be obstacles. You will soon discover that
Special Students are not so designated because of any
special abilities most of them reveal, or because of any
special esteem in which they are held. Some professors, in
fact, object to having any Special Students in their courses.
And as you doubtless know already, Special Students do not
receive degrees."
Giles felt a rush of tingling warmth in his face. Who
wanted a degree? Wasn't it enough to be in, to get the
knowledge? But before he could speak the dean went on:
"Now-the question is, do you believe you can find four
teachers, or even three, who will admit you?"
"Why," Giles asked in a glow of recovery, "how many
will there be to pick from?"
"Oh-several hundred if you count all kinds. But you
must remember that some of them are not very sure pick-
ing."
"I'll find them! If I can't find three or four out of
several hundred, why then I don't deserve to stay."
"That's the way to talk! That's the way to talk! I
only wish I could reacquire some of your confidence."
THE HILLIKIN
29
Giles did not catch all that was implied in that last re-
mark. But what did that matter? He was in!
"Thank you!” he said with generous fervor.
"Don't mention it!" the dean replied informally, as if
he were not a dean at all. "I hope nothing arises to pre-
vent you from carrying your plans through. You will re-
ceive my note in to-morrow morning's mail, and it will pro-
vide you with a signed statement of what I have told you."
Giles descended the granite steps three at a time.
Some moments later he was aware that he was on the way
to his room. He must get that confirming note and keep
it. The dean might be killed by a street-car or something.
But why was he going to his room now? The note could
not arrive till the next morning.
He turned off at Broadway. He strode in majesty over
the rough brick sidewalks and smiled and smiled at the
buoying thought that at last he was connected with some-
thing held in esteem by many people, something which
might for once provide him with the benefit of the doubt.
He looked at everybody on both sides of the street. Why
were they all so grim? Hadn't they ever smiled at anything
in their entire lives? He wanted to greet them, to say
"It's a pretty fine day," but nobody seemed receptive.
At length he came to the top of a low hill. A Greek
temple of an apartment house dominated the scene. It was
good to see the expanse of white columns. It was good to
see the silky-looking white curtains at the rows of windows.
It was good to see the street itself stretching down the other
side of the hill and on and on toward Boston. It was good
to see anything that might now be seen in Cambridge.
Cambridge was now his city. Nobody could keep him
from enjoying it. Here it was, all about him. The histori-
cal spots, the tarry odor of Harvard Square in the noonday
sun, the Irishmen working in unparalleled leisure on the
Common as he passed, the sunsets over the Charles, the
30
THE HILLIKIN
fringes of dark-eyed Italians everywhere, and all of Harvard
itself, or at least some breath of it-these were now to
be included in what he could call his.
If he could see far enough away to the westward there
-across Massachusetts and across New York and across
Pennsylvania and a strip of Ohio-he could see Wiggam's
Glory. He looked. He could see! He saw it all-its over-
grown, neglected hills, its abandoned coal tipples, its tumble-
down Company Rows, its frowsiness, its griminess, its un-
limited means of human degradation. And there his father
and mother still struggled.
He must go to his room, now that it was safe to tell them,
and write them a long letter about his plans.
How magnificent Memorial Hall looked this afternoon!
For a week he had adjudged it a somewhat ugly thing. But
this afternoon it was not ugly. "Old Mem" he had heard
some of the advance guard of students call it. That was
how he felt about it himself. He must practice "Old Mem"
until it slipped from the tongue casually.
The workmen in Divinity Field were away with a load
of sand. Crows whisked in fright from the low trees at the
near edge of the field, set up a mighty clamor as soon as
they felt safe, and did not stop either their flight or their
noise until they were well in the woodland beyond the high
board fence. He liked their black, ungracious bodies. He
liked their wild noise. They reminded him of the morning
back in Ohio when he and Beulah Lettinger, after a wild
dance on the hilltop at sunrise, had been startled by the
crows.
That woodland-what was it doing there in the middle
of the city? Was it not about time to find out? Yes, after
what the gods in University Hall had reported, he thought
he felt ready to look into things a little-his future envi-
ronment.
He could barely put a hand over the top of the boards.
THE HILLIKIN
31
But he grappled and struggled, like a turtle on the side of
a smooth rock, until he succeeded in pulling himself up.
Then he sat for a time on the thin edge of boards with his
feet on the flat upper railing and surveyed the woods. The
crows had taken refuge in some lonely-looking evergreens
toward the top of a hill no higher than the one crossed by
Broadway. In the intervening space, maples and beeches
and oaks towered green and dark close over him against
great soft white clouds high in the heavens to the eastward
where they caught the full brightness of the afternoon sun.
At his feet, and as far on as he could see under the trees,
songbirds darted about in September quiet among the clumps
of wild-rose bushes.
Since he was on top of the fence, he could see no good
reason why he should not jump down on the other side and
make further explorations. He was aglow with a sense of
adequacy, and wanted to plunge into something unknown
and lose himself in discovery.
He walked in open spaces under silent trees. He crept
cautiously among the closely-grown clumps of rose bushes.
He stood from time to time with mouth open and ears alert,
but heard nothing save an occasional distant screech of
street-car wheels on a corner.
He came to a winding road. Was he in somebody's pri-
vate grounds? While he hesitated at this question, and en-
joyed the country feel that the woods afforded, he caught
sight of a tall man rounding a curve in the road near the
top of the low hill. The man came striding down the drive-
way with his hat swinging at the end of a long arm. His
high, angular face looked straight ahead rapturously. As
he came on down, Giles noticed that his forehead seemed
all the straighter because he parted his iron-gray hair up
loosely in the middle. The ardor of his face, the mental
glow expressed in the easy rhythm of his movement, the
contour of his strong but slenderish body, all suggested free-

dom. Giles had not meant to gape at him, yet there he stood

"I never knew I existed till one year when I was in col-

"One morning he opened a book and read. Not that I
hadn't heard professors read before, heaven knows! But
when he read, it was not just the Old Man, but Dante, and
Beatrice, and Italy, and hell and heaven, and life without
limits. It was only a short passage-just a snatch, in fact
-about the New Life. But at the end of that hour when
I pushed out through the double doors with the rest of the
mob, I knew what a New Life can be.
"I ran all the way across the Yard, side-swiping and jos-

THE HILLIKIN
as if they might be meaning not to reprint him. When one

They stood at the gate for a time as he talked, and then

"Up to that time the world had been only one grand and

"Before I left college I read all of Dante. Since then
I have managed to read most of him every year. I don't
read just to enjoy Dante-though that would be enough—
but to live again that experience of coming upon a new

"A new intelligence doth-’
"But I'm afraid you think I rave. And the Old Man
would be the last person in the world to want me to do that.
He talked, you know, about divine balance. He provided

They came up toward the long house on the top of the

"When I came up here this afternoon," at last the stranger

draymen cracked their whips and shouted at their horses,

They moved off together down the driveway. Only the

Giles moved breathlessly under the trees lest he disturb

with silence than when he had come over only a short while
ago. He stopped occasionally and took panting breaths
while he experienced the silence and tried to comprehend
the new world about which the stranger had spoken.
And he himself was to have teachers like that!
He climbed the high board fence. The men were back in
the sand pit again; and a score of dark-eyed children from
the edges of Somerville were climbing to the low branches
of the great willow at the end of the street, and swinging
to the ground with gleeful screams and shouts. Altogether
the world seemed once more an active place.
So Giles hurried homeward, yet not too rapidly, for he
wanted to dream of great professors who would welcome
him into their courses and reveal to him the glories of a
new heaven or at the very least, a new earth.

CHAPTER IV
ILES bent low over his notebook and tried to record
what Professor Etheredge Blount was saying. Pro-
fessor Blount sat high on a square little dais, shuf-
fled the materials of his lecture with a nervous left hand,
and chirruped away like a defiant sparrow. He had now
been lecturing for more than a month, but he was still de-
fining the limits of the course, and reading letters from titled
English women who appreciated his contributions to un-
trampled corners of the field of knowledge. He was not
unshakably certain that he was a distinguished person.
What was more, he knew that his students felt less certain
about the matter than he did.
Professor Blount seated students alphabetically. Toward
the end of the year when a professor was supposed to know
some of the members of his courses-and Professor Blount's
courses were always small-he did not like to read the name
of some student from the printed slip and then suffer the
embarrassment of looking toward the wrong part of the
room for a response. He had discovered, too, that students
seated miscellaneously sometimes changed their voices and
answered for absent neighbors. It was better to have them
seated alphabetically.
So Giles Dabney sat beside H. Pearsall Dwyer, a pink,
well-massaged sophomore who was a connoisseur in sea-
foods, Turkish baths, and silk underwear. While Giles
tried to get the point of a letter from Lady Somebody-or-
other commending Professor Blount for a recent article in
which he had simply demolished Professor Somebody-else
of Oxford, H. Pearsall drew from his pocket a strange-
36
THE HILLIKIN
37
looking knife and opened it. Its one blade was very long
and very slender.
"Paid old Henry the locksmith five dollars to make it
for me," he whispered to Giles, and proudly studied the
long blade. "I'm going in farther than anybody yet." And
with a smile of victory on his face, he began to probe
cautiously into the edge of the thick plank desk where
generations of undergraduates before him had carved
deeply, "To Hell with Yale."
During the next lecture period he tried just as bravely to
make notes in Professor Phinney's course while fellow stu-
dents on all sides read French novels concealed behind note-
books, or wrote their names over and over forty or a
hundred times in India ink at the top of a blank page and
waited for a pause in the professor's lecture. He lectured
by ranting monotonously, like a sophomore orator. Any-
where past the middle of the class period, if he paused, his
students shuffled their feet noisily as if they understood
that the lecture was done. In order to obviate as much
embarrassment as possible, the professor therefore grad-
ually increased his speed as he approached the half-hour
mark, and by the time the bell rang at the end of the hour
he was shouting along at the top of his voice without daring
to pause for breath.
Giles tried with no less conscience to extract something
of consequence from Professor Rowan's course, but with
little more success. The course was a large one that per-
sisted in seating itself miscellaneously. Roughly the stu-
dents were divided into those who did not like the course
but sat up front where they could hear in order to reduce
the chances of flunking, and those who preferred flunking
to sitting up front. One day when by hazard Giles sat
precisely half-way between the professor and the door and
had been able to catch only occasional remarks about some-
thing that was or was not "constitutional," Elliott Keyes,
38
THE HILLIKIN
next to him, slipped a notebook over for approval. One
page had been neatly designed as a New England gravestone,
and bore the inscription: "Here lyes ye body of Elliott
Keyes, done to death by Professor Rowan."
Were all the great professors at Harvard dead? It was
difficult for Wiggam's Glory to make Harvard out.
But Giles managed to keep a place in the multitude. If
he could not understand this cultivated world into which
he had cast himself, at least he could keep on trying. He
missed no classes. He went to every kind of semi-public
meeting of fellow students where he was welcome-and to
some where he was not. From time to time, after he had
spent a long evening in going over his lecture notes and in
reading supplementary assignments, he sat back in his
chair out of the bright light of his green-shaded oil lamp
and tried to make something orderly, something intelligible,
out of all the seeming confusion that surrounded him.
He thought he began to understand the students first. For
instance, there were the ones that in the secrecy of his
own provincial self he called the Pekingese. They were
not numerous, but they were socially important. They
looked well kept. And they smiled. They forever smiled.
They smiled in a superior, irritating fashion. They recog-
nized the physical presence of others they had to do so
when the snow was deep and the board walks were narrow.
But mentally these others were non-existent. They could
never come to know what the Pekingese knew; they could
never come to feel what the Pekingese felt. Pekingese at-
tracted Pekingese. And in one another's reassuring pres-
ence they were hermetically separated from the mental
world of the commonalty.
They were devoted to the bric-a-brac of life. They scru-
pulously pronounced a new musician's name according to
the latest mode, and dug up the details of his amours. They
made witticisms about some distinguished woman writer
THE HILLIKIN
39
whom nobody but themselves had ever heard of, and con-
sidered whether she were not superior to Sappho. They
examined the fringes of religion, regretted the many short-
comings of Jesus of Nazareth, and reached the conclusion
that when anyone looked at the matter as they did, there
was not much left, anyhow. They went into detail about the
superiority of one Italian hotel's menu over that of another.
They regretted that some unvisited street in some unvisited
town was losing its atmosphere-though they never thought
of creating atmosphere by originality in any new street in
any new town.
They were interested in polite life. They wanted to dis-
cuss questions that would never bring them to the bloody
heart of any matter. They wanted to busy themselves with
matters that would leave the world no more exciting whether
they reached a right conclusion or a wrong one. If some-
body-Giles Dabney, for instance-raised an essential ques-
tion, such as whether a man was to go under or to survive,
they only smiled at one another as if to say, "Clearly, this
person is not one of us!"
Then there were the Ants. Every morning they poured
into Harvard out of the ant-hill-out of Dorchester, out
of Newton Centre, out of Arlington and Belmont and
Charlestown and Chelsea and Melrose and Somerville and
Roxbury and Brookline and Quincy and Allston and
Brighton and Lynn and Salem. From a hundred tunnels
they emerged into the sunlight-Anglo-Saxon, Semite, Latin,
and Celt-basked in the brightness for an hour or two, and
then hurried back into the ant-hill and busied themselves
with ant-hill affairs so completely that by the next morning
they had forgotten the sunlight of the day before.
And then there were the Wolves. They came with sharp
eyes from everywhere-from North Dakota, from Alabama,
from West Virginia, from Kansas, from Maine, from Cali-
fornia and Nevada, to say nothing of stragglers from Japan,
40
THE HILLIKIN
Bulgaria, New Zealand, and Peru. They had long starved
for a life more satisfying than the one they lived, and others
of their kind had directed them to Harvard.
They were ravenously hungry-often hungry in body as
well as in spirit. They found the cheapest rooms in the
college dormitories. They found still cheaper rooms in Cam-
bridge houses far from Harvard Square, yet within walking
distance. They pushed out along Broadway beyond the
hilltop, along Cambridge Street past the Holy Ghost Hospi-
tal, along Kirkland and Oxford into Somerville, along Mas-
sachusetts Avenue and Avon Hill Street and Raymond and
Garden into North Cambridge. They filled hall bedrooms;
they filled front parlors separated from the families of hon-
est carpenters and journeyman plumbers by only a double
door or portières. They boarded at Randall Hall because
the service there was à la carte plus combinations, and they
could effect economies. When they were obliged to make
greater economies still, they created demands for new res-
taurants along the Avenue northward and neglected Randall
Hall. Then they began to walk to Porter Square and save
yet another nickel on a meal. For enough nickels saved
meant remaining at Harvard.
They came as undergraduates; they came as graduates of
other colleges to study law or business or architecture or
engineering or literature or theology. If they were mar-
ried, their wives borrowed the use of one another's washing
machines and kept one another's babies on Symphony nights
at Memorial Hall. Some came driven by the sheerest self-
ishness, some by accumulated family ideals of righteousness
or enlightenment, some by the imperious clamorings of a
creative mind that was no more to be explained than a sport
in biology.
They saw the Ants daily, and scarcely gave them a
thought. But the Ants saw them stalking mountain high
over everything, and recognized in them an overwhelming
THE HILLIKIN
41
power. Of the Pekingese the Wolves knew nothing except
what they saw when they skulked wide-eyed through the
region of the Gold Coast or through glittering ways in Bos-
ton hotels where jaded débutantes pretended to enjoy the
thrill of coming out. The Pekingese bore with the Wolves
-smilingly. They found a certain pleasure in being the
quintessential part of an institution that attracted these
rough beings from afar, though they sometimes professed
fear that the Wolves might some day overrun the institu-
tion and make it into what it already professed to be-a
national university.
Giles was not overawed by these various Harvards. He
liked to walk through the Yard and bump against coonskin
coats that were unnecessarily ponderous, against hurrying
men who only a few breaths before were eating breakfast at
home with mother and sister, against lanky youths with
long-distance eyes from the Rocky Mountains.
And then there were all sorts of men who did not seem
to fall readily into any of these groups. There must be
more Harvards than he had ever surmised. Yes, of that he
began to feel sure.
Perhaps if he should hunt long enough, he might find as
great a diversity of professors-and some good ones. With
this hope, he began a persistent search. One day at the
end of a class period he walked across the Yard just behind
an Anglo-Saxon king and an Elizabethan gentleman who
wore a Shakespearian beard and spoke an un-American
language. He could not help hearing what they were say-
ing; and he decided at once that unless they taught some
subject in which he felt sure he could develop no interest,
he meant to enroll in their courses at the beginning of the
next college year. He often saw a dark tragic actor who
seemed to be a professor, and decided that he wished to
investigate his courses, also. He was sure the man would
be interesting, for although he looked like an actor he never
42
THE HILLIKIN
seemed to be acting. At a certain hour he passed regularly
still another man whom he thought he ought to know more
about. This was a fair-faced young scholar of thirty-five
who walked along calmly and modestly as if he lived in a
world that deserved great respect. Giles Dabney liked him
all the better, too, when he one day saw the young pro-
fessor's wife meet him in the Yard to go to some appoint-
ment that they had made. Anybody who knew how to pick
out a wife like that must be a good professor.
Giles heard students speak from time to time about their
preferences, too. One night he trailed four or five juniors and
seniors up the resounding stairs to Professor MacLaurin's
room-Professor Scott MacLaurin, according to the college
catalogue, but Old Scott, according to the many students
who looked upon him with affection. Professor MacLaurin
left the impression with strangers that he was an austere
and crusty recluse, yet he was a magnet who drew to his
rooms all kinds of men.
"Right there by the fireplace, young man," the professor
commanded when Giles ventured into the great living-room
and found a dozen students already sitting before the fire.
"That is the only chair left. Sit there."
Giles sat close against the side of the fireplace until the
fresh supplies of coal that had been heaped upon the little
grate blistered the side of the chair and whirled cloudlets
of painty odors to Professor MacLaurin's nostrils.
"Damn you, man," the professor exclaimed in great glee,
"you must be Scotch to hold the fort like that!"
He laughed a resonant, joyous laugh, and all the students
laughed with him. Giles felt his face growing hotter than
the fire had made it.
Still he was glad he was there. For the conversation
encompassed a world he had learned little about, even in his
courses. In the presence of such an oracle as Professor
MacLaurin, many of the students were moved to think all
THE HILLIKIN
43
sorts of untrammeled thoughts, and sometimes to express
them with startling clarity. Once or twice Giles was almost
moved to join in the conversation himself. But instead he
sat very still and listened. It was better, after all, just to
hear Professor MacLaurin and to feel the great empty spaces
of his own mind filling up.
Professor MacLaurin told stories for an hour or more
about Turgenev and Maupassant and Hardy and Barrie
and Meredith and Kipling and Conrad. He talked with
glowing illumination about orchestras and musicians and
theaters and actresses and plays. Giles had never known
that the world contained so many interesting people, espe-
cially actresses. The ones that had come to the Strand in
Centerville, Ohio, had always been a pretty threadbare lot.
Old Scott had known all the great ones. He explained why
one was greater than another, and with words that had
sharp edges of meaning he spoke of how one ability dif-
fered from another. He knew musicians, too, and what they
had composed. He only regretted that he was going to be
out of town on Thursday and miss Tschaikowsky's Sym-
phonie Pathetique.
Giles felt that in two hours the darkness and the cold of
the universe had been pressed back an immeasurable dis-
tance.
When the rest rose to go, he who had remained speechless
rose to go with them. But Old Scott restrained him with a
friendly stentorian command: "It is your turn to carry the
chairs back into the other room. So you are to tarry five
minutes-but not a damned minute longer.”
Giles liked the firm speech. It possessed the required
poetic reality. It brought to mind old Genevieve back in
the Ohio hills who told the Coal Company to go to hell
when she did not choose to have her fields disfigured with
tipples and gob piles and red sulphur water.
He needed only a minute for carrying the chairs back.

CHAPTER V
HE maddening promises of March quickened Giles's
untamed spirit. In such days, who wanted to
bother with a notebook or a note-bound professor?
He wanted to encounter something that was not yet in final
form. He wanted to meet writers who were still writing;
he wanted to see actors and especially actresses who still
could cause the people in the theater to forget the mud that
had been splashed upon them in the street; he wanted to
hear musicians who composed their own music. That would
be romance—or, if you preferred, reality. What you called
it seemed to depend on whether you saw it from Wiggam's
Glory or somewhere else.
Anyhow, he was going to hear an orchestra this evening.
He had a preliminary celebration by eating a full twenty-
one-cent combination dinner at Randall Hall. He continued
the celebration by reading in a pleasant book, instead of
studying. Then a half-hour before time, he slipped into his
overcoat and trudged away in the freezing snow. It was
cheering to have something hazardous under one's feet.
A line of automobiles and cabs stretched along the curved
driveway up to the north entrance of Memorial Hall. He
walked circumspectly up the other arc of the driveway,
trembling with joyous excitement. But he hesitated to push
into the crowd. The men were in evening dress which
heavy overcoats did not wholly conceal, and the women were
in satiny wraps and soft-looking furs.
"Oh, goodness!" one woman exclaimed to somebody near
her. "I believe Reggie has all the tickets. When do you
suppose he will be here?”
47
48
THE HILLIKIN
Giles whirled and ran. His ticket! He had forgotten to
get it from the drawer of his desk.
Wasn't it lucky he had started a half-hour ahead of time?
And wasn't it a good thing that he had learned how to run
when he was growing up in the hills?
On the way back when he had the ticket snug in his
inside coat pocket he broke through the soft ice at a crossing
and went more than ankle deep in the water. But what did
that matter? He was on his way to a Symphony!
In the transept of the hall he found himself caught in a
swaying jam of well-dressed, unafraid people. He towered
too far above the level of heads and shoulders. Never had
he been so fully aware that he was from Wiggam's Glory.
He was glad nobody troubled to look at him more than
once.
The pleasant people made all sorts of family inquiries of
one another. They signaled across shoulders and between
heads to friends at a distance as if they all were accus-
tomed to see one another from time to time, but not too
often. The space stretching upward from the white tablets
of heroic names at one's shoulders till it was lost in the
shadowy groined arches of oak echoed with so many cheer-
ful, well-modulated living voices that little was left of the
lingering voices from the past.
At last Giles was in the Theater and was led by a
stiff usher to a seat in the first balcony. A solid, confusing
array of white shirt fronts and bare shoulders completely
surrounded him. He was afraid to look; yet he looked-
until individual fronts and shoulders and faces began to dis-
engage themselves. The faces seemed happy as they buzz-
ingly talked together. They seemed to be much at home.
Giles did not see one face known to him. Yes, there
was the president. And there were two or three deans-and
Professor Etheredge Blount. Professor Blount looked about
as if to say, "But why did so many of you trouble to come
THE HILLIKIN
49
when you are incapable of understanding what it is all

moth. Close behind her followed a stubby man with much
white shirt front and with a very bald, very serious head.
And high above the stage, looking directly down, were
a hundred or more students. They looked down with lively
faces, and Giles felt comforted. If he sat in magnificent
isolation, he also sat in the reassuring presence of some of
his kind.
A broad-faced student with honest eyes and spreading
friendly mouth slipped into the seat next to him. Giles
knew this must be the Cherub.
They did not speak, but they oscillated a little, like neigh-
borly bodies. After a moment the man leaned over. "Did

"Yes."
"In luck, aren't we?"
"Should say so!"
"Ever been to one?"
"No."

"Vermont."
"I'm from Ohio."
"My name's Kinloch."

They squirmed a kind of "How do you do?" that was cut

There was something unworldly and rhapsodic in such a
display of men behind French horns and viols and harps.
The other instruments were more of the earth; the chaos

THE HILLIKIN
What followed after the conductor marched out on to the

The Cherub read in his own copy.
"He's dead!" he whispered, and smiled. "That's one

"Yes, and he opened Carnegie Hall in New York. I saw

In the intermission at the conclusion of the symphony,
they walked about in the transept and tried not to feel
obvious. They were not wholly successful, and came back
to their seats early.
Giles had been in that seat before. It was beginning to
seem like home to him. And was he not somewhat related
to what he saw about him? There was Mrs. Aldrich. Had
he not dressed up her jungle of a garden for her? She had
a way of looking younger and younger. At first he had
supposed her to be about thirty-five-almost an old woman.
Now she seemed to be only about twenty-seven.
Her husband reached back of her and pulled her wrap
up over the edge of the seat and across the top of one of
her shoulders. Think of touching a shoulder like that!
And think of taking both of those shoulders very close in
one's arms. Evidently her husband thought very little about
such matters. He seemed old and serious.
THE HILLIKIN
51
During the Suite the Cherub became much alive. A half-
dozen times he exclaimed, "I've got to take Music 4. I can
see that."
He and Giles walked homeward together, at ease in their
own honesties. They confessed that they had come to
Harvard with matchless dreams.
In the quiet of his room, Giles knew that he must hear
an orchestra again soon. Still if he did not, this one he
had heard would last a lifetime. It echoed softly in his
ears. It rose to a deafening crescendo. It passed like a
glorious cinema before his eyes. It surged in his blood
until he was ten times more alive than he had ever known
a man could be.
He threw on his coat and hurried out into the blue, glit-
tering night. He must walk alone and keep on experiencing
just what he now experienced. This was the way a man
ought to feel from the time he was born until he dropped
dead.
He walked back through Divinity Avenue-out in the
middle of the crunching street-and on toward the Delta.
Memorial Hall was as deserted and forgotten as monuments
usually are. But it was still there, with Bossuet and
Chrysostom and all the other worthies in stone looking
awkwardly down from their places round Sanders Theater.
The clock high in the nondescript tower startled him by
beginning to hammer out eleven resounding strokes.
He walked through the Yard. One of two men who
passed was saying in his own defense: "But hell, after all,
it's nothing but some buildings and some trees and some
professors, is it?" Yes, Giles thought it was. Something
that was not literal shone from the windows in Hollis and
Stoughton and all their less seemly neighbors. The bright-
ness had overtones for anybody who had ever seen Wig-
gam's Glory. So had the Symphony. There was something
great and symphonic about life itself if one could only find
52
THE HILLIKIN
it. That was one thing he was not going to let anybody
knock out of him. Against all the petty short-circuits and
groundings that an artificial sophistication could contrive,
he meant to be adequately insulated.
He crossed the Avenue and wandered along the Gold
Coast in sublime solitude. Four or five students tumbled
from a doorway, and one of them bumped into him. He
was decent, and turned to beg pardon.
"Oh, hello, Dabney! I didn't mean to crash into you.
We're just off for a little relaxation."
Giles was not disturbed by this unexpected encounter
with H. Pearsall Dwyer, who did not wish to forget how
things tasted. Nor was he disturbed when he walked on
through the narrow streets down toward the Charles, and
the young Italian gentry of the neighborhood, out of work-
clothes for an entire evening, talked with vivacious swagger
and jostled him the least bit unnecessarily; nor by the dark-
eyed girls who suddenly tried to assume ladylike airs when
they saw him, as if they were accustomed to welcome the
late wanderings of students. Now that he had found a
level for his life, he meant not to let anything haul him
down from it.
The next day immediately after a ten o'clock class he
went to Boston. He had discovered the evening before at
Memorial Hall that his overcoat did not look enough like
the others. Since the sun was warm, he went without his
old coat. He would wear the new one.
As he walked up through Bromfield Street with the new
coat on, he felt that Wiggam's Glory was very far away.
In a shop where a red flag was displayed, an auctioneer
sold Japanese tea sets and vases. Vases! Wouldn't his
father open his eyes at that his father whose one dream
was to make a vase that suited him?
He stepped inside.
"Hello, there!" a woman said, right at his chin. "Saw you

at the Symphony last night. You are getting to be quite

on her. Lots of women-and I suppose lots of men-go
forth to recapture youth and its prizes."
She sat in silence for a moment. "But goodness, I mustn't
let myself get philosophical."
She reverted to symphonies. She glided easily to plays.
By the time she had eaten her salad, she had fallen into
pleasant chit-chat about college. She was sure there was
no other experience to equal it. "So happy, so irrespon-
sible"
THE HILLIKIN
55
After an agreeable while, they were on the street again
and Mrs. Aldrich was saying that if it were not for his
studies, she would take him shopping just to let him see what
warlike qualities the present day requires in a woman. But
he had better run along, she supposed. And she must.

CHAPTER VI
OON Giles was evaluating all things by their capacity
to save him from slumping into passivity and color-
lessness. From the simplest experience he sought to
extract a lyric essence.
One Saturday midnight after he had visited Old North
Church in the afternoon, he tramped his way to Lexington
over the route of Paul Revere. He meant to experience
some of the glow of the original journey. He returned on
a slow but moving early-morning street car. His legs ached.
His body sagged. He congratulated Paul Revere on having
had a horse.
But the next day or the next-he felt better. And
he would always remember the ride of Paul Revere. Already
he could smile a dull smile at his own absurd experience.
It had become history. So he ventured to other spots
where tablets announced that anything of importance had
taken place. He climbed Bunker Hill-it was not much
of a hill-and he climbed the monument. He visited the
Navy Yard and saw the Constitution. He went down to
the Charles and "stood on the bridge at midnight." He
went to Salem and visited Witch Hill, and to Plymouth
and visited Plymouth Rock. He went to Concord and
slept one Saturday night in the scrubby wild woods on the
shore of Walden Pond and thought he got a taste of what
Thoreau professed to enjoy. In any event, he heard plenty
of owls, and he wondered how Thoreau dealt with skunks.
He and the Cherub had joint enterprises, too. Every
week they went to Boston and paid a quarter for a rush
seat in Symphony Hall. The Cherub wanted to understand
56
THE HILLIKIN
57
the music; Giles wanted to repossess the rapturous feeling
that all life was something symphonious. On occasion, too,
they made journeys of exploration to outlying regions.
Once, after they had tramped about in the Newtons all
afternoon and had arrived at the conclusion that probably
nothing much had ever happened there, Giles proposed that
they look into a great red-brick church and see what it was
like. The Cherub preferred to stay outside and let Giles
go in. He knew little about Catholics and was suspicious
of their churches.
Giles mounted the high steps to the main entrance and
tried the heavy doors. They were locked. Then he went
to the entrance beneath the steps and found the door to the
lower chapel open. He stepped inside.
In the instant while an easy-swinging door had closed
with a breath of sound, he had passed from clangor and
confusion and hurry into quiet and repose. No other human
being was in the long room, which the low ceiling and the
very modest altar far up front made seem even longer
than it was. Through a half-opened window the sun spread
some last red rays over a seat directly in front of him.
He dropped into the seat, moved over so that the sunlight
did not strike his eye, and then enjoyed the utter tran-
quillity.
After a few moments in which he was aware of only the
tiny flame on the altar, a side door opened, and very, very
quietly a woman entered. She touched her fingers to the
water in the font and then in humility began praying the
stations. Something in her manner told Giles that she was
unaware of the presence of anyone else in the church. She
made her way slowly along one side and then the other.
Evidently she was a very penitent sinner. In the dim light,
Giles thought he could make that much out.
She was in earnest, wasn't she? No matter how much
of a mask she wore when she was outside, she was not
58
THE HILLIKIN
trying to bluff anybody in here. "Here I am," she cried
with her looks, "just as I am." And she was beautiful.
When at last she had finished she came slowly back
toward the main doorway. Would she see him? As she
came nearer and he saw the light of a great honesty in her
face, he hoped she would not see him. She was almost at
his side. He held his breath. She was not looking in his
direction. She was not thinking of anybody who might be
sitting there in the church. She was looking ahead as if
she were about to see the invisible. He was glad when she
did not turn her head; and when he had heard the swing-
ing door come shut with the gentlest of thuds he himself got
up from his seat and went out.
It was not easy to explain to the Cherub why he had
remained so long inside. Giles was not certain that he
could explain to himself just why the incident had seemed
to have significance. But that it had some, he could not
doubt. The woman thought she was alone. For once she
was just herself, without any pretense. She was not won-
dering how she was going to look to somebody. She was
alone with the memory of her sins, and that took all the
sham out of her face. She was even less self-conscious than
the beautiful Samaritan woman who one day questioned
Jesus at the well.
Giles meant to bring together a great body of experiences
that had color and meaning. Yes, he must hunt for them.
And they did not all seem to be in the College Yard.
He and the Cherub commenced going to plays at the
Colonial, the Hollis Street, the Castle Square. Giles meant
to have knowledge of the theater when he became as old
as Professor MacLaurin.
There came to the Colonial an impetuous and beautiful
actress who jolted Boston into enthusiasm. The two went
to see her-paid a dollar apiece for standing-room. With-
out waiting for further evidence, they decided that she was
THE HILLIKIN
59
greater than any Professor MacLaurin had met in a life-
time. As they left the theater and boys in blue and yellow
livery were shouting the sale of "original photographs" of
the great actress, Giles and the Cherub looked hesitantly,
sheepishly at each other, and then bought.
In a feature story two or three days later, Giles learned
that this heroine was born in Ohio. He must tell the Cherub.
No, he would not tell the Cherub. He had something else
in mind.
Early in the afternoon he slipped the photograph into
his coat pocket and went to the Boston hotel mentioned
in the article. He did not know Professor MacLaurin's tech-
nique in meeting so many distinguished figures of the stage,
but he knew how he himself meant to meet this one.
He sent up a rather large card which a one-eyed man
in a show window near Harvard Square had printed while
he waited.
He was fearful. The longer he waited the more fearful
he became. Maybe he had better steal away. Yes, he
would steal away before that smart-looking bell-boy came
back to say superciliously that she was not seeing anybody
to-day.
Yet there he stood. And then he saw the bell-boy re-
turning and could not run away. Why hadn't he gone
when he first thought about it?
"You are to go right up," he heard the bell-boy say very
decently.
A beautiful woman of thirty-five whom he had never seen
met him at the door. He stood puzzled-perhaps longer
than he meant. Maybe, after all, she had one eyebrow
that looked like the one he had seen on the stage.
"Aren't-aren't you from Ohio?" he asked hesitantly
while he tried to recall some of the things he had planned
to say.

"Give Hell To." Below he had made more specific nota-

to worry. The Boss has shut down on my money for the
rest of this year. Maybe I can wangle a little more out of
Mother, but I'm afraid I can't pay you much of your salary
or bonus either until next fall. I've got a little left, but I
ought to celebrate with it-passing all my courses!"
While seniors in white flannels led fathers and mothers
and brothers and sisters and radiant fiancées to commence-
ment spreads, Giles packed up his belongings, borrowed a
wheelbarrow from a gardener who was working on a lawn
near by, and moved into a very inexpensive room over a
banana shop on Beacon Street in Somerville. The street
cars made too much noise, and there were water bugs in the
bathroom. But then he did not expect to spend much time
there during the summer. Mrs. Aldrich's lawn would re-
quire attention, and who knew but what he might find
other work as well?
