CHAPTER VII
HE dull, dead days that followed commencement
bore down heavily. Giles tried to forget them by
devoting his free time to the book-work required
of University Guides. Did he mean to be a guide? Did
he know Cambridge well enough? That was difficult to
say. He needed only to know it better than a certain
number of his competing fellow candidates.
By the time he had qualified, Cambridge seemed to be
taking on an incontinent activity. Cambridge ladies who
did not appear often in the College Yard during term time
because they feared students might be a rude lot began
to stop often to feed the squirrels and criticize the latest
building the institution had erected. Tourists began to
rush about with guide books in hand, looking distantly for
historical spots. The Summer School proclaimed itself in
session. Women students who tried to appear young threw
themselves with conscious languishing on the green grass
in front of Holworthy Hall and thought how thrilling it was
to be in a place intended only for men. There was a demand
for University Guides.
The glories of Harvard-could not Giles reveal them?
Soon he was such an experienced cicerone that quite with-
out overt lying he could leave the impression that he must
have played in Harvard Square as a child. When the elderly
ladies in a party talked much about their extensive and
swift travels in Europe and asked Giles if it were true that
all the glass flowers in the University Museum were made
by Harvard undergraduates, he could answer with an au-
thoritative "No." When other ladies asked if the glass
66
THE HILLIKIN
67
flowers were really not the natural flowers with the glass
melted round them, he could answer with some similar
authoritative negation. When a man from Texas who wore
both belt and suspenders looked at the deep colors of the
Brimmer Window and said that he did not believe it had
ever cost thirty-thousand dollars-"if it was worth that
much, somebody would have stole it before this”—Giles was
able to convince the rest of the party, if not the man him-
self, that the figures he had quoted were authentic. By the
time summer was approaching an end, Giles had developed
so much self-respect by constantly talking to people who
knew less than he did on a certain point, that he imagined
he almost felt like a professor.
But when the last lingering Summer School students had
drifted away Giles began to lose his sense of importance.
Before he left Beacon Street in the morning, he saw only
the saucy sixteen-year-old Greek girl downstairs and two
or three frowsy-headed women customers. During the day
he saw only arrogant hucksters and grocers and express-
men who treated him as an equal when they stopped where
he worked and asked when Mrs. Aldrich would be return-
ing. Steadily he slipped down and down into a state of
great humility. Only a weekly letter from home cheered
him. He was glad that the corn and the fruits were
ripening, and that his father occasionally made some fruit
jars at the pot-shop in the hollow.
One day when a letter was due he received two. The
second was in a shaky handwriting and looked feminine. It
was from the Cherub's mother up in Vermont. Her son
Rodney, she explained, had somewhere contracted typhoid
fever. He had been helping to build a new highway and
must have drunk some water out of a contaminated roadside
spring. He had grown worse until he was now a sick boy.
In truth, the doctor had doubts about his recovery. In his
semi-deliriousness he talked much of Giles Dabney. Would
68
THE HILLIKIN
Giles, if he were in Cambridge and not afraid of the fever

seemed to want to tell him something.

were ahead of their fellows. The colors interwove them-
selves in his mind with the deep sadness that enthralled him.
After he had squirmed and marveled and feared for hours,

tremendous bit of domestic drama. He wanted to know
what had happened to that part of the Cherub which had
made him the Cherub.
He heard the Hunnewells go to bed. He heard the wail
of a swiftly moving train far below in the valley. Then the
silence outside was so great that it pressed through the
doors and windows and walls of the room and rang close in
his ears.
He turned the wick of the squat nickel lamp a little higher
and read in a book which he had meant to read on the
train.
At midnight he put the book aside, got up from his chair,
and tried the outside door, which seemed to lead into the
side yard. When he found that it would open without
squeaking, he slipped out.
A friendly collie got up from an old rug on the side porch,
expressed pleasure in seeing him, and then stood waiting in
the moonlight to be invited to go. Giles beckoned to him
to come along.
For no particular reason, he walked toward the house of
the Kinlochs. On and on he walked, the collie just ahead
THE HILLIKIN
73
of him or at his side, until he was near. There was a light at
the kitchen window. He had better go nearer.
Mrs. Kinloch was busily ironing as if it were the middle
of the morning instead of the middle of the night. A wan
smile held her face as if she were hoping fearfully that what
she possessed might not be wrenched from her.
There was something strangely unreal in a woman work-
ing thus after the middle of the night. In the abnormal
aliveness of night his entire experience since he got off the
train suddenly seemed unreal-the old man's little speaking,
the fairyland created by the frosts, the rhythmic hills, the
flash of lake, the black mountains against the bright west.
These were the dream; the reality was in his midnight ama-
teur reflections. As he walked slowly back along the path,
enveloped in night and silence, he was more alive than any
symphony had ever made him. Like a soldier who feels
magic protection about his own life, he did not think of him-
self as a subject for his contemplations. He only felt a
certain exalted pride in being able thus to consider existence
as he supposed a professor of philosophy would consider it.
The next morning he went again to talk with the Cherub.
But the Cherub was not there even so much as the day be-
fore. The wearied figure in the broad bed was using all of
its last remnant of strength in a desperate defensive battle
that was not to be evaded.
Giles felt so unattached to anything left there in the
house that he wanted to run away. He did not wish to
seem a coward. But when the country doctor told him that
he had known patients to linger for a week in Rodney's
state, he made preparations for withdrawal. He would stay,
he assured Mrs. Kinloch, if he could be of any assistance;
but since she herself had said he could not, he would go
back to Cambridge and hold himself in readiness to come
at her call.
He boarded a late night train that came tearing down
74
THE HILLIKIN
through the hills from Montreal, and sat the night through
in a long day coach where a half-dozen others were trying
to sleep without paying the price of a berth. He slept but
intermittently. While the vestibules banged and rattled and
squeaked in metrical little sequences, he was constantly
awaking to wrestle with the case of the Cherub. Instead of
wearying, he became more alert until the light of day broke
in upon his great concentration, and the vestibules subsided
into the silence of a stop in Porter Station, Cambridge.

CHAPTER VIII
HE immediate result of Giles's trip to Vermont was
a great revulsion of feeling against Wiggam's Glory,
Ohio. The Kinlochs' faces, it is true, had slowly
been shaped by the hurt that follows empty longing. He
was filled with pity by the meagerness of their lives, by
the fact that they were obliged to fight without improved
implements of warfare. Still, in their isolation in the Ver-
mont hills, in their daily pain, there was a certain grandeur.
They could stand upright in clear view of the mountains,
the valleys, the waters of the earth, and the limitless arch
of heaven, and take their bludgeonings in a contest that
seemed not without importance. But in Wiggam's Glory
men were denied even the satisfaction of feeling that their
futile warfare possessed the substance of accredited tragedy.
Their warfare was petty and dirty-between man and man.
It was fought over the crass question of whether somebody
should have more when he already had much, or whether
somebody else should have a little when he now had nothing
at all.
In his hot swirl of feeling, courses and professors, letters
from titled women about some pleasant historical tidbit or
other, actors and actresses before a satiated public, and
even his adored Symphonies in Memorial Hall, became
something trivial and artificial. Why should he cling
to anything so like a hothouse as a college? Why, espe-
cially when the one person with whom he felt in accord,
with whom he could speak or remain silent, was hovering
at life's end? With this understanding fellow wayfarer
already more or less faded into nothing, why should he
75
76
THE HILLIKIN
himself stay where he felt utterly unattached? And why,
most of all, when he was soon to be without money with
which to stay?
What he wished to do was to grasp the world with a
mighty hand, and give it such a wrench as would make
every crooked thing straight—at least, somewhat straight.
He was not interested in professors' falsetto proclamations.
He wanted to get away from the sense of sham with which
his surroundings somehow filled him, from the feeling that
he was missing the sharp, clearly defined essential points.
The lassitude which envelops Cambridge in late vacation
time accentuated his loneliness. He felt completely dis-
located. He seemed not to count at all.
He would move back to Mrs. Sanderson's. That would
fill up a morning. But finding a wheelbarrow and transport-
ing his effects consumed less time than he had hoped. In
the afternoon he thought he might as well go up and take
a look at Mrs. Aldrich's lawn and flowers. She might be
coming home pretty soon now, and she must have no justi-
fication for making any remarks about a jungle. He had
now been away three or four days.
He was startled to see her walking back toward the studio
as if this were not her first day at home.
"Oh, how do you do?" she called merrily, as if he were
not a gardener at all. "Where have you been keeping
yourself?"
She came half way to meet him. "I'm so glad to see
you." And as proof of her words she finished her hand-
shake with the slightest fillip of added pressure.
"I was called away," he explained as if the weeds might
already be a foot high.
"Oh! Nothing unfortunate, I hope."
"I'm afraid so. A friend-my best friend, I guess-is
at the point of death."
THE HILLIKIN
77
"Why, how perfectly terrible! I'm awfully sorry. Was
it an accident?"
Her attitude was so sympathetic that Giles hesitated not
to begin telling her about the occasion of his journey to
Vermont.
She interrupted him. "Come out to the studio and have
a cup of tea. I've nothing much to do and nobody to
talk to. Mr. Aldrich and two or three of his associates are
fishing in Canada. And certainly you can take a little
time. The lawn and the flowers are perfect-just perfect.
I'll have Marilda bring us some tea from the house " And
she darted off, more in the manner of Mickey Morrison
than in that of an established Cambridge matron.
The studio! From time to time Giles got implements
from this made-over barn of a building, but the interior of
the studio had remained unseen to his eyes. He did not
so much as know the kind of artistic work that gave it its
name.
"I haven't been in here since June," she explained when
she came back. "I was on my way out when I saw you."
She seemed pleasantly breathless as she pushed a great
iron key into the lock and turned, and then swung the
heavy door back on its rusting strap-iron hinges.
"Whew! Stuffy!"
Giles helped her to open the picture-book windows in
the south end of the long room.
Under the high north window was a half-finished figure
in dried-up cracked clay-a fiery winged horse with his
front hoofs high in the air and a nymph of a woman in full
nude restraining him.
She noticed that he looked. "I used to devote a little
time to clay-before I was married. My husband fitted
this up as my workshop-thought it well to keep a young
wife occupied, you see. But it's chiefly my library now."

Low shelves flanked the wide fireplace and stretched

Giles sketched the Kinlochs' hard life.

THE HILLIKIN
79
"Oh, I don't know as I could agree to that."
Giles did not mean to have a tragic end himself. He

Giles reflected for a moment. He lost enough of his aware-

She squealed cheerfully in laughter. "I just knew you
would say that. It's like you to do it. But you know you

Giles looked across the table in skepticism. She seemed
to have had her share of pretty nearly everything worth

this moment. The touch of pumped-up tragedy only bright-

She returned his look with a baffling smile. It was part
earnestness, part amusement, part shrewdness. Giles had

"Did you never see a little girl dressed up in a grown

the excitement soon wears away if she is told she has to
keep on doing it."
"I've seen coal miners' girls who had to wear their

"Well, I've never seen them, but I know exactly how
they feel."
Giles had to smile at her unwitting comparison of herself
with the coarse-voiced, profane thirteen-year-olds in No. 5
Company Row who wore oversized, half-buttoned dresses
when needs were especially great.

THE HILLIKIN
How could she feel so tragic? She was in possession of

"No, I never felt like that."

insipid remarks that are supposed to be daring, and laugh
flatly, and adjust their brooches, and-grow older. I try
to make them young, to bring them to me instead of letting
them haul me to them. But I'm usually in the minority
about forty to one. So you see who's winning."
She sat petulant and peppery as if in truth she were
where she had never intended to be.

"I've been trying to read Maeterlinck this summer.

THE HILLIKIN
Giles wondered if he ought not to be going.

as you like."
Giles took the white and blue roll, uttered a simple
good-by that expressed enthusiasm for life in general, and
swung off across the lawn down toward the street, his feet
treading the turf lightly as if they were scarcely touching
solid earth.

CHAPTER IX
AS it true that there was some mighty hand spread
constantly above one's head with finger points
reaching down to keep one going in a given direc-
tion? And did the hand always take one in the direction
of tragedy?
Late in lonely afternoons Giles walked mile upon mile
and tried to think upon these questions. And since he was
a hopeful amateur in philosophies, he reached conclusions.
"I guess not! I guess not!" he said, guided chiefly by a
strong determination. "There is no such hand-or if there
is, it lets a man go where he will."
Still, there was the case of his Grandfather Boyd.
Through four years of bloody war his grandfather marched
unscathed, only to come back from Appomattox to engage
in the less renowned battle with Coal and have Coal give
him a blinding blow in the face so that he went sightless
through the rest of his days on earth, and sightless into
the enveloping mystery when the earth no longer restrained
him. There was his father, too; and there was his mother.
They had battled against something quite as terrifying as
enemy soldiers or Coal, and they were still down there in
the hills in a state of siege. And Gustie Malloy-Gustie
who was so badly torn between enjoying the flesh and be-
coming a nun! And the Kinlochs, too! Like a slender
she-wolf Mrs. Kinloch had tried to stand protectively over
all her precious offspring; yet destiny had directed all but
one of them into some other sphere or none and that
one seemed now about to follow the others. Even Mrs.
Aldrich, who acted as if she were completely on top of
83
84
THE HILLIKIN
the world, had confessed that something had irresistibly
brought her to where she now had no wish to be.
And when he clumsily tried to consider his own life, he
could find instances. What save some alien will had sent
him off to Vermont? Certainly he had not made the trip
through any willing of his own. Certainly he had not sought
to attach to it any personal importance. Yet who could say
where those two or three days might not take him? Already
as a result of them, he had a new feeling toward Wiggam's
Glory and every other spot that was encumbered with dirt;
a new feeling toward Harvard and other organized centers
of learning; a new feeling about suffering and pain and life
and death. The trip to Vermont had become a part of him
and henceforth would be a part of everything he thought
or did. The trip to Vermont had become destiny. So had
every other episode and incident during the summer, during
the preceding college year, and on and on back to the very
first simple impression that he received after his mother had
given him birth.
Thus he sought desperately to be mature and reason
things out. Perhaps there was some kind of hand over
one's head. But, he persisted in thinking in his stubborn
provincial way, perhaps it was partly one's own hand.
Early one evening when he had walked and struggled in
reflection until the river at his side became far-off and unreal
like a conventional decoration, and the first football candi-
dates and advance guard of non-athletic students on Boyl-
ston Street loomed against the twilight like strange inhabit-
ants of an alien world, he went with great regretfulness to
his room. He threw open the door-and in a flash he
understood, he thought, why raccoons and squirrels that he
had wounded mortally when hunting had always crawled
out again from their holes when they felt the clutch of the
final agony. It was better to be in the open when one had
to face great problems. But perhaps he was lonely and
THE HILLIKIN
85
growing over-serious. Perhaps when more of the advance
guard of students had arrived, he would feel more secure.
Two letters! And one of them from Vermont! It was
addressed in a firm masculine hand evidently more accus-
tomed to the ax or plow handles than to the subtleties of a
steel pen.
Giles knew from the thinness of the letter that it was
brief. He was afraid to look at it-even at the outside
of it. Yet what else had it been sent for? With great
trembling he tore it open.
Before he noted whether it bore a date or a salutation he
saw the tall first words. "He's better." In truth, that was
almost the whole letter. For Mr. Kinloch had merely
added: "He's of a tough breed, and if he don't overeat, I
guess you'll see him back in Cambridge sooner or later."
For ten minutes Giles sat and enjoyed the strong pound-
ing of his heart.
Who was afraid of destiny, he wanted to know? Cou-
rageously he picked up the other letter. It was on vellum-
like stationery. It was from Mrs. Preston MacIntyre
Aldrich. She was ready to discuss that driveway. Should
Giles receive this letter in time, would he come with the
blue-print and his notes and plans that evening? She was
free.
He forgot all such abstractions as guiding hands above
people's heads and bumped about in his room like a young
bull, banging into chairs and his desk and the bed as he
hastened in preparations. Then he took the shortest pos-
sible course athwart streets that converge in Harvard Square
and was soon in sight of Mrs. Aldrich's house.
He rang, and stood breathless, half from exertion, half
from the mere excitement of suspense.
"You are to go to the studio," the maid said when at last
she came.
He was to go to the studio again!

The room was bright from a crackling fireplace. Every

the idea that what the country most needed-at least the

But when Giles sat alone in the spacious bedroom just
off the parlor, those few minutes with the Cherub were the
only ones in the past two days that were sharp in his mind.
He was not caught up by any childish fear of death. He
had seen too much of death among the miners of Wiggam's
Glory to be conventionally frightened at just one more in-
stance, even when that instance happened to be the best
friend he had found in Cambridge. But in the philosophic
manner of the country youth who has seen little yet has
reflected much, he allowed himself to be impressed by this
tremendous bit of domestic drama. He wanted to know
what had happened to that part of the Cherub which had
made him the Cherub.
He heard the Hunnewells go to bed. He heard the wail

For no particular reason, he walked toward the house of
the Kinlochs. On and on he walked, the collie just ahead
THE HILLIKIN
73
of him or at his side, until he was near. There was a light at
the kitchen window. He had better go nearer.
Mrs. Kinloch was busily ironing as if it were the middle
of the morning instead of the middle of the night. A wan
smile held her face as if she were hoping fearfully that what
she possessed might not be wrenched from her.
There was something strangely unreal in a woman work-
ing thus after the middle of the night. In the abnormal
aliveness of night his entire experience since he got off the
train suddenly seemed unreal-the old man's little speaking,
the fairyland created by the frosts, the rhythmic hills, the
flash of lake, the black mountains against the bright west.
These were the dream; the reality was in his midnight ama-
teur reflections. As he walked slowly back along the path,
enveloped in night and silence, he was more alive than any
symphony had ever made him. Like a soldier who feels
magic protection about his own life, he did not think of him-
self as a subject for his contemplations. He only felt a
certain exalted pride in being able thus to consider existence
as he supposed a professor of philosophy would consider it.
The next morning he went again to talk with the Cherub.
But the Cherub was not there even so much as the day be-
fore. The wearied figure in the broad bed was using all of
its last remnant of strength in a desperate defensive battle
that was not to be evaded.
Giles felt so unattached to anything left there in the
house that he wanted to run away. He did not wish to
seem a coward. But when the country doctor told him that
he had known patients to linger for a week in Rodney's
state, he made preparations for withdrawal. He would stay,
he assured Mrs. Kinloch, if he could be of any assistance;
but since she herself had said he could not, he would go
back to Cambridge and hold himself in readiness to come
at her call.
He boarded a late night train that came tearing down
74
THE HILLIKIN
through the hills from Montreal, and sat the night through
in a long day coach where a half-dozen others were trying
to sleep without paying the price of a berth. He slept but
intermittently. While the vestibules banged and rattled and
squeaked in metrical little sequences, he was constantly
awaking to wrestle with the case of the Cherub. Instead of
wearying, he became more alert until the light of day broke
in
upon his great concentration, and the vestibules subsided
into the silence of a stop in Porter Station, Cambridge.
T
CHAPTER VIII
HE immediate result of Giles's trip to Vermont was
a great revulsion of feeling against Wiggam's Glory,
Ohio. The Kinlochs' faces, it is true, had slowly
been shaped by the hurt that follows empty longing. He
was filled with pity by the meagerness of their lives, by
the fact that they were obliged to fight without improved
implements of warfare. Still, in their isolation in the Ver-
mont hills, in their daily pain, there was a certain grandeur.
They could stand upright in clear view of the mountains,
the valleys, the waters of the earth, and the limitless arch
of heaven, and take their bludgeonings in a contest that
seemed not without importance. But in Wiggam's Glory
men were denied even the satisfaction of feeling that their
futile warfare possessed the substance of accredited tragedy.
Their warfare was petty and dirty-between man and man.
It was fought over the crass question of whether somebody
should have more when he already had much, or whether
somebody else should have a little when he now had nothing
at all.
In his hot swirl of feeling, courses and professors, letters
from titled women about some pleasant historical tidbit or
other, actors and actresses before a satiated public, and
even his adored Symphonies in Memorial Hall, became
something trivial and artificial. Why should he cling
to anything so like a hothouse as a college? Why, espe-
cially when the one person with whom he felt in accord,
with whom he could speak or remain silent, was hovering
at life's end? With this understanding fellow wayfarer
already more or less faded into nothing, why should he
75
76
THE HILLIKIN
himself stay where he felt utterly unattached? And why,
most of all, when he was soon to be without money with
which to stay?
What he wished to do was to grasp the world with a
mighty hand, and give it such a wrench as would make
every crooked thing straight-at least, somewhat straight.
He was not interested in professors' falsetto proclamations.
He wanted to get away from the sense of sham with which
his surroundings somehow filled him, from the feeling that
he was missing the sharp, clearly defined essential points.
The lassitude which envelops Cambridge in late vacation
time accentuated his loneliness. He felt completely dis-
located. He seemed not to count at all.
He would move back to Mrs. Sanderson's. That would
fill up a morning. But finding a wheelbarrow and transport-
ing his effects consumed less time than he had hoped. In
the afternoon he thought he might as well go up and take
a look at Mrs. Aldrich's lawn and flowers. She might be
coming home pretty soon now, and she must have no justi-
fication for making any remarks about a jungle. He had
now been away three or four days.
He was startled to see her walking back toward the studio
as if this were not her first day at home.
"Oh, how do you do?" she called merrily, as if he were
not a gardener at all. "Where have you been keeping
yourself?"
She came half way to meet him. "I'm so glad to see
you." And as proof of her words she finished her hand-
shake with the slightest fillip of added pressure.
"I was called away," he explained as if the weeds might
already be a foot high.
"Oh! Nothing unfortunate, I hope."
"I'm afraid so. A friend-my best friend, I guess-is
at the point of death."
THE HILLIKIN
77
"Why, how perfectly terrible! I'm awfully sorry. Was
it an accident?"
Her attitude was so sympathetic that Giles hesitated not
to begin telling her about the occasion of his journey to
Vermont.
She interrupted him. "Come out to the studio and have
a cup of tea. I've nothing much to do and nobody to
talk to. Mr. Aldrich and two or three of his associates are
fishing in Canada. And certainly you can take a little
time. The lawn and the flowers are perfect-just perfect.
I'll have Marilda bring us some tea from the house" And
she darted off, more in the manner of Mickey Morrison
than in that of an established Cambridge matron.
The studio! From time to time Giles got implements
from this made-over barn of a building, but the interior of
the studio had remained unseen to his eyes. He did not
so much as know the kind of artistic work that gave it its
name.
"I haven't been in here since June," she explained when
she came back. "I was on my way out when I saw you."
She seemed pleasantly breathless as she pushed a great
iron key into the lock and turned, and then swung the
heavy door back on its rusting strap-iron hinges.
"Whew! Stuffy!"
Giles helped her to open the picture-book windows in
the south end of the long room.
Under the high north window was a half-finished figure
in dried-up cracked clay-a fiery winged horse with his
front hoofs high in the air and a nymph of a woman in full
nude restraining him.
She noticed that he looked. "I used to devote a little
time to clay-before I was married. My husband fitted
this up as my workshop-thought it well to keep a young
wife occupied, you see. But it's chiefly my library now."
78
THE HILLIKIN
Low shelves flanked the wide fireplace and stretched
along that side of the room and one end.
"Well, you have books enough for a library."
"I read quite a little, in fact."
A book lay spread open with the pages downward on
the long table at the back of the sofa that fronted the
fireplace. She picked it up to see what it was.
"Goodness, I was reading that before we went away.
Have you read it-Alfred de Musset's Confessions of a
Child of the Century? I was following up George Sand,
you see.'
""
"No, I haven't read it."
"I suppose it is awfully old-fashioned now. But then
I'm an old-fashioned person."
Before Giles needed to make reply, Matilda came with
a tray of silver, a plate of sandwiches, and another plate
of ingeniously devised cakes.
"Yes, that table-the little one," Mrs. Aldrich said
approvingly.
She sat and served the tea, and Giles sat opposite and
marveled at the silverware. Think of eating from such
beautiful things! When did anybody ever have enough
time to do all that hand work?
"It was too bad about your friend," Mrs. Aldrich began
as soon as she had poured the tea and passed the sandwiches
and taken two or three herself. "Tell me more about it."
Giles sketched the Kinlochs' hard life.
"Isn't destiny brutal?" she asked when he had finished.
She seemed to be thinking of something she had thought
of before.
"Sometimes, yes."
For a moment Giles sat with the image of his father and
mother close before his face.
"Always, I think," Mrs. Aldrich added. "I think life
is fundamentally tragic."
THE HILLIKIN
79
"Oh, I don't know as I could agree to that."
Giles did not mean to have a tragic end himself. He
had clear intentions of the quality of his career, even if he
did not see the career itself very sharply.
"Well, how many people do you know to whom destiny
has been really-really kind?”
Giles reflected for a moment. He lost enough of his aware-
ness of being a provincial to venture upon direct chivalry.
"It seems to have been kind enough to you."
She squealed cheerfully in laughter.

might have to change your mind even about me."
Giles looked across the table in skepticism. She seemed
to have had her share of pretty nearly everything worth
having. And never had she seemed so enchanting as at
this moment. The touch of pumped-up tragedy only bright-
ened her gray-blue eyes and her gray-pink face.
She returned his look with a baffling smile. It was part
earnestness, part amusement, part shrewdness. Giles had
to pick up a thin sandwich and take a bite in order to free
himself.
"Did you never see a little girl dressed up in a grown
person's clothes?" she asked, not content to drop the ques-
tion of her own plight. "For a time it's great fun. But
the excitement soon wears away if she is told she has to
keep on doing it."
"I've seen coal miners' girls who had to wear their
mothers' clothes to school sometimes," Giles observed, not
too seriously.
"Well, I've never seen them, but I know exactly how
they feel."
Giles had to smile at her unwitting comparison of herself
with the coarse-voiced, profane thirteen-year-olds in No. 5
Company Row who wore oversized, half-buttoned dresses
when needs were especially great.

THE HILLIKIN
How could she feel so tragic? She was in possession of
much. He remained speechless in reflection for a moment,
but the skepticism did not leave his face.
"You don't believe me, do you?"
He became more self-conscious; yet he was eager to have
her go on.
"Oh, I-"
"Tell me this," she demanded, breaking into his hesitant
words. "Did you ever feel that everybody you knew was
trying to make you grow old by an unnatural hurrying
process?"
"No, I never felt like that."
"Well, that's the way I feel all the time. Everybody I
sit down to dinner with is at least ten years older than I
am-and more likely twenty or thirty. Stout dames talk
about their daughters in college. Old pouter pigeons of
grandmothers talk about their newest grandchild, and make
insipid remarks that are supposed to be daring, and laugh
flatly, and adjust their brooches, and-grow older. I try
to make them young, to bring them to me instead of letting
them haul me to them. But I'm usually in the minority
about forty to one. So you see who's winning."
She sat petulant and peppery as if in truth she were
where she had never intended to be.
A
"I've been trying to read Maeterlinck this summer.
wheezy old dowager with a dozen rings on her fingers-the
wife of one of my husband's associates-recommended him
as delightfully steadying, or something of that sort. But he's
romantic, or sentimental, or-well, he's mushy, anyhow. He
says we should ever live as if we were on the eve of the
great revelation, whatever that may mean. But the only
thing I've ever found that's worth having is left farther
and farther behind. He says not to think of destiny as if
it were connected only with disaster. But how are you
THE HILLIKIN
81
going to help it when joy is confined to a high hilltop and
you are plunging down the hill headlong?"
Giles had not meant that his modest account of the Kin-
lochs should loose such rhetoric as this. And she still had
something to say.
"Do you know you are only just about eight or ten years
younger than I am?" And she laughed desperately at her
honest words. "Yet I'm done for, or pretty nearly, and
you still have everything ahead."
Giles sought to be hopeful. "You don't look much
done for."
"Oh, you perfect dear! Say it again. I wish I could
hear it every day until I get so old that the sweet words
become humorous. Sometimes I still think maybe they're
true in part. I wish I could make myself believe the whole
of them. I'd like to. I'd like to kick out of my electric-
massaged old world just once more-my electric-massaged
old world that has been created by this kind destiny of
yours. Do you know what I'd like to do? I'd like to go
down to Harvard Square some football day and when the
music comes along, seize a banner and shout 'Forward'
like Joan of Arc, and lead ten thousand to some hard-
fought, frenzied victory, and then dance at the head of the
snake dance afterward till I fell exhausted."
"Why say! You're not done for at all!"
And they laughed in high-pitched accord.
"Well, I'd like to do it—just as a kind of glorified dying
gasp."
"If you tried it, you'd die immortal-almost as immortal
as Joan."
They laughed until they were quite down from the absurd
heights.
Mrs. Aldrich wiped her eyes with a wisp of handkerchief,
and finished off with a dulcet pianissimo measure or two
from the main movement of their laughter.
82
THE HILLIKIN
Giles wondered if he ought not to be going.
"I believe," he said, in an effort to seem at the same time
unhurried and unaffected in speech, "I had better be trotting
along."
"Oh, must you?" But she did not seek to restrain him.
"Then we'll have to settle our destinies another time."
She was not certain that he read any second meaning into
her words. Still, it was better to turn to purely factual
matters. "Here, maybe your protests about the driveway
are to bear fruit. Take this blue-print of the grounds with
you and be prepared to show me on paper precisely what
you have in mind. We'll talk it over-soon; almost as soon
as you like."
Giles took the white and blue roll, uttered a simple
good-by that expressed enthusiasm for life in general, and
swung off across the lawn down toward the street, his feet
treading the turf lightly as if they were scarcely touching
solid earth.

CHAPTER IX
AS it true that there was some mighty hand spread
constantly above one's head with finger points
reaching down to keep one going in a given direc-
tion? And did the hand always take one in the direction
of tragedy?
Late in lonely afternoons Giles walked mile upon mile
and tried to think upon these questions. And since he was
a hopeful amateur in philosophies, he reached conclusions.
"I guess not! I guess not!" he said, guided chiefly by a
strong determination. "There is no such hand-or if there
is, it lets a man go where he will."
Still, there was the case of his Grandfather Boyd.
Through four years of bloody war his grandfather marched
unscathed, only to come back from Appomattox to engage
in the less renowned battle with Coal and have Coal give
him a blinding blow in the face so that he went sightless
through the rest of his days on earth, and sightless into
the enveloping mystery when the earth no longer restrained
him. There was his father, too; and there was his mother.
They had battled against something quite as terrifying as
enemy soldiers or Coal, and they were still down there in
the hills in a state of siege. And Gustie Malloy-Gustie
who was so badly torn between enjoying the flesh and be-
coming a nun! And the Kinlochs, too! Like a slender
she-wolf Mrs. Kinloch had tried to stand protectively over
all her precious offspring; yet destiny had directed all but
one of them into some other sphere or none and that
one seemed now about to follow the others. Even Mrs.
Aldrich, who acted as if she were completely on top of
83
84
THE HILLIKIN
the world, had confessed that something had irresistibly
brought her to where she now had no wish to be.
And when he clumsily tried to consider his own life, he
could find instances. What save some alien will had sent
him off to Vermont? Certainly he had not made the trip
through any willing of his own. Certainly he had not sought
to attach to it any personal importance. Yet who could say
where those two or three days might not take him? Already
as a result of them, he had a new feeling toward Wiggam's
Glory and every other spot that was encumbered with dirt;
a new feeling toward Harvard and other organized centers
of learning; a new feeling about suffering and pain and life
and death. The trip to Vermont had become a part of him
and henceforth would be a part of everything he thought
or did. The trip to Vermont had become destiny. So had
every other episode and incident during the summer, during
the preceding college year, and on and on back to the very
first simple impression that he received after his mother had
given him birth.
Thus he sought desperately to be mature and reason
things out. Perhaps there was some kind of hand over
one's head. But, he persisted in thinking in his stubborn
provincial way, perhaps it was partly one's own hand.
Early one evening when he had walked and struggled in
reflection until the river at his side became far-off and unreal
like a conventional decoration, and the first football candi-
dates and advance guard of non-athletic students on Boyl-
ston Street loomed against the twilight like strange inhabit-
ants of an alien world, he went with great regretfulness to
his room.
He threw open the door-and in a flash he
understood, he thought, why raccoons and squirrels that he
had wounded mortally when hunting had always crawled
out again from their holes when they felt the clutch of the
final agony. It was better to be in the open when one had
to face great problems. But perhaps he was lonely and
THE HILLIKIN
85
growing over-serious. Perhaps when more of the advance
guard of students had arrived, he would feel more secure.
Two letters! And one of them from Vermont! It was
addressed in a firm masculine hand evidently more accus-
tomed to the ax or plow handles than to the subtleties of a
steel pen.
Giles knew from the thinness of the letter that it was
brief. He was afraid to look at it-even at the outside
of it. Yet what else had it been sent for? With great
trembling he tore it open.
Before he noted whether it bore a date or a salutation he
saw the tall first words. "He's better." In truth, that was
almost the whole letter. For Mr. Kinloch had merely
added: "He's of a tough breed, and if he don't overeat, I
guess you'll see him back in Cambridge sooner or later."
For ten minutes Giles sat and enjoyed the strong pound-
ing of his heart.
Who was afraid of destiny, he wanted to know? Cou-
rageously he picked up the other letter. It was on vellum-
like stationery. It was from Mrs. Preston MacIntyre
Aldrich. She was ready to discuss that driveway. Should
Giles receive this letter in time, would he come with the
blue-print and his notes and plans that evening? She was
free.
He forgot all such abstractions as guiding hands above
people's heads and bumped about in his room like a young
bull, banging into chairs and his desk and the bed as he
hastened in preparations. Then he took the shortest pos-
sible course athwart streets that converge in Harvard Square
and was soon in sight of Mrs. Aldrich's house.
He rang, and stood breathless, half from exertion, half
from the mere excitement of suspense.
"You are to go to the studio," the maid said when at last
she came.
He was to go to the studio again!

The room was bright from a crackling fireplace. Every
object that caught any of the light seemed also to catch
some of the warmth.
"Oh!" Giles exclaimed when he felt himself so suddenly
in the brightness. "I'm used to fireplaces."
"Out in your mysterious Ohio?"
"Yes."
"Well, I thought it was cool enough to have a little fire
built. Anyhow, I like firelight, don't you? It gives one a
sense of security. No matter how dark and cavernous the
universe may seem, you forget it before a cheerful fireplace.
Everything is so definitely centered. You can't feel in very
much of a void so long as the fire is burning.
"But now let's talk shop. Where's the blue-print? Sup-
pose we sit here. How's that?" And she snapped on the
light in the great reading-lamp on the table at the back of
the sofa.
They sat with the lamp on the table at their backs. Giles
indicated just where the changed driveway would come. To
make the matter doubly sure, he produced a detailed sketch
of the grounds as he would make them if he were free to
do as he chose.
"Goodness! Have you done all this since the other day?"
"No-o-o. I've been fussing around with it all spring and
summer-whenever I've had a little odd time."
She dropped her hand and her end of the sketch to her
lap and leaned back and away enough to look at him.
"How perfectly marvelous! Where did you get all these
ideas? Did you consult somebody down at the university?"
"Oh," Giles answered, embarrassed by the praise, “it's
not so much. It's a perfectly natural thing to do."
"Why, it's just too wonderful for words-that you could
do it without having somebody tell you how. I should think
you'd feel like-like a god, or something."

87
Giles flinched and colored. "No, I'm sure I don't feel

"Well, you've every reason to. How much do you sup-

She gave him more of a push with her shoulder. "You

THE HILLIKIN
His face was swept by a luxuriant ruddiness. "Why

She looked at him as if she were almost certain that
he was unsophisticated rather than wise.
"Then I'll tell you!" and she threw herself into his arms
and covered his lips with hot kisses. He did not resist.
But she was aware that her frenzy had not moved him.
Quickly she sat up and laughed as if it all had been
pretense. She would make herself desired through some
other strategy.
"But see here! We're skating on thin ice. You had
better run along now-and see if you can decide whether
I was serious or only-"
She arose as if she expected him to go. In her wisdom
she knew that he would crush her in his arms. In her
wisdom she knew that. Yet he arose without speech, and
with so much blood pressing into his face that he loomed
larger than ever, and moved toward the door.
She wanted to scream when she saw that she had blun-
dered again. She moved toward the door with him and
watched for some promise from a face that seemed neither
regretful nor resentful, but only inscrutable.
At the door she desperately seized one of his hands,
lifted it in a quick caress to her face and imprinted not
only her lips but her teeth upon it, dropped it quickly
and said, "You know, don't you, that Venus always wins?"
Then she stood in amazement while he went on out into
the night without a word.
When at last he became aware of himself he knew only
that he was walking. He vaguely remembered that he had
turned into Brattle Street. And here he was passing Long-
fellow Park on his way down toward the river.
She had tried to be young again, hadn't she? And in
the end she had displayed about as much finesse as one
of the girls down in No. 5 Company Row. They tugged
THE HILLIKIN
89
and pulled at a man, too, in the hope that he would come
to life. The only difference was that if he did not, and
left them, they hurled at him an unprintable good-by that
evidently was not a part of Mrs. Aldrich's vocabulary.
Well, she had not stampeded him, anyhow. Something
of the same content crept over him which he had known
back in the Wiggam's Glory area when he would not let
Theda and all the rest of the personal workers in the
revival meeting lead him forward to the mourners' bench.
The cases had elements of difference, of course. Still some-
thing within him approved in each case. He had not been
stampeded.
As he walked farther he was able to see his own part in
the episode a little better. Almost getting into a snarl
with a young married woman! She was a somebody in
Cambridge, too! He smiled as he slowed down to a snail's
pace along the river path. He had had such an experience,
hadn't he? He was not dreaming. Yet who would believe
him if he should tell the story? Mrs. Aldrich, in her posi-
tion of importance, could rise up in great indignation and
declare the story absurd, and everybody would believe her
and nobody would believe him. Or maybe some of his fellow
students might. They were a believing lot-on some
subjects.
And she was pretty nearly a beautiful woman, too. His
clarified mind fell to reviewing what had taken place. He
visualized everything, including Mrs. Aldrich. She threw
herself into his arms. She pressed herself close against
him. Who could say that that was not different from
taking some thick-headed, stout-backed Company Row girl
into one's arms? And it was not all acting either, even if
Mrs. Aldrich had tried in an afterthought to make it so.
She had trembled and was hot in her wild desire of him.
He himself now trembled. Nobody could deny that she
was a fascinating woman. He saw her as a silvery moth

THE HILLIKIN
Giles wondered if he ought not to be going.
"I believe," he said, in an effort to seem at the same time

"Oh, must you?" But she did not seek to restrain him.
"Then we'll have to settle our destinies another time."
She was not certain that he read any second meaning into

you and be prepared to show me on paper precisely what
you have in mind. We'll talk it over-soon; almost as soon
as you like."
Giles took the white and blue roll, uttered a simple
good-by that expressed enthusiasm for life in general, and
swung off across the lawn down toward the street, his feet
treading the turf lightly as if they were scarcely touching
solid earth.

CHAPTER IX
AS it true that there was some mighty hand spread
constantly above one's head with finger points
reaching down to keep one going in a given direc-
tion? And did the hand always take one in the direction
of tragedy?
Late in lonely afternoons Giles walked mile upon mile
and tried to think upon these questions. And since he was
a hopeful amateur in philosophies, he reached conclusions.
"I guess not! I guess not!" he said, guided chiefly by a
strong determination. "There is no such hand-or if there
is, it lets a man go where he will."
Still, there was the case of his Grandfather Boyd.
Through four years of bloody war his grandfather marched
unscathed, only to come back from Appomattox to engage
in the less renowned battle with Coal and have Coal give
him a blinding blow in the face so that he went sightless
through the rest of his days on earth, and sightless into
the enveloping mystery when the earth no longer restrained
him. There was his father, too; and there was his mother.
They had battled against something quite as terrifying as
enemy soldiers or Coal, and they were still down there in
the hills in a state of siege. And Gustie Malloy-Gustie
who was so badly torn between enjoying the flesh and be-
coming a nun! And the Kinlochs, too! Like a slender
she-wolf Mrs. Kinloch had tried to stand protectively over
all her precious offspring; yet destiny had directed all but
one of them into some other sphere or none and that
one seemed now about to follow the others. Even Mrs.
Aldrich, who acted as if she were completely on top of

Out of the unreal night that enveloped the city came

He listened. He heard them again. Then the silence was

bridge or perhaps on it. The evening was unusually

"Where is the big crimson H two hundred feet high?”

"Why, right there," the other voice answered unsteadily.
"Can't you see that?"

"Where?"

THE HILLIKIN
The man knew how to swim and was working gamely.

And with the guarantee of support in an emergency, he
did. So there was no occasion for heroics at all.
A policeman was soon at hand. He told the two-dozen
people who came down from the river pathway that there
wasn't a thing in the world to be excited about. Never-
theless, he fell into reminiscence over years of excitement
on this particular beat. While he recounted how a girl
who had come down from a Hasty Pudding dance one
night had tried to jump in, and, when he saved her, had
turned upon him with more Irish profanity than he him-
self, by the Holy Virgin, could ever hope to command,
Giles crowded his wet feet into his shoes, put on his dry
coat and vest and hat, and in almost total eclipse slipped
away toward Harvard Square and his room.
He strode through the Square more or less oblivious of
the startled look which occasional persons gave him. He
was remembering that Mrs. Aldrich had his sketches of her
proposed lawn, and that she might be disposed to keep
them.
G
CHAPTER X
ILES sat on a bench in the park along the Charles
a day or two later and watched godlike young men
of his own age sweep by on the water in graceful
featherweight shells. Somehow he arrived immediately at
the conclusion that poverty is man's only real misfortune.
He could row like that if he had time for practice. He
could do a hundred things that he much wanted to do if
only he did not have to be thinking all the time about money.
He was worse off than last year, for he had perforce used
up what he brought with him. And in case he did not
go back to finish his work for Mrs. Aldrich-
Well, in case he did not go back, that only added one
more complication to his financial future. He smiled as he
thought of his predicament. If he chose to let his sense
of the ridiculous have way, he could tell a fine story about
himself: "Man from the wilds of Ohio earning his way
through Harvard afraid to go back to his former employer,
prominent Cambridge matron, lest she abduct him!" He
might write it some day. But that would not bring him
in any money just yet, and he needed money now.
He was not diverted from the starkness of his own plight
by the return of fellow students. He saw them, yes. They
rushed everywhere for new and second-hand furniture.
They came from bookshops with armfuls of books on the
Economics of Transportation, Greek Culture in the Sixth
Century, B.C., the Ethnography of Mexico, Socialism and
the Single Tax, the Morphology of Insects, Phoenician and
Aramaic Inscriptions, Machine Design, the History of
Music since Palestrina, Forest Economics, Old High Ger-
93
94
THE HILLIKIN
man, Celestial Mechanics, Rural Social Development,
Poetry Since the Death of Scott, Electrochemistry, History
of Babylonia and Assyria, Architectural Design, Vedic
Sanskrit, Municipal Government, Norwegian Dramatists,
Lyric Poetry, Horticulture, Playwriting, the Planning of
Cities. With books hugged close they stopped occasionally
to look through shop-windows at displays of neckwear and
hats and shoes. Shopkeepers, disgruntled over the paltry
nickels of Summer School women, rejoiced at the sight
of all these men. Men had pockets, and some of the pockets
had money in them. But for Giles the only cheering pos-
sibility in all the crowd was H. Pearsall Dwyer. The finan-
cial prospect might be slightly improved if he could come
upon H. Pearsall before he spent his fresh supplies of free
money on sea foods.
One day he chanced to meet H. Pearsall, swarthy red
from a month's vacation in Canada. H. Pearsall was sorry
to report that he was dreadfully hard up. His father was
learning as his son grew older. Giles would have to wait
-but only a few weeks. It would then be in order to
write or better still, to telegraph-for money, on the
supposition that books had unexpectedly been required by
extravagant professors. If he could wangle anything out
of his dad in that way, it would be Giles's money or at
least a part of it would.
Giles's reflections turned again to Mrs. Aldrich. When
would she be sending the check along for his summer's
work? And, everything considered, how much might he
expect? Might she think lavish generosity would bring
him back? Well, if she tried that, he would be able to
scrape together enough to pay the first installment of his
term bills. His financial embarrassment would disappear--
at least for the time, since the mere getting of food ought
alone to be easy enough. On the other hand, what if she
should report to the college employment office that he was
THE HILLIKIN
95
an irresponsible person who left off work without finishing
it, and refused to pay him at all-unless he came back and
finished? Possibly his predecessor had left abruptly, too,
because she had become demonstrative in her efforts to
cling to youth. But one morning all his speculations were
put to rest by the arrival of a check from her. It bore the
simple notation, "In full," and was neither generous nor
stingy.
So the year still presented a problem in finance. No new
book was purchased until all efforts to pick up a second-
hand copy had been exhausted; no article of food on any
menu was ordered until the list had been scanned for
anything that might be cheaper; no concerts were attended
until it had been ascertained that they were to cost nothing.
On the crisp November morning of the Yale game he
was tempted to spend money. Why not take the two dollars
in his pocket-since he had a five-dollar bill in his desk-
and buy a football ticket and feel like everybody else for
a few hours? He walked across the Yard three or four
times in an effort to decide, and ended the matter by going
off and getting himself belatedly accepted as an usher. He
would have free admission and a paper-plate luncheon be-
sides. And couldn't he see the game almost as well? He
would usher for a couple of hours while everybody was
coming early to see everybody else arrive, and then he
would be free. What did it matter if some late straggler
did stop on the steps to watch, and obscured the view of
others? In a jiffy he could be shown to his seat fourteen
places over-perhaps while time was being taken out in
the game.
Nervous, happy, and proud to have become an economic
wizard, he started early to Soldiers Field to eat his paper-
plate luncheon in the baseball cage.
Harvard Square was already filled with cheerful people
who carried heavy-looking steamer rugs and red and blue
96
THE HILLIKIN
blankets over their arms. As he pushed his way through, a

He turned and faced Mickey Morrison. "Oh-h, Hillikin!"
she repeated in intimate voice and took his arm in prepara-
tion for an entreaty. "Can't you get me a ticket for the
game? It would be so wonderful."
Giles hesitated. How much money was it he had with
him?
"Please do! Elizabeth Ann and Aggie think they're so
smart and won't take me. They're afraid I'll tell what

them a thing or two."
Giles decided that it was two dollars and forty-five cents
that he had in his pocket.
"Mother won't care. She knows you. She has seen you
over in Mrs. Aldrich's flowers-thousands of times. And
dad would think I had treated them just right."
She was a nice kid, Giles had to admit.
"I'm not so sure that I can get a ticket," at last he said,
as if that had been his reason for hesitating. "If I buy
one from a scalper, I may be blacklisted. I might- Sup-
pose you linger here or there in that doorway-till I run

Mickey thought he was never coming back; but he did.
"Here you are! And it's in my section, too."
"Oh-h! You're a perfect brick!" she cried so heartily

Giles ignored this echo of grown-up sisters' speech. "But

They told me that's the reason I couldn't go unless they

to go home and put plenty of warm things on. It will be

She
knew the Hillikin ever so well, yet going with him was
not quite like bumping along with her father.
"Get your hot-dogs here!" a man in a kitchen-wagon
at the south end of the bridge called to the world at large.
"Oh, Hillikin! I believe I've changed my mind. I think
I'd better have a hot-dog. Or no, I'll tell you what: just
get me two, with lots of mustard, and I'll take them with
me and eat as slow as my jaws will work and help put in
the time."
Giles bought them for two dimes. He felt greatly re-
lieved when he descried no other vendors on that side of
the street.
The man at the proper gate promised to see that she
got in as soon as the gates were opened. Then Giles rushed
to the baseball cage, swallowed some forkfuls of something
that looked like chopped meat, ate a soft roll, gulped down
a cup of coffee, and hurried to his post high in the Stadium.
Mickey bounded out of an entrance below him. "They
just let us in!" she exclaimed as she ran up the steps toward
him, joyful at being there, joyful at seeing somebody she
knew, joyful at seeing bundled-up human beings swarming
98
THE HILLIKIN
out everywhere into the sunlight that flooded the Stadium.
Giles noticed that she had eaten her hot-dogs, but he said
nothing. With all sorts of vendors under the Stadium, he
did not wish to raise the question of food.
"Wouldn't it be just a scream if they'd happen to sit
close here somewhere? I'd make a nose at old Aggie every
time she looked."
Suddenly the young lady in her gained the upper hand,
and she marched over to her seat in strictest decorum.
Once when every usher was at his busiest, Giles saw
Mickey talking with two young women and two young men.
She was gleeful. They did not wish to be, but in such a
public place, with everybody pushing past, they felt obliged
to treat her somewhat civilly, somewhat as an equal—which
was the one thing in the world she most desired. So great
was her triumph that after she had shouted and screamed
herself hoarse in the progress of the game, she was allowed
to go home with them as a partly accredited member of the
family.
Giles thought the afternoon was worth the two dollars
and twenty cents. But that amount would have to be
replaced. At his level of living, two dollars and twenty
cents would have paid for his meals, such as they were, for
the better part of a week.
He hunted up H. Pearsall Dwyer. H. Pearsall was too
regretful for words-though he uttered many in an effort
to show how regretful he was. His father had sent him
some money, yes. But the game had been an expensive
one for him—so expensive, in fact, that he was financially
crumpled. His father was enlarging his pottery business,
too, and was more and more inclined toward college econ-
omies.
Giles forgot his own needs for a moment.
"Pottery? Where is his pottery business?"
"Down in Jersey."
THE HILLIKIN
99
"Do you know much about the business yourself? Know
where he gets his glazes-especially the colors?"
"Why? Are you interested in pottery?"
"My father is just a little bit."
"What's his line?"
"Oh, nothing much-fruit jars and the like. He fusses
around sometimes with vases and jardinières, but he's not a
chemist, and he can't get colors. He has a little one-horse
shop that he plays around in during the summer.”
"What's his business in winter?"
"Used to dig coal-until the mines went out."
While Giles's eyes were lowered, H. Pearsall looked him
over. Here was something new.
"You were thinking my father might sell him some formu-
las, or something like that?”
"It flashed through my mind."
H. Pearsall's pink face brightened with kindliness and
shrewdness. "Say, I've got an idea. Suppose I write to
my dad and see what he'll do, and you accept that as pay
for the tutoring. You know I'm pretty convincing when I
write home. Have to be!"
Giles reflected. "My dad"-he liked to speak of him as
H. Pearsall had spoken of his "might not be able to buy
the formulas. And I don't imagine your dad would care
to sell. But if he'll agree to supply some colors ready
to be mixed or to be put on, I'll take you up."
H. Pearsall was delighted. He saw his father, short, a
trifle sturdy, and with cheeks still almost as pink as his
own, sitting at his walnut desk reading the letter and
smiling that his son was at last interested in pottery. And
Giles saw his father, as excited as a boy, sweaty and glow-
ing in the warm kiln at the pot-shop down in the hollow,
viewing at last, after a lifetime of waiting, a vase that had
the right coloring and glaze. The right kind of soft green!
Think of that! And the right kind of blue!
100
THE HILLIKIN
But these generous reflections brought no money. And
there had to be money. Mrs. Sanderson would be wanting
her twelve dollars at the end of the month.
He got a place as a waiter in a restaurant just off Har-
vard Square. It guaranteed him food-better food than
he had been living on-and a little money besides. But it
cut the heart out of study. Before the end of a fortnight,
he was disappointed; before the end of a month he was
in perpetual rebellion against such a regimen. Where were
those soft-jowled, pot-bellied men who made lofty speeches
at expensive dinners about the glories of working one's way
through college? He wanted to kick one of them in the
round paunch and bowl him into the river.
Still, examinations were farther away than hunger. For
the present he would labor and eat. Yet even when he
was fed, he could not escape thinking of money. Money!
Yes, money! How could one have money without losing
college?
Through some strange irony, he seemed to be growing
into an authority on the subject. As he hurried one eve-
ning from the restaurant to get at his college work, a man
came up alongside. "I don't believe you remember me,"
he said, "but we have met-though under slightly unusual

laugh. “Down at the river-do you remember? Or rather
in the river. I was the least bit under the weather. But
I'm all right now, as you see. Gunning is my name.'

"I've seen you in here, and I've wanted for some time

Giles felt honored, but he expressed fear that he was not

Giles had been thinking that he ought not to try to keep

Gunning was not certain that he could qualify as a

THE HILLIKIN
"Sure! I know a fellow who sings in a choir of a Sunday

That was the kind of job Giles was looking for, but he

When he had accumulated twenty dollars, he gave up the
restaurant to devote himself to college. Incidentally, he
read every Want Ad. in the newspapers. He cultivated the
acquaintance of men who had more profitable and less ex-
hausting jobs than his had been. A student from Indiana
whom he knew made good money by steadying an old
man who was shaky but who insisted on walking an hour
every day. Another-on Wendell Street-earned his board

-but not too hungry-boarders. Another went to Ran-
dolph Hall every other day and got a man up and saw

to bed. Another taught small boys in a Unitarian Sunday
School where he was at liberty to draw upon any resources,
sacred or profane, that would keep the boys occupied
throughout a half-hour.
One evening when he was bent over his desk in the
light of his green-shaded lamp, a pleasant, fair-haired Jew
who said his name was David Rathman came in to present
a proposition. He was interested in courses in art, and he
was paying his way by stamping names on key-ring markers
THE HILLIKIN
103
and suit-case labels. He tried to do an artistic job, he ex-

to pay him one large dollar for a German silver key-ring
marker bearing the letters, "Giles Dabney, Special."
Did Rathman know others who resorted to ingenuity in
order to stay in college? He was happy to say that he did.
One of his friends drafted ingenious excuses for three men
who were much absent from college. Another wrote letters
to fathers and mothers to let them know that their sons
were slightly ill-ill enough to stay in bed and receive an
extra check or two, but not ill enough to require a long
and expensive trip to Cambridge. Just the right balanc-
ing of the circumstances called for an amanuensis who
was not only discriminating but well-informed. The man
of Rathman's acquaintance, though, who was making the
best money, was engaged in an enterprise that required him
to make his mind into a kind of telescope which he could
invert as he wished to make things look either much larger
than they were or much smaller. He expanded classroom
notes for a select group of men-selected on the basis of
economic standing-who went to class too irregularly and
too perfunctorily to make notes of their own; and on the
other hand, he reduced fat volumes to ten-page summaries,
so that a man could make some general acquaintance with
the contents without loss of important time from social
enterprises.
The world seemed to be filled with men whose eyes had
to be sharpened to a more desperate need than meeting
the requirements of college courses. Giles thought he could
distinguish one of these men as far as he could see him,
from the free man who moved along the board walks in less
desperate fashion. He discovered, however, that in one
instance he had long been deceived. The well-dressed,

THE HILLIKIN
105
"You see I have a theory that a man ought to come out

"Last year I got a good job with a concern that answers

through all the vital statistics to see if there was anybody
else and there was the idea! I figured up that if I'd
buy all the papers for a given day-three hundred or so-
I'd have five or six thousand deaths of adults reported.
Not these elaborate obituaries that Cambridge women look
eagerly forward to in the Transcript! But always dates of
birth and death, and street address-the important infor-

Suppose you'd send a package C.O.D. to each one of them!
You see how it would be, without my telling you. They'd
say, 'Oh, I don't know what it is, but it's something poor
dear Henry ordered before he left us, and it's only a
dollar.'

THE HILLIKIN
"I paid cash for two thousand pairs of these, and I got

Yet with all the many examples before him, Giles was
unable to find any easy money. "It takes time," David
Rathman counseled. Evidently it did.
The college year had been only a struggle for money.
When for months had he thought about anything else?
If he went back to the restaurant, there would be no
time left for what was supposedly his chief concern in
Cambridge. But at last he did go back-only to have the
stolid, fat proprietor tell him that he was already carrying
more men than his trade required.
What was left of the poetic world faded with accelerated
swiftness one morning when the postman arrived. The
college office begged to remind Mr. Dabney that his work
was falling below the standard of the preceding year. In
fact, one professor reported him in danger.
The College Yard, the sacred spot of his early days in
THE HILLIKIN
107
Cambridge, began to symbolize restriction and hurt. His
chief great desire was only to get away as soon as possible.
"When I get out of this place" he said when he walked
in loneliness and saw gray-bearded professors and cheerful-

as if they were enjoying what they were about "When
I get out of this place." On this particular occasion he
walked along Quincy Street.
In an effort to catch a more hopeful breath, he walked
on down toward the river. He reflected that hunger-
the physical hunger of which he had never so much as
dreamed in all the frowsiness of Wiggam's Glory-was
only a few days ahead.
"Why so sad?" somebody called cheerfully from across
the narrow street.
It was Gunning.
"I've been wanting to see you," he began amiably.
"Wanted to tell you how grateful we've been for what you
tipped us off to last fall. We got the idea-to cash in on
the things we know best. You'll be amazed-perfectly
dumfounded-when I tell you what we've been doing."
He laughed a dinner-party laugh. "But we've been as
prosperous as Shylocks."
Giles was eager at once.
"But you'll never believe it-never."
He waited a few steps to let Giles prepare himself.
"We've been providing Harvard atmosphere-at dinner-

killing? We are ostensibly engaged as 'help'; two strug-

never anything to do that can't be done in dinner clothes.
That is all understood by the dowagers who engage us.
And, of course, it is understood by us! Our real function

THE HILLIKIN
is to be brilliant, to shine, to glitter, to elicit exclamation

"I specialize in art-but with a capital A, you know.

so much as made a payment on one of Max's slightly used

When Gunning left him, Giles walked on in bewilderment,
looking for work in Central Square, in Inman Square, in

He must have a bite to eat. He ate it in a restaurant
where the jar of mustard on the counter had dried up while
it waited for customers. He read a tousled morning paper
left by somebody, listened to a man and a woman in the
back room trying to outdo each other in applying strong
epithets to each other, and then after darkness wandered
homeward.
In a vacant lot a premature summer carnival, a dis-
reputable assemblage, held forth. A stout but flabby looking
man in tights stood before a tent with his arms folded to
display his enormous muscles while a barker announced that
this giant-the strongest man in the world-would lift a
seven-hundred-and-fifty-pound weight. If anybody doubted
that this was the true weight, he could look at the figures
on the side of it himself. The strong man would perform
other superhuman feats. As a preliminary to all of these,
he would wrestle anybody in the audience who cared to
come forward and risk having his bones crushed. "If any
person puts him down, that person will receive a ten-dollar
bill. Here it is!"
Giles paid an admission and went in. When the offer
was repeated he went forward.
The strong man snorted and smiled, and the crowd howled
110
THE HILLIKIN
in glee. Whether they were to see a man's bones crushed
or not, they would see something.
The barker generously implored Giles not to risk his life.
"Remember," he said, addressing the crowd, "the manage-
ment positively will not be held responsible for broken
bones. You all hear that, don't you?"
"Ye-ah, we hear!" the crowd roared. "Turn 'em loose."
The giant was as soft and as awkward as Giles had
guessed. It was no special trouble at all to tumble him to
the mat like a hogshead.
The barker declared that the fall was a fluke of some
kind. The strong man had never been put down before.
But when the crowd booed and began to call for their money
back, he gave Giles the ten-dollar bill.
Giles went again the next evening. He kept in the back-
ground, but the barker saw him, withheld the offer, and
then came privately and told him that it would be just as
well for his health if he never visited the carnival grounds
again.
But before the ten-dollar bill was gone, David Rathman
came in one evening to say as opportunely as if he had
stepped from the page of a story book that he had learned
of a very elderly Cambridge lady who was looking for a nice
student to read to her of afternoons.
She usually went to sleep soon after Giles began to read.
So it was always possible for him to work a little in prepara-
tion for examinations while he was ostensibly engaged in
earning an adequate supply of bread.

CHAPTER XI
E was to go down to Maine for the summer and
read for the pleasant old lady whenever she re-
quired and receive money. That was the ar-
rangement, yes. But before she was ready to depart from
Cambridge, she leaned back in her wing chair one afternoon
for a nap, dropped smilingly to sleep, and did not wake
up again.
Perhaps it was just as well; for only a day or two after-
ward his father, to whom he had not yet written about his
proposed summer in Maine, addressed an important letter
to him. "Your mother," his father wrote in his customary
manner of subordinating himself, "is rather anxious to see
you. So here's money enough to pay your way home.
Maybe we'll be able to scrape enough together to pay your
way back in September."
And yet he found it difficult to feel sure that he wanted
to go. To go might mean that he was never to come back.
Did he dread more the slow, numbing death of being stuck
in the hills, or the unending acute pain of trying to stay
in Harvard?
Eventually he decided—and he decided to go. It would
not be quite the triumphal return he had once dreamed of
making, but it would be a return. For his father and
mother, who had never been away, there would possibly be
something of triumph in it. He could tell them about many
places. Now that he thought about the journey a little
more intently, he felt ever so slightly exalted at the
prospect.
The next day he felt more exalted still. He received a
111
112
THE HILLIKIN
letter from New Jersey. "My son," the letter went on to
say, "has told me about your father's interest in pottery. If
you will stop off when you next chance to pass through
Trenton, I shall be glad to go over the case with you."
"When you next chance to pass through Trenton!" Did
a man on his uppers make trips through New Jersey every
two or three days? Yet in this instance he could write and
say to Mr. Dwyer that he hoped to be passing through
New Jersey on the following Wednesday and would stop
off in Trenton in the early afternoon.
On the boat from Boston to New York, he came upon
John L. Jones, who immediately invited him to sleep in the
upper berth in his stateroom-"since I can't very well sleep
in them both." John L. Jones was in a talkative mood. He
confessed that his minor ambition in life was to possess a
Salvation Army girl, and that his major ambition was
always to be so situated that he could wring the other
fellow's neck. He had made a little money-quite a little-
on his Invisible Suspenders.
"I made money, all right. But the other day I heard
that somebody or other wanted to see the president of the
Dorchester Distributing Company. So I did not go back.
When I get to New York, I'll ask the Dorchester post office
to forward my letters-if any, you see-to Seattle, Wash-
ington. After a time, I'll write a note from somewhere
else and send some money and ask that they be forwarded
to Mexico City. If anybody tries to have some Spaniard
sit around and wait for Quigley till I report, he'll pay out
a hell of a lot of money."
The next morning Giles was off the boat soon after the
longshoremen began to roll all the barrels in the world across
the deck just above his berth. By noon he was on his way
to Trenton, and five minutes before the appointed hour he
was at Mr. Dwyer's office.
Mr. Dwyer was a stocky man with a square, serious face
THE HILLIKIN
113
almost as pink as H. Pearsall's, and with very abrupt but

Immediately H. Pearsall appeared and stood at attention.
"You are to take care of any order that comes from

H. Pearsall, as sleek and as pink as when he sat in Pro-

back, he said with a smile which indicated that they were
somehow on the same side of a barricade that divided the
people of the world, "Sit down till I come back."
At last he reappeared. "I'm having some samples packed
up for you," he said slyly, as if he were carving "To Hell
With Yale" while Professor Blount lectured.
He sat down jauntily in his comfortable desk chair, leaned
back, and said without affectation, "I sure did appreciate
the way you pulled me through gay Etheredge's course."
Gay Etheredge! They discussed all the vulnerable points
in his character-and they found several; they discussed
college in general; they entered into a consideration of all
that might be expected when college was over.
The door from the factory opened and a man in white
overalls and a white cap that advertised hardware appeared
with a large rectangular, heavy-looking bundle tied with
stout rope and with a wooden hand-grip on top. When he
had withdrawn H. Pearsall said, "Take this to your dad,
with my compliments—if you can swing it. Or maybe you'd
better say with mine and yours. There's plenty in there to

Culbertson was only one-tenth as large as it had been
two years before. Part of the shrinkage was the result of
Giles's sojourn in suburban Boston; part of it was actual.
With the mines gone, there was no business to do; so why

Windows were plastered over with faded circus posters.
Nobody was left. Yes, he heard somebody-some men's

You've been away a year, haven't you?"
It was Theda, who had once tried to lead him to the

She still held it.
"Two years," he corrected her.

As soon as he left his father and mother's hill he was
in a young wilderness. Two or three years, added to the
earlier years of abandonment by Coal, had obscured a land-
scape that he thought he knew to the last square yard.
Blackberry briars growing in uncombated freedom had
spread till it was difficult to creep along the path. Hickory
and black-walnut bushes two or three feet high had sprung
up in the path itself. And always, in every low spot where
there was moisture without direct contact with the red
sulphur water from abandoned mines, horseweeds were
pushing upward in tropical luxuriance.
When at last he came out of the jungle above Company
Row, his face and hands smarting, his coat and trousers
revealing loops of woolen thread that tenacious briars had
pulled from the texture, he stood aghast. Only a few of
the black houses were left. He had seen often enough what
happens when human habitations are abandoned wholesale,
yet he was not prepared to see this utter desolation of the
Row. What had become of the houses themselves in two
years-houses that had slammed and banged with cramped,

126
THE HILLIKIN
where the hours were proclaimed by bells in ivy-clad towers,

No, if this quagmire of life in which he suffocated at the

The quagmire was more than a quagmire; it was a fun-
nel-a funnel from which there was no escape except to

Had he never tasted the other life, had he never known
of its existence more than his father and mother had, his
suffering might not have been quite unbearable. But to

other world until one should at last tumble into an incon-
spicuous and briary grave! Suppose he had dwelt in that
other world very modestly! Suppose not one person in
Harvard Square should ever miss him if he never returned
there! The hell of living in a dirty, suffocating world
when his memories were always of another would be a
thousand times more harrowing than any orthodox hell
promulgated by the long line of ministers who had shouted
at him throughout his youth.
On his way back, the briars scratched him again and

By the next morning Giles knew he meant not to stay

His mother would suffer. As she stood relaxed over the

great smothering if he remained in this region which men
