CHAPTER XIII
IS confidence still bore him up when the heavy
train from the West swept down along the Charles
into Brighton and Allston. Old Mem! There was
the bulging tower, there far across the lowlands. To-day,
thousands of his generation were converging on that tower.
They were coming. They were coming from everywhere.
Suppose he should take the roads back which all these
thousands had followed since yesterday, or the day before,
or the day before that-what kind of houses would he find?
How many of these houses would be surrounded by such
life as he had come from, where the deformed spirits of men
and women were kept in a lifelong retreat in their battle to
believe that not all was degradation?
Suppose one were to assemble all the houses in one
compact row arranged alphabetically according to the list
of names of students in the University Catalogue! Would
there not be some discrepancies in architecture? How
many doting mothers would choose to call on the mothers
who according to the impartial sequence of the alphabet lived
next door? Certainly the mothers would present a greater
diversity than the sons. The sons had all sorts of aspira-
tions or none; yet something they had in common. It
brought them all back to Cambridge this morning.
And he was one of this vast army!
But soon his confidence was put to the test. College
bills had gone up. And when he went to learn whether he
might hope to become a candidate for a degree, a brusque
little man told him that while such a thing could be done,
it was at least as difficult as for a rich man to experience
the kingdom of heaven.

THE HILLIKIN
Giles suffered a collapse of spirit. Then he grew feverish.
What if he did have to make up entrance examinations?
What if he did have to work late in a noisy restaurant and
then tutor a lazy man when he himself ought to be working
on his own courses? Could he not keep the rush of exac-
tions distributed? He would utilize every minute. He
must keep going without going under.
While other men walked in leisure, and talked without
desperation, and proclaimed to one another that no small
part of the good life was a sense of being undisturbed, he
brushed them on the board walks, bumping into those who
failed to do their half in giving way, and thought only of the
irreparable consequences of falling behind in his schedule.
He hurried when he got up. He hurried to work, and
from work to class and from class to work, and from work
to his room. Then when his eyelids were red and swollen
with fatigue, he dropped into bed to sleep a little so that
he could get up and hurry through another day of the
same kind.
One day when he rushed across the Yard with his mind
desperately fixed on getting somewhere before it was too
late, he almost ran over Professor MacLaurin.
"Damn you, Dabney!" the old professor exclaimed, half
in hotness, half in amusement. But what he wanted was
not an apology, but an explanation of why Giles had not
been coming to sit in the firelight with him.
Giles hurried through an explanation which left Professor
MacLaurin in possession of a reminder that if any man who
worked his way as Giles was doing should get two hours
behind schedule, that meant two more hours to be subtracted
from sleep.
The next day Giles received a letter from Professor Mac-
Laurin's address. "Music, my dear young man, is just
as essential as sleep." Clipped to one corner of the large
THE HILLIKIN
133
sheet of paper was a season ticket to the Sanders Theater

So once a month he listened to music and knew that life

lived in another part of town now, and his year's absence

any case, before each performance, and during intermissions,

There were the Aldriches, for instance. They were among
the elect non-Harvard persons who always had good seats
at the Harvard Symphonies. He already knew what Mrs.
Aldrich was like. And although he did not know Mr. Aldrich
so well, he knew what he was like also. He was intelligent.
He was informed. He found great pleasure in promoting
the elegancies of culture among those who stood in least
need-among those who had long ago become masters in
"the joy of living everyone's life but one's own." Thanks
to stern grandfathers, Mr. Aldrich was able to coast along
without economic worries, at peace in the cool assumption
that he was superior by gift of heaven to all other mortals
except a few of his own class. Toward any spiritual aristo-
crat left by less lucky grandfathers in a lower economic
category, he was precisely as sympathetic as toward the man
in the moon.
Giles preferred to look at the new president and his wife.
The new president was still a devoted lover at fifty-five.
Anybody who looked could see that he had always been
a devoted lover, and that he always would be. Giles liked
the half-playful manner in which the president spoke, and
the dignified affection with which his wife listened. Dare
he think it—yes, he did: her dark hair and brows were like
his own mother's, back there in Wiggam's Glory. If only

THE HILLIKIN
his mother had been able to get away! His body ached

He liked to study the group as a group, too—the aca-

universe, orbits sometimes crossed, and there resulted oscil-
lations and collisions and explosions. In three years a
man begins to pick up shreds of what is going on, especially
when he works where all sorts of people do much of their
talking. And if he went out into the transept of Memorial
Hall while the orchestra caught its breath, he could see
how graciously the affinities and antipathies sorted each
other out.
But always after a day or two of hurrying with closely
concentrated mind and body, the rhythm of the symphonies
and suites left his ears, and the implacable march of col-
lege superseded it. He must not lose step. If he should
develop appendicitis, or contract pneumonia, or get so little
sleep that his nerves should snap, the result would be dis-

an education would be over some day and he could win
content in a free world. Whenever he felt the curious eyes
of the less hurried upon him, he only built higher the
impenetrable wall around himself and kept more alone.
One day at noon in the restaurant when he was so sleepy
that he was dizzy, two men sat down at a table which he
was clearing. He noticed first the pink-faced old man be-
cause his eyelids were red. The old man evidently stayed
up also and read after the rest of the world had gone to
sleep. The other man had a younger, Spanish-looking face
and a classic head. While Giles kept shoving plates under

135
the outer edge of the rest of the dishes on the tray, he

"Just so! Just so! Spirit, since its essence is to aspire,

It was as if the man had touched Giles's hand with a
great new charge of physical energy. It was as if he had
with some magic gesture swept all of Giles's temperamental
and enforced non-conformities into a unified onrush of
belief in himself. Hesitantly, defensively he had dared to
believe that he possessed qualities worth keeping. But
now here was a disinterested and impartial witness who
assured him that he might believe unhesitantly and posi-

He swung the tray of dishes high on his open hand with
an ease he had never known. He made his way down the
long aisle between tables-a sturdy six-footer who looked
taller when he carried a tray-not minding how many
people looked at him the second time or what they said.
As he kicked the swinging door open with a huge foot and
slid the heavy tray onto the platform of the dishwasher,
he was sharply aware for the first time in his life that in
the unusual combination of inheritance, childhood, and im-
mediate circumstance, destiny had pressed upon him a po-
tential advantage. So much was above him that unless he
sought to become all that he had not been, he was just
mud. He had come to life. Many of these about him
might now be a little finer mud than he was, but they had
not come to life. They would never change their quality.
"Spirit!" Always before the word had been odious to

was the path toward Mrs. Fealty's and No. 5 Company
Row. He would follow that.
As soon as he left his father and mother's hill he was
in a young wilderness. Two or three years, added to the
earlier years of abandonment by Coal, had obscured a land-
scape that he thought he knew to the last square yard.
Blackberry briars growing in uncombated freedom had
spread till it was difficult to creep along the path. Hickory
and black-walnut bushes two or three feet high had sprung
up in the path itself. And always, in every low spot where
there was moisture without direct contact with the red
sulphur water from abandoned mines, horseweeds were
pushing upward in tropical luxuriance.
When at last he came out of the jungle above Company
Row, his face and hands smarting, his coat and trousers
revealing loops of woolen thread that tenacious briars had
pulled from the texture, he stood aghast. Only a few of
the black houses were left. He had seen often enough what
happens when human habitations are abandoned wholesale,
yet he was not prepared to see this utter desolation of the
Row. What had become of the houses themselves in two
years-houses that had slammed and banged with cramped,

He hurried to his room. He had to read a play. It was
called The Pretenders. He thought he could have written a
better one himself. Was he not accumulating material on
that subject?
He felt amused. Yet he could not save himself from
irritability when every pretender he knew was having an
easier time of it than he was. Where was the logic of
the world? The art of life seemed to consist of the art
of hocus-pocus.
The year whirled toward an end. Could he keep going?
Well, here he was, still going-still going. Sometimes a
mighty rage sustained him; let people look at him if it would
do them any good! Sometimes he was inferior and over-
sensitive, and ready to throw down tray or book-bag and
engage in a fist fight with anybody who dared to look
at him the second time. The words of the Spaniard were
muddied over.
One day as he hurried along Washington Street in Boston

"Tea-hound!" Giles muttered to himself in contempt.
And yet he had to admit that Housleton would probably

CHAPTER XIV
ILES sat in Professor Tillinghast's class and slept.
Not that Professor Tillinghast was a soporific.
He was, in truth, the right kind of combination of
informed man, active man, and poet to make a perfect
teacher. He talked about Greece and his listeners saw the
shores of the Mediterranean illuminated by the brilliance
of one city. He spoke of the Renaissance and they saw
the entire western world touched into life by that strange
fusion of ideals. He spoke of ugliness glorified in the world
to-day, and for the first time they saw how easy it is for
men to sleep while their world befouls itself. He pictured
an ideal world which men could have for the asking—if
they asked ably and persistently enough-and even the
dullest of his students caught fleeting clairvoyant glimpses
of something half-seemly, and stirred the least bit in their
seats as if they could almost be prevailed upon to help
awaken the sleeping and the dead. "When you come back
to your class reunions," he admonished them, "be sure to
bring reports of what you have done to banish ugliness and
make the world a livable place."
When Giles had first listened to Professor Tillinghast,
he was sure he himself was a poet and a seer. In the
restaurant if he wearied of listening to two men who tried
to choose between baked halibut and fried scallops, or if
his arm ached from carrying one tray-load of dishes after
another from smeary tables to the kitchen, there was always
in the back of his mind the heartening reality of what
Professor Tillinghast had said. He was able to look into
the steam over the crunching dishwasher and see the
141
142
THE HILLIKIN
vigorous figure and the bronzed, earnest face of this poetic
teacher.
But as Giles struggled in his effort never to be too late
at a given point, he began to feel a creak in his back be-
tween his shoulders. Sometimes when he swung the tray
of dishes aloft on his hand the creak became a sharp hitch.
Then he began to suffer pains in the back of his neck. Nor
did they leave him when he sat down in the quiet to study.
Instead, they reached up and spread over his head and
brows and face. If they became unbearable, he tied a cold
towel tightly round his head and studied on.
The pains were gone after a night of sleep. That fact
comforted him when he was obliged to put work aside and
go to bed. But eventually he began to feel traces of them
in the morning. And then they were almost as sharp in
the morning as they had been the night before. With the
sharpness there was a dull heaviness in his right arm and
shoulder. It would be heavenly to stay in bed and sleep for
another hour, but he made himself get up and face the day.
So he chanced to fall asleep in the course that was his
favorite. The man next to him thought he saw Professor
Tillinghast cast a glance in that direction, and gave Giles
a bump with his knee. Giles quickly knew what had hap-
pened and sat with eyes half-closed as though he had been
only feigning sleep. Then he casually resumed the taking
of notes.
At the end of the hour Professor Tillinghast requested
three or four men, including Mr. Dabney, to tarry for a
word.
The other men evidently conferred about agreeable mat-
ters. As they finished, one by one, they hurried cheerfully
from the room. Giles waited. Professor Tillinghast gath-

on his fur-lined coat and easy-looking hat, and was ready
to go. "I'm in a hurry," he explained; "I have a bundle

'Expect'! There's no expecting about it. It's settled.
I know you've got nothing else to do or you wouldn't be
here. I'll 'expect' something myself. I'll 'expect' you to
be over here good and early to-morrow morning-and every
other morning till the fall-roses are blooming."
That was why Giles did not tell his mother that his stay
was to be brief. That was why he was able to spend lazy
afternoons riding over the hills with old Doc Seabright, and
between cases of cholera morbus and hands yanked off
like shredded meat by a threshing machine, between
chronic rheumatism and chronic defeatism, between tiny
births and tinier deaths, hear all sorts of non-academic com-
mentary on why men and women are not thoroughbred
angels. That was why when Genevieve paid him in Sep-
tember and he walked homeward through deep green woods
where Virginia creeper as bright as blood marked the out-
lines of tree limbs fifty or sixty feet from the ground, he
was filled with such confidence that he took it for granted
not only that he was going back to Harvard, but that he
meant to get out of the pariah class known as Special Stu-
dents, and into the class of prospective candidates for a
degree.
His father warned him. He himself had once tried to
get out of the class he was in-the coal-digger class. No-
body dare call it an easy thing to attempt. When a man
has once been put in a pigeon-hole, people do not like to
have their classifications all upset some day by finding him

CHAPTER XIII
IS confidence still bore him up when the heavy
train from the West swept down along the Charles
into Brighton and Allston. Old Mem! There was
the bulging tower, there far across the lowlands. To-day,
thousands of his generation were converging on that tower.
They were coming. They were coming from everywhere.
Suppose he should take the roads back which all these
thousands had followed since yesterday, or the day before,
or the day before that—what kind of houses would he find?
How many of these houses would be surrounded by such
life as he had come from, where the deformed spirits of men
and women were kept in a lifelong retreat in their battle to

people looked at him the second time or what they said.
As he kicked the swinging door open with a huge foot and
slid the heavy tray onto the platform of the dishwasher,
he was sharply aware for the first time in his life that in
the unusual combination of inheritance, childhood, and im-
mediate circumstance, destiny had pressed upon him a po-
tential advantage. So much was above him that unless he
sought to become all that he had not been, he was just
mud. He had come to life. Many of these about him
might now be a little finer mud than he was, but they had
not come to life. They would never change their quality.
"Spirit!" Always before the word had been odious to
him. Mr. Avery, the Methodist preacher back in Wiggam's
Glory, had always used it as if it meant a kind of translucent
dead man. But this Spaniard had used it as if it meant
something else—something that was very much alive, some-
thing that was aliveness itself. Giles felt that he under-

He made inquiries. Nobody could answer them.
All day he walked and thought, and ate nothing. That

crazed, yet dead, he went to the dean's office the next
morning as soon as it was open.
"I was just trying to reach you by telephone," the dean

telegraphed this morning that I should add your name to

So Professor Martell had- Well, Giles did not care what

He only knew that his knees trembled until he might sink

"Are you glad it's all over?" the dean inquired in kind

"Yes." He could say no more. He could scarcely say

"By the way," the dean went on as if it were all a part
of the day's work, "here is a guest ticket for the Commence-

Giles had no friend who could use the ticket, but he
accepted it. It was good of the dean to keep it for him.
He ate a huge breakfast. But he ate too rapidly. With
every bite he saw anew how narrow his escape must have
been.

When they parted, Huntt's pitying look did not humiliate

He hurried to his room. He had to read a play. It was
called The Pretenders. He thought he could have written a
better one himself. Was he not accumulating material on
that subject?
He felt amused. Yet he could not save himself from
irritability when every pretender he knew was having an
easier time of it than he was. Where was the logic of
the world? The art of life seemed to consist of the art
of hocus-pocus.
The year whirled toward an end. Could he keep going?
Well, here he was, still going-still going. Sometimes a
mighty rage sustained him; let people look at him if it would
do them any good! Sometimes he was inferior and over-
sensitive, and ready to throw down tray or book-bag and
engage in a fist fight with anybody who dared to look
at him the second time. The words of the Spaniard were
muddied over.
One day as he hurried along Washington Street in Boston
toward a shoe store where a sale was in progress, and
stepped close to a doorway to avoid the crowd, he brushed
past Mrs. Aldrich and Housleton as they entered a tea
room. Mrs. Aldrich was listening radiantly while Housle-
ton discussed the increasing favor of South Sea philosophies.
"Tea-hound!" Giles muttered to himself in contempt.
And yet he had to admit that Housleton would probably
hire a corps of tutors and graduate with better grades than
he was going to be able to make himself.
He continued in a snarly temper for days. He was in a
snarly temper when he finished his last examination. He

CHAPTER I
'D like you to get a good first impression," the round
little president of Steel City College explained as he
conducted Giles Dabney over the shaded academic
The president was a self-centered man who covered over
his egotism with a coating of joviality not unlike the sweet-
ness of fly-paper. He wanted to bind people to him—if he
could.
Giles Dabney was impatient. He had spent a long sum-
mer among his native Ohio hills. He had accumulated
boundless energy. In the process, he had cast off the last
vestige of the inferiority that sometimes troubled him in
Cambridge. He was henceforth to be a different Giles Dab-
ney. He was burning to plunge into teaching, to move
mountains, to remake the world. And now, instead, he had
to walk along and seem interested in the president's long-
winded account of each recitation hall on the campus, just
as if it were not like a thousand other recitation halls on a
thousand other campuses.
When they had at last passed on from an ugly old brick
building covered so completely with ivy that it was rendered
almost beautiful, they came to two extra-size yellow Italian
villas among towering shellbark hickories.
"These represent a new set of influences in the college,"
the president observed without making himself altogether
clear. A little farther on along the ridge when they came
to a battlemented citadel in stone, he remarked cheerily but
with the least trace of apology, "The man who donated that
building likes stone; in fact, deals in stone."
157
158
THE HILLIKIN
He cast a glance at Giles's inquisitive face and im-
mediately felt uneasy. So he shifted deftly to the subject
of stone in general, then to the geologic formation of the
hill, and then to the topography of that part of the state.
Evidently this was one of his favorite subjects, for he dis-
coursed upon it with ease until they came to a rustic pergola
high on the narrow point of the ridge.
He paused, made a sweeping gesture, and said proudly:
"But this is what I really brought you out to see."
Steel City spread beneath them so close to the precipitous
hillside that they might have taken a running leap and
plunged several hundred feet into the heart of it.
"Now, what do you say to that?" the president asked,
his ruddy face beaming.
It was an inferno of smoke and flame and steam. Smoke
belched and tumbled and rolled from stacks without num-
ber-from stacks in huddles, in long rows, in hollow squares.
Smoke obscured everything within reach of the eye-the
jagged line of skyscrapers on the left, the maze of canal-
like streets, the river and its nondescript bridges, the en-
veloping threads of railroads that swept in parallel curves
toward a notch in the hills far on the right, and, beyond the
mills and the railroads, an expanse of lake that might have
been blue if one could have seen it without looking through
smoke.
The president awaited the first fair impress of this mighty
civilization.
"Pretty terrible smoke!" at last Giles remarked.
The president winced as if he had received a pin prick
when he had been expecting a caress.
"Pretty good smoke-for us," he replied with emphatic
joviality. "Smoke means endowment."
He became a more open protagonist. "No, sir! We
don't take anybody's dust when it comes to industry. And
we are moving right along in education and culture. We

Giles hurried across the Yard toward the restaurant. He
could live, he was certain, for at least a week without any
sleep whatever.
But soon the weariness overcame him again. There
seemed to be no pulse in life, no currents of mind. Men
looked like so much animal matter moving about-and not
very interesting animal matter at that. And he felt like
mere animal matter himself.
One afternoon he walked out of the Cockpit in Sever Hall
trying to decide as he went whether he should go to his
room to rest awhile, or go directly to the restaurant, put
his head down on a quiet table in a corner somewhere, and
rest there. In hesitation he allowed himself to be carried
along with the crowd toward Thayer and University.
"Well, sir, I'm glad to see you still on your feet," a
mature friendly voice said close to his ear. "What are
your plans for the next half-hour?"
Giles admitted that he was trying at that very moment
to formulate them.
"Then come and go to a University Tea with me. Ever
gone to one? No, I see you haven't. There are plenty of
others in that class. But come along. I want you to meet
Mrs. Tillinghast and some of our friends, and I want to talk
with you about a matter."
"But I'm not dressed."
"Yes, you are. I'm going right from my classroom.
You're all right. Come along."

easy to talk with Mrs. Tillinghast, to talk with other wives

"Now this is what I had in mind. What have you
planned to do next year?"
Giles confessed that the present year was large enough
to keep him occupied without looking beyond it.
Professor Tillinghast then told him of a letter he had
received from an institution a distance away that was
thinking of engaging a man to develop courses in landscape
architecture. "You know the subject is new in most uni-
versities. This president would be quite ready to start with
a young fellow who holds only a bachelor's degree-if he can
meet the rest of the array of requirements. The institution
is up on the high rim of the city it adorns, and you might
have a chance to do something-though I don't know."
Giles read the letter as he walked slowly along. The
president, it seemed, was interested more in almost any-
thing else than the candidate's knowledge of his subject.
He wanted, above all, "dynamic personality, ability to fit
in with the citizens of the great and growing city and with
friends of education generally, tact in dealing with red-
blooded students, and strong Christian character. I am of
the opinion that landscape architecture is a coming subject
and I wish my institution to get credit for being one of the
first in the field."
"Hadn't thought of teaching," Giles explained.
"It's one way to get along. You might hit upon some-
thing-if you think you can stand the-the incidentals."
Giles was ready to try. He wanted to feel sure of some-
thing ahead for once in his life. Yes, he would accept the
post if Professor Tillinghast recommended him.

A few days later the president telegraphed to confirm the

How easy!
Now, if he could only hold off the impending doom to-day,
to-morrow, and every day between now and summer!
He felt his legs growing wearier, his back and neck more
like a piece of dried meat, his thinking more difficult to
command. When he sat down in his room, he usually be-
came aware a half-hour later-or an hour-that he had been
staring at the wall all the while.
He managed to keep going until final examinations were
upon him. He began a last review of his notes and reading.
One day when he had gone over a dozen pages of his notes
without remembering what was written on a single page, he
sat and wished the postman would come and bring him a
letter that would clarify his mind. The postman was a
trifle late, but he came; and he brought a letter. It was
from a professor. This professor requested Giles to come
to his office.
When he arrived the professor confronted him with some
reports on reading. "They are without the slightest spark
of originality, and sometimes they are nothing more than
so many empty sentences strung together incoherently. I
am the more disappointed because your first report last
autumn was promising.
"Now, this is what I wished to say: If you care to take
the final examination, of course it is your privilege. But
really, I cannot hold out great hope for you, and I thought

149
take your degree then? In that case you would not need

Not to try for credit? As a matter of fact, he was taking
the course for little else. Spend another year here? He
should much prefer to jump into the Atlantic Ocean. No,
he told the professor, he meant to take the examination.
It came last on his schedule, and he would have some extra
time for preparation.
He felt like a wooden man. But he meant to keep going;
a hundred times a day he bolstered up his spirit by making
the overt declaration to himself.
He knew now that the life he saw about him in the Yard
was but a dream. There was no conceivable reality in men
who walked pleasantly, confidently about when examinations
were only days away. As reasonable as to expect men to
be pleasant and confident when they faced the electric chair!
He went through the first examination; the second; the
third. Now he had three days before the last one. He
would make old "Hatchet" Martell eat his words.
He went to sleep when he studied, roused himself, walked
in the sun to a given street corner and back, studied again,
went to sleep again. The night before the examination he
was so weary that he was not troubled with sleeplessness,
as he had feared he might be.
He was still asleep long after his customary time of aris
ing. When at last he did turn over and look at his watch,

examination, to be sure. And what was that he meant to
look up at the library on his way to the examination room?
Perhaps it did not much matter, since he could not think
of it. Were there other points likely to be dwelt upon that
he should look up? He could think of none.

The students in the French class were well schooled in habits
of laziness, and consequently were little disposed to ask
questions. They were likable, moreover, and listened with
interest to his frequent asides on French gardens and French
châteaux.
The next year he had twice as many students in his own
courses-and twice as many in French. Time passed
swiftly enough. Toward the end of his second year when
the college was preparing the catalogue for the following year
he announced a first-year course in city planning.
"Reads mighty well, Professor; mighty well," the presi-
dent assured him. "It will make a good impression on
friends of education. I hope I'll be able to relieve you of
that French in another year or two and make you a full
professor in your own field."
Giles Dabney was so ebullient with unwasted energy that
he troubled less than he should about whether he was ex-
pending it in his own field or in some other. It was good
just to encounter any kind of resistance and see it give way.
What if a few years were stealing by? Would there not

THE HILLIKIN
be plenty of others? Some day he would be established.

The dean of women did not like him. He was a great,
heavy browed, unsubdued, tempestuous person who always
looked at her as if there were something in the world to laugh
at. She could not for the life of her understand why some
of the girls secretly-and even half-openly-were so crazy
about him.
Faculty wives had their opinions, too. As often as not
he got on the wrong side of them when they were going to
the dinner table. He violated dinner etiquette, moreover,
by talking about things of importance. And if he liked a
woman, he talked to her too long. Was it possible, they all
wanted to know, that he was a typical Harvard man?

left the Theater, some part of Giles that was yet alive
found pleasure in the thought that he was a figure in the
endless, magnificent procession that marched in and out
of this building on successive commencement days.
A schoolgirl who was doing her best to be a young woman
pushed through the crowd to him.
"It was perfectly gorgeous!" she exclaimed. "And you
looked grand, just wonderful, in your cap and gown. And
I was the only person on our street that had a ticket. Mrs.
Aldrich, who thinks she is so smart and has to be in every-
thing, thought I was spoofing when I told her I was on my
way to the exercises."
They moved slowly among the begowned and behooded
professors, black and crimson and blue and purple and
scarlet and white and orange-every color adopted by in-
stitutions in a hundred parts of the world.
154
THE HILLIKIN
"This is where the intellect comes out in full bloom,"
isn't it?" Giles asked.
"Yes," Mickey answered. But she was thinking about
something else.
Giles walked on with her toward the point of the Delta.
"Hillikin," she suddenly asked with pretended eager-
ness, "don't you want Aggie? She and Perce have had
an awful bust-up."
Giles colored as he thought of taking such a plunge.
"Don't you?" And she tugged at the sleeve of his gown.
"Don't you?" She seemed half serious.
"I'm afraid that would be out of the question."
Mickey pretended that she thought not, but she did not
mind, if it seemed so to Giles.
"Just wish you the grandest success," she said in an
effort to be completely grown-up and casual, when they
were once at the more easily managed business of saying
good-by.
Giles was grateful for somebody's good wishes. But he
had to hurry. He must get over to the New Lecture Hall
where the diplomas were passed out after the exercises-now
that candidates for degrees had become so numerous. Then
he must try to see three or four professors before they were
out of sight, and the dean-who already was beginning to
assume the outlines of a friendly ally in his less disturbed
mind—and finish packing up his belongings, and have a
check cashed, and get to South Station, and catch a train
for the West. College seemed to be over.
PART TWO

CHAPTER II
ILES DABNEY had never imagined how deeply an
academic community could be stirred by the death
of a prominent manufacturer of improved can
openers. When the faculty assembled at the main brick
gate of the campus to take automobiles to the home of the
late Mr. Mott, it seemed that all the funereal clothing in
the neighborhood had been brought to light-including four
or five rather badly cow-licked silk hats.
The funeral proper was to be held in the First Presby-
terian Church. Mr. Mott had recently allowed his name
to be used in connection with the minister's proposed addi-
tion of basket-ball floors, swimming pools, and a Christian
dance hall to the church's equipment; so, since he was a
member of no church, the Presbyterian minister claimed
the privilege of saying the last sad rites. Inasmuch as the
services were thus to be public, President Wiswall was am-
bitious to have his faculty more completely represented than
any other organization in the city. He wished his organiza-
tion to make a favorable impression on all who might be
present, especially since the college was to receive such a
generous portion of Mr. Mott's estate. Nothing counted
like appreciation.
They drove slowly up the winding private avenue to the
porte-cochère of a long, low, pressed-brick house with wide
projecting tile roof. The ones who had most recently
breathed the heavy odor of floral tributes in their own houses
and had heard the dull last sounds at the final parting of
some one from their own lives listened with sinking hearts
to the subdued thud of automobile doors as they were
170
THE HILLIKIN
171
closed by a doorman or one of the undertakers' assistants.
But Giles in his youthful buoyance could but consider the
situation æsthetically. Why, he wondered, must new wealth
always express itself in beveled glass around doorways?
And why did it so often express itself in smooth, monotonous
bricks that were as exactly alike as a machine could make
them?
He was still pondering many such irrelevant questions
when they had all moved in undeniable but swift pomp to
the great spreading church on Central Avenue. This church
rambled so far over the landscape that it seemed to call for
no additions of any kind in order to be fully adequate for
all purposes of worshipful assembly.
It was, in any event, larger than the funeral required.
President Wiswall had no need to fear the impressiveness
of his college delegation. It was quite larger than that of
the Chamber of Commerce, which easily took second honors.
The church auditorium spread like a miniature football
stadium of golden oak round a pulpit that displayed more
oak of the same kind. The faculty sat in the front rows
of the middle section of pews-very prominently in so vast
an empty space. Just behind them sat the Chamber of
Commerce; and behind these, a few unattached business
acquaintances who took a long chance on sacrificing profits
for an hour in order to honor a man who was once just as
good as any of them. All sat stolidly in the heavy at-
mosphere of floral tributes which they had ordered sent.
A half-dozen very distant relatives slipped into the rear
pews on one side. Mr. Mott had never acknowledged them
as his kin-at least not in his later life. But now that he
was dead, they dreamed wistfully of some modest crumb
that might fall to them from his great abundance. Like
starving pheasants they stood ready to dash in and grab
up a good feed or two if there was so much as half a chance.
Likewise, Mrs. Christine Alworthy, who had been Mr.
172
THE HILLIKIN
Mott's housekeeper for years, slipped modestly into a side
pew.
The undertakers in long-tailed coats moved about with the
expressionless faces of mummies. In the heavy silence, a
flock of English sparrows burst into a wild chattering and
scolding in the ivy at the top of a high Gothic window and
filled the church with their echoes. The undertakers stood
in the aisles and nodded a few last directions. The organist
floated into a soft, tremulous prelude, and the choir in sub-
dued voices formally initiated the service.
President Wiswall had once been a minister, and there-
fore he had been invited by the pastor, Mr. Ellington, to
take a modest part in celebrating the memory of Mr. Mott.
As they sat behind the pulpit desk while the choir sang, they
seemed to express perfect content with all that Mr. Mott
had done in a long lifetime.
President Wiswall's prayer was discreet and low in tone
at the beginning; but it soon developed into an enthusiastic,
intimate address to the Almighty. President Wiswall re-
minded Him that most of His own qualities were to be
found in the record of the dear departed. The good man
had never been blessed with children-President Wiswall
said so without experiencing any hurt-but he had adopted
the entire city as his own. Especially had he been in-
terested in the education of youth. He had been circum-
spect, far-sighted, frugal, and abounding in unobserved
altruistic impulses.
President Wiswall went on and on, his round uplifted face
glowing with fervor, until Giles Dabney thought there would
be nothing left for the minister to say.
But there was. Mr. Ellington enlarged upon Mr. Mott's
interest in religion. He had not, it was true, always at-
tended the means of grace. But his entire life had been but
a kind of perpetual sacred service to humanity. On this
point Mr. Ellington expanded until his own professed Chris-
THE HILLIKIN
173
tian ministrations seemed in comparison altogether colorless
and puny. Yet even to these impotent pastoral ministra-
tions, Mr. Mott had always lent an indirect moral support.
Right up to the end of his life he was willing to listen when-
ever church enterprises were brought to his attention.
Especially had he shown interest in wholesome, steadying
amusement for the young.
President Wiswall gazed off in feigned calm. At the
moment he could not help wondering just the least bit if
Mr. Mott might not have deducted something for the swim-
ming pools and the Christian dance hall. But this inward
nervousness was fleeting. Suppose Mr. Mott had remem-
bered the church. Would there not be more than an
abundance left for the college? One must not be piggish.
The next morning the president sent for Giles. At last
there was to be relief from that course in French.
"We are now about to launch forth into an era of great
things—including the adoption of the name 'university' in-
stead of 'college.' I am holding up the printing of the
catalogue a few days for additional announcements. Sup-
pose you bring your ideas of a complete department to me
on paper. Could you do that-within a couple of days?"
Since Giles Dabney had been dreaming of little else, he
found no difficulty in putting something down on paper.
Two days later he went with his copy to the president's
office.
The gray-haired woman spoke to him as if she had dis-
agreeable thoughts in her mind. Quite brusquely she told
him to go in.
President Wiswall was pacing the floor in restrained fury.
Giles stood at the door hesitant for a moment as if he had
come upon some one engaged in an immorality.
"Come in!" the president commanded; "and sit down."
His wrath was in his voice as well as his legs. He paced
160
THE HILLIKIN
They went in.
"Oh, how do you do, Miss Stover!" the president said
with well-modulated cheeriness.
A shrinking, dried-up little woman of his own age flut-
tered and rustled like a clinging leaf in an October breeze.
"This is Mr. Dabney, the man I've spoken about. Or
maybe I'd better call him 'Professor.' Anyway, he is going
to start us off in landscape architecture."
In response to Miss Stover's bashful, nervous invitation,
Giles Dabney walked into the two rooms of the lower floor
and saw the cast of Venus de Milo and the figures from
the frieze of the Parthenon that constituted the department's
chief visible equipment.
Upstairs in the bare room with faded blue walls and torn
window blinds, the president grew confidential again. He
wanted his new faculty members to know how shrewdly he
was looking out for things. "She is the niece of another of
my trustees. Some day I expect to see the work in art
prosper."
He elaborated on the entire institution's bright future.
All the way down the stairs and on out into the shade of the
straight lines of maples, and across toward the new brown
brick administration building, he glowed over the im-
mediate and more remote prospects. "I think I've got
things shaped up so they'll not only stay put but get better."
Abruptly he dropped his voice. "Here comes one of my
trustees who lives over back of the campus. You are a
young man and I am-well, not so young; so you won't mind
if I suggest that I don't know as I'd swing along quite so
informally with my hands in my pockets. He might not
get a favorable first impression."
At the office he volunteered one further word of parental
advice. "You have your eyes open, I can see that. But
I have helped to mold the careers of a lot of college
teachers and good ones, too, even if I do say it myself.

175
me he had never in his life been known to have a generous

As his wrath descended to trivialities, Giles Dabney began

The president paused in his marchings and mopped his

""
He could not finish the sentence. He mopped his neck
again, dropped into his high-backed swivel chair, and panted
with great heaves. He leaned forward and let his face fall
limply into his hands. Giles wondered if he were to be
a lone witness to an apoplectic death.
After a moment President Wiswall sat up. "You'll have
to carry that French another year-there's simply nothing
else to be done. I wish we might continue the landscape
architecture. It gives a better impression than almost any-
thing we have added in ten years. But with the French
armies holding off the Germans as they are, and France's
prestige mounting every day, we've simply got to continue
all courses in French."
Giles Dabney stumbled from the office in a daze. What
was this the president had said about giving up landscape
architecture and going on with French?
How far he walked before he began to think, he did not
know. But he had not gone far before he began to see.
His inner life had followed a sharp declivity ever since his
arrival at Steel City College. Was this the logical culmina-
tion of his great dream?
He had come to Steel City with much of the poet in him.
Already he was far enough away from where he had started
to see that. Some of his seasoned colleagues had smiled at
176
THE HILLIKIN
his enthusiasms and remarked that he was very young.
Perhaps he had been. He had asked: "What cannot a
man do with all the world and all his life before him?"
Well, it was not difficult just now to see that a little of
life was no longer before him. And some of the world had
disappeared, too. He had seen himself planning civic cen-
ters and public gardens and sanitary groupings of factories,
and in a hundred other ways making cities livable. Yet
now here he was acting as a kind of bell-boy for a man
who directed education, but did not know what education
was.
He wandered on back through the campus toward the
open country. If he continued to walk, he might eventually
begin to think.
Always he had felt alien on the campus, but always he
had clung to the belief that some day he would feel more
at home. Now he knew that he did not belong there—that
he had never belonged there. There might be other
campuses where he did belong-but certainly not this one.
How far could he go and still get back in an hour and
a half?
He passed the huge shallow bowl of hillside and valley
that he had often dreamed of making into a park and lake.
Then he struck off across flattened hills that stood bright
but bare and unpromising in the late winter sun. The crisp
wind made further subtractions from any warmth in the
sun's brightness. Nothing that he could see contributed
much to buoyance. But at least there was no disin-
genuousness here, no pretense, no smothering of one's
spiritual integrity.
He came to the top of a ridge, and the long view ahead
filled him with a deep sense of freedom. To be alone! To
walk unrestrained in honesty of spirit without wondering
what kind of impression one were going to make! To feel
the cool sharp wind blowing everything clean of subterfuge!
THE HILLIKIN
177
To forget the whole world of pretense that human beings
had slowly built up into standards of conduct, and invite
the gods from unpolluted skies!
He wanted to laugh-at what he was not altogether cer-
tain. He was only filled with the conviction that there
was something in the world to be laughed at.
He stood on the ridge and shouted. He shouted as he
used to shout in his own hills through his long youth. Then
he laughed.
Yes, laughter was appropriate, there was no doubt about
that.
He threw his head back and laughed again. His voice
rebounded from the nearest hill. He tried again, and his
voice came back. Then he laughed on and on and listened
in profound content to the clear echo. Oh, what a joke there
was on somebody!
Abruptly he stopped laughing. Was not the joke on
himself?
He tried to straighten it all out. He had dreamed a great
dream. Well, was not that a perfectly appropriate thing
to do? Was not the dreaming of dreams the chief dif-
ference between human beings and some other beings that
are content to wallow in mud and their own dung? And it
had been an honest dream. It had from time to time
changed color a little, yes. Once he had thought that what
he sought was romance. Now he knew that what he sought
was reality. It had been an honest dream, in any event.
He had not tried to keep the breath of life in it by any
artificial respiration. It had been easy-natural, in fact—
to believe that he was being led by sure-footed destiny
toward enterprises that would give him a satisfying sense
of enlargement.
And here he had been inveigled into spending the better
part of five years doing chiefly nothing of consequence. He
had sat by the hour in senseless faculty meetings in which
178
THE HILLIKIN
grave-faced men and women sought to determine whether
young ladies should be sent home for climbing in at dormi-
tory windows after the dean's closing bell; or whether a foot-
ball player who had come up to commencement time two
and a half credits short of the prescribed number should be
permitted to sit on the platform or be required to receive
his diploma privately through the back door.
It had been worse than doing nothing of consequence.
For he had contributed to something that he knew all the
time was intellectually fraudulent. True, he had done so
in the hope of having a better opportunity to do something
honest. But the opportunity had not come. And now it
might never come!
Like a gaunt statue he stood in the gusty silence on the
ridge and stared across cool-looking hills with unseeing
eyes. He had come to nothing! He had let respectable
weaklings lure him into thinking that if he only waited long
enough, a cowardly procedure would be transmuted into
something strong and courageous.
He would be thirty some of these times. And he had
done nothing at all; in fact, had done something worse
than nothing.
A flash of self-awareness brought him back to his sur-
roundings. He was standing on a ridge two miles west
of the college considering his own shortcomings. Back
in his own hills he had once dawdled and had said that
he would never be caught dawdling again. Yet here he
was and he had been beguiled by much the same kind
of make-believe respectability. It seemed that one could
die of torpidity anywhere. Was there some defect in his
nature that was to keep him dawdling as long as he lived?
All the way back he made a desperate effort to think
well of himself. But a man must do something, must fill his
life with some great richness, if he is to think well of
himself.
THE HILLIKIN
179
He came up to the campus in time for his class. There
went the president to conduct his course in Christian Ethics
-the one course he thought it appropriate for the president
of the college to teach. While he sat as guide and exemplar
before his students, would he raise questions about such
matters as the ethics of means and ends?
Giles Dabney meant to raise some of these questions
with the president in the privacy of the administration
building. He meant to ask him if he ever was able to dis-
tinguish between the honest and the expedient. Then he
meant to tell him to go to hell.
From this forthright youthful resolution he derived such
a buoyant warmth of spirit as he had not known since he
first came to Steel City. It was as if he had been con-
verted to religion.
CHAPTER III
TOW that he was awakened, his unmolested sources
of energy filled him with the wildest impatience.
No
He feared that despite himself the college might
once again exert its soporific influence upon him and leave
him there where he was to the last of his days. Life seemed
to be an unending battle with the forces of drowsiness.
He must see President Wiswall at once.
But the president was not to be seen. The gray little
woman in the outer office said something about high blood
pressure and at least a fortnight of absolute relaxation.
A fortnight! Perhaps longer! Giles climbed the hollow,
resounding stairs to his recitation room wondering how he
could hold his plans in abeyance so long.
When he walked into the room the amateurish designs
of country clubs that he had hung on the walls wherever
there was space suddenly impressed him with great use-
lessness. Was there one of them that amounted to anything
whatsoever? Suppose he had never directed the designing
of one of these artificial countrysides, would anybody have
been the loser in so much as the slightest detail? Suppose
he had never come to Steel City-
He turned to the window to escape a heavy suffocation.
Everywhere students poured from mid-afternoon classes.
They did not seem like individual human beings; they
were molten but cooling lava that simply spread out wher-
ever it was poured and then followed the channels that
offered the least resistance.
What did it mean-the whole thing-that was what he
wanted to know? Here were all these lusty young males
180
THE HILLIKIN
181
submitting sleepily to four years of spoon-feeding, when by
nature they should be busy establishing households, if they
ever meant to do so, and multiplying, and replenishing the
earth. And here were as many-even more-marriageable
young females submitting to the identical process. He
viewed them with sympathy-the girls especially. Looked
at in the lump-viewed from a distance when they were
decked out in bright colors and marching rhythmically in a
spring tree-day procession-they were impressive. Yet if
a man were to pounce down into a bevy of twenty of them
and lay hold of whichever one by the impartial laws of
chance happened to be nearest, would he probably be con-
tent with her?
And this was not the only college. Multiply all these
cowed youngsters by five or six or seven hundred and find
the total college population of the country. They were
going out into the world by tens and hundreds of thou-
sands. Might they not be expected to do something
momentous?
There was a time when most of them had dreamed dreams.
Some of them had advanced to the point where they had
will enough to grapple with embryonic ideas. But sooner
or later they fell into the easy ways induced by institu-
tions. Day by day, week by week, month by month,
year by year, they surrendered a little more of the fresh-
ness of outlook and individual inquisitiveness with which
they were born.
Out of the hundreds there before his eyes, possibly ten
or fifteen would survive the deadening process of learning
how to seem superior, how to carry prestige advantageously.
They would not let education deprive them of their capacity
to dream. They would believe and protest and rebel with
such vigor and such persistence that when they were
through with college they would still be in possession of
some of their power of initiative. They would, then, actu-
182
THE HILLIKIN
ally shift the currents of life ever so slightly by their sturdier
individuality.
But the others! The others would drift back to their
home towns and become respectable parasites on an eco-
nomic organism which they had been led to believe was
more sacred than life itself. They would make a few feeble
efforts to change certain things in minor non-essential de-
tails. But they would find even this too difficult, and
sooner or later would surrender the last of their “impracti-
cal ideals." They would fill their houses with sleek, heavily
upholstered furniture; they would distribute sparsely on
the walls a few lurid ready-framed prints of French cathe-
drals and Egyptian moonlight; they would adorn a living-
room table with a set or two of books secured as premiums
with the Literary Digest; and then they would settle down
to playing bridge and saving money so that some day their
children would be able to enjoy all the glorious educational
advantages that they themselves had so much enjoyed.
The greatest tragedy of it all, Giles Dabney thought,
was that it need not be so. Originally they had possessed
enough potential strength of mind to overturn the world.
As late as when they entered college they still had brief
clear flashes of vision. But in the end they were subdued
by their much lounging before wide fireplaces, by dizzying
house-parties and endless dances, by the lazy intoning of
lectures by professors who were no longer active in earn-
ing the distinction they enjoyed-by the total effect of a
complicated mechanism which places a higher premium upon
surface brightness than upon depth and honesty and origi-
nality.
Giles Dabney was adding to the subject-matter of the
conference he meant to have with the president.
The president returned after a month. Before Giles
Dabney had opportunity to ask for an appointment, the
president sent for him. The letter was formally phrased.
THE HILLIKIN
169
he died, but when men have money to give, you can't be
too fussy about details. Anyhow, there has been no ground
for fear. He is a widower and childless."
Mr. Mott kept the college in suspense for three weeks.
Then he grew better-much better. He was soon able to sit
in the sun parlor and read. Then one morning, after he
had professed to feel unusually well, he wilted down with an
unsteady heart that hesitated, fluttered, and stopped.
President Wiswall convened the faculty. The college was
sending a floral tribute, of course. But with all the circum-
stances considered, he thought it only decent that the entire
faculty attend the funeral.
It was so voted.
Students read on bulletin boards that
for an entire afternoon they were to be denied the sources
of education on account of the lamented death of the Hon-
orable James P. Mott, friend of the college. Thereupon
they engaged in gleeful siren whistlings, rebel yells, happy
shrieks, and skylarkings in general, as with books under
arms they aimlessly made their way from the campus.

The president at once became suave and conciliatory.
"But do you think that is just the thing for an American

He enjoyed his mild nastiness, and stalked from the

He strode the walks of the campus defiantly. He taught
classes with nonchalance, as if all such proceedings were
now mere matters of history. He heard the philanderings
of students and the brightest little jokes of the brightest
little professors as if everything were a puppet show. What
did all this empty chatter amount to when the world was
in conflagration? And down in the heart of him he wanted
to know how he had lived all this time without being more
aware himself of what the conflagration meant.
He told one of his most serious men students what he had
decided to do. He told one of his clear-headed colleagues
who sometimes confided in him. Soon he felt eyes gazing
upon him wherever he went. Most of the eyes were ad-
miring or envious, but of this he did not feel sure. He
was irritated; he wanted to turn and stare everybody out
of countenance.
On the last morning, he decided to be brazen and let
188
THE HILLIKIN
them look at him if they chose. He went to chapel and

If anybody wanted to look at a man who was sitting there

A colonel in smart khaki sat on the platform chatting

to depend upon the young men in the colleges for leader-

They must hold themselves in readiness.
President Wiswall arose again, glowingly. He wished
to make an announcement that affected them all. He had
not secured permission to do so, but since he had been
in possession of the facts for several days, he could see no
reason why the students, in these stirring times, should not
enjoy the honor that had come to them, just as he had.
They would be proud, he felt sure, to know that a brilliant
member of the college faculty was to be Steel City's first
contribution to the great crusade in which every loyal
American was now vitally interested. The man who had
brought this great distinction to the college was Professor
Dabney of the Department of Landscape Architecture.
There was wild, shrieking, hysterical applause. The

As his wrath descended to trivialities, Giles Dabney began

The president paused in his marchings and mopped his

He could not finish the sentence. He mopped his neck
again, dropped into his high-backed swivel chair, and panted
with great heaves. He leaned forward and let his face fall
limply into his hands. Giles wondered if he were to be
a lone witness to an apoplectic death.
After a moment President Wiswall sat up. "You'll have
to carry that French another year-there's simply nothing
else to be done. I wish we might continue the landscape
architecture. It gives a better impression than almost any-
thing we have added in ten years. But with the French
armies holding off the Germans as they are, and France's
prestige mounting every day, we've simply got to continue
all courses in French."
Giles Dabney stumbled from the office in a daze. What
was this the president had said about giving up landscape
architecture and going on with French?
How far he walked before he began to think, he did not
know. But he had not gone far before he began to see.
His inner life had followed a sharp declivity ever since his
arrival at Steel City College. Was this the logical culmina-
tion of his great dream?
He had come to Steel City with much of the poet in him.
Already he was far enough away from where he had started
to see that. Some of his seasoned colleagues had smiled at

THE HILLIKIN

CHAPTER IV
WO years later when Giles Dabney next saw Steel
City, he walked up the street and tried to recapture
that one great moment when he had felt accepted
of his fellows. But the incident seemed as remote as every-
thing else that had occurred prior to two years ago. Was
it true that he had once had a childhood, and a youth, and
a novitiate in the profession of teaching?
College Hill seemed more of a hodgepodge than ever.
But life moved on among the maples. Professors hurried
to classrooms and laboratories with an assurance of their
own value to mankind that Giles Dabney for the moment
envied. Sorority girls raced belatedly to class as usual,
dabbing powder on their noses as they clicked along on
high heels. Two new buildings were in process of con-
struction. A steam shovel sputtered and hissed and groaned
as it gouged out a place on the hillside for a third.
He stopped to watch the shovel. Students brushed past
him on the cement walk and glanced at his uniform and
the inch or two of multicolored ribbon on his chest as they
would have glanced at any other last year's clothes or anti-
quated detail of millinery. The war was over.
Did he care to look in on anybody and revive ancient
history? He smiled ironically, as he thought that perhaps
he might drop in and present his compliments to President
Wiswall. Why not? Two years had left him for the
moment in the state of mind when the difference between
one thing and another seems very slight. Yes, why not
go in and see what tricks the old boy was up to at the
moment?
191
178
THE HILLIKIN
grave-faced men and women sought to determine whether
young ladies should be sent home for climbing in at dormi-

ball player who had come up to commencement time two
and a half credits short of the prescribed number should be
permitted to sit on the platform or be required to receive
his diploma privately through the back door.
It had been worse than doing nothing of consequence.
For he had contributed to something that he knew all the
time was intellectually fraudulent. True, he had done so
in the hope of having a better opportunity to do something
honest. But the opportunity had not come. And now it
might never come!
Like a gaunt statue he stood in the gusty silence on the
ridge and stared across cool-looking hills with unseeing
eyes. He had come to nothing! He had let respectable
weaklings lure him into thinking that if he only waited long
enough, a cowardly procedure would be transmuted into
something strong and courageous.
He would be thirty some of these times. And he had
done nothing at all; in fact, had done something worse
than nothing.
A flash of self-awareness brought him back to his sur-
roundings. He was standing on a ridge two miles west
of the college considering his own shortcomings. Back
in his own hills he had once dawdled and had said that
he would never be caught dawdling again. Yet here he
was and he had been beguiled by much the same kind
of make-believe respectability. It seemed that one could
die of torpidity anywhere. Was there some defect in his
nature that was to keep him dawdling as long as he lived?
All the way back he made a desperate effort to think
well of himself. But a man must do something, must fill his
life with some great richness, if he is to think well of
himself.
THE HILLIKIN
179
He came up to the campus in time for his class. There
went the president to conduct his course in Christian Ethics
-the one course he thought it appropriate for the president
of the college to teach. While he sat as guide and exemplar
before his students, would he raise questions about such
matters as the ethics of means and ends?
Giles Dabney meant to raise some of these questions
with the president in the privacy of the administration
building. He meant to ask him if he ever was able to dis-
tinguish between the honest and the expedient. Then he
meant to tell him to go to hell.
From this forthright youthful resolution he derived such
a buoyant warmth of spirit as he had not known since he
first came to Steel City. It was as if he had been con-
verted to religion.

A fortnight! Perhaps longer! Giles climbed the hollow,

When he walked into the room the amateurish designs
of country clubs that he had hung on the walls wherever

whatsoever? Suppose he had never directed the designing
of one of these artificial countrysides, would anybody have
been the loser in so much as the slightest detail? Suppose

He turned to the window to escape a heavy suffocation.
Everywhere students poured from mid-afternoon classes.
They did not seem like individual human beings; they

ever it was poured and then followed the channels that

180
THE HILLIKIN

"As much as I have a chance at."
Giles Dabney arose. "But I must be going."
Outside, with time on his hands, he thought he had better
walk over to the pergola and see what the city had done
in two years.
It had extended itself, without question. Millions of men
had to be killed in Europe, and Steel City had equipped
itself to produce whatever engines of slaughter might be
required. Mills had sprung up as hastily and as insub-
stantially as mushrooms. They had supplanted smaller,
peace-time factories; they had displaced abandoned, tumble-
down office buildings, dirty grocery stores, and dingy saloons
in the edges of downtown; they had lifted their belching
smokestacks where before for millenniums only trees and
green grasses had covered the hillsides. Yes, Providence
had been very good to Steel City in wartime. Objects that
were partially visible two years ago were now completely
obscured by the smoke. Steel City had become a defiant
citadel of some monstrous civilization.
While he stood there the weariness of many months began
to leave him. He felt his pulses stirring with something of
their forgotten youthful power. Here in this hateful ex-
pression of the perverted human spirit was something that
invited battle more insistently than all the heroic men who
were ever marshaled on the other side of No Man's Land.
The monster fascinated him. It reached out of the valley
like some blundering octopus and sucked in the fields, the
clear-running streamlets, the quiet, detached homes of men,
the brightness of the skies, and soon made them all ugly
and misshapen and hideous like the rest of itself. Or no,
it was not an octopus; it was a conflagration, licking up
everything that had life in it, and leaving only wreckage.
For the more fortunate minority of its inhabitants, it was
subtly debasing; for the great majority, it was unlivable.
He stood long and contemplated all that he could see,
182
THE HILLIKIN
ally shift the currents of life ever so slightly by their sturdier

But the others! The others would drift back to their
home towns and become respectable parasites on an eco-
nomic organism which they had been led to believe was
more sacred than life itself. They would make a few feeble
efforts to change certain things in minor non-essential de-
tails. But they would find even this too difficult, and
sooner or later would surrender the last of their "impracti-

upholstered furniture; they would distribute sparsely on
the walls a few lurid ready-framed prints of French cathe-
drals and Egyptian moonlight; they would adorn a living-

with the Literary Digest; and then they would settle down
to playing bridge and saving money so that some day their
children would be able to enjoy all the glorious educational
advantages that they themselves had so much enjoyed.
The greatest tragedy of it all, Giles Dabney thought,
was that it need not be so. Originally they had possessed
enough potential strength of mind to overturn the world.
As late as when they entered college they still had brief
clear flashes of vision. But in the end they were subdued
by their much lounging before wide fireplaces, by dizzying
house-parties and endless dances, by the lazy intoning of
lectures by professors who were no longer active in earn-
ing the distinction they enjoyed-by the total effect of a
complicated mechanism which places a higher premium upon
surface brightness than upon depth and honesty and origi-
nality.
Giles Dabney was adding to the subject-matter of the

and their fundamental great sincerity. They were doing
something that they honestly wanted to do.
He tried to walk unperturbed. But the aisle seemed miles
long. And the applause, now that he was in the thick of
it, was more overwhelming than he had imagined anything
could be. Wildly, earnestly, persistently it went on and on
until nobody could mistake its import.
Here at last was somebody approving what he himself
had decided to do. It was not so much what he was doing—
just offering himself as a target for young Germans of his
own age who were already practiced in the use of deadly
weapons-but it was enough, even if he never did anything
else. His hungry, waiting spirit felt unalone. These stu-
dents, by the nature of their occupation skilled in all the
intricacies of laziness, seemed to recognize reality when
they saw it! With their applause bursting his ears, he felt

"Oh, I see! Fine!
Fine! And you never got a scratch?"
"No, nothing worth mentioning.'
""
"I wanted to get over myself," the president remarked in
an effort to be nonchalant, as if going over were a kind of
Cook's tour; "but I couldn't quite make it. I couldn't leave
my job here-source of supply, you know."
"I see you are doing some building."
"Yes, we are quite a little, in fact. Providence has been
very good to us all through the war. It almost makes a man
believe in direct reward for service rendered. Twenty-nine
gold stars on our Service Flag in the chapel!"
His eyes glistened for the briefest moment. Then he was
back on the subject of building.
"Not the best time in the world to build, with materials
and especially labor so high. But it's a mighty good time
to get funds. Steel City has come through the war in fine
shape. We have thirty-five per cent more mills than we
had two years ago. And of course they have made money.
So I tried to get some of the surplus directed toward College
Hill instead of having it all turned over to the United States
Treasury in the form of excess profits tax."
He smiled in content.
"And at last I found a man to endow that professorship
in landscape architecture."
He flinched at his own words, and sat back in his swivel
chair and fingered the edge of his desk.
"I-I was a little embarrassed after that speech of mine
in chapel that day you left-that promise I made in the
presence of everybody. You see, this man who gave the
194
THE HILLIKIN
money had in mind also somebody who was to get the good
of it-for a time, at least. So I had to make the appoint-
ment right away. I don't know as inheriting professors is
the best way of getting them, but sometimes it is not left
to us to decide. Anyhow, I could not know—that is, for
a certainty-that you would be back, though naturally we
hoped so. And we received enough money so that I thought
I could keep my promise to you even if you should turn up.
Of course I can't give you the professorship. But I'm
always as good as my word, and if you feel that I ought
to take you back, I can pay you the same salary next year
that you received when you left. Beyond next year I
couldn't make any promises."
Two years before, Giles Dabney could not have heard
the president's bland, scurvy proposal without rage. But
with the advantage of the past two years, he only remarked,
“Oh, no; I have never had the slightest thought of coming
back."
The president was greatly relieved. "I doubted whether
you meant to return. You never seemed to be quite com-
fortable in Steel City. Perhaps you'll land in a town more
in accordance with your-principles."
"But I have no thought of leaving the city.”
"You haven't?"
"No, I mean to stay here and practice my profession.”
The president could not prevent a shadow from flitting
across his face. But quickly he caught himself up and
went on blithely: "I suppose it will be true that with plenty
of extra money in town there will be more people ready to
have somebody look after their grounds and all that sort
of thing."
"But I don't have in mind doing much of that.”
Again the president seemed troubled. "You mean that
you are going to try out some of those wild ideas-pardon
me of making the city over?"
THE HILLIKIN
195
"As much as I have a chance at."
Giles Dabney arose. "But I must be going."
Outside, with time on his hands, he thought he had better
walk over to the pergola and see what the city had done
in two years.
It had extended itself, without question. Millions of men
had to be killed in Europe, and Steel City had equipped
itself to produce whatever engines of slaughter might be
required. Mills had sprung up as hastily and as insub-
stantially as mushrooms. They had supplanted smaller,
peace-time factories; they had displaced abandoned, tumble-
down office buildings, dirty grocery stores, and dingy saloons
in the edges of downtown; they had lifted their belching
smokestacks where before for millenniums only trees and
green grasses had covered the hillsides. Yes, Providence
had been very good to Steel City in wartime. Objects that
were partially visible two years ago were now completely
obscured by the smoke. Steel City had become a defiant
citadel of some monstrous civilization.
While he stood there the weariness of many months began
to leave him. He felt his pulses stirring with something of
their forgotten youthful power. Here in this hateful ex-
pression of the perverted human spirit was something that
invited battle more insistently than all the heroic men who
were ever marshaled on the other side of No Man's Land.
The monster fascinated him. It reached out of the valley
like some blundering octopus and sucked in the fields, the
clear-running streamlets, the quiet, detached homes of men,
the brightness of the skies, and soon made them all ugly
and misshapen and hideous like the rest of itself. Or no,
it was not an octopus; it was a conflagration, licking up
everything that had life in it, and leaving only wreckage.
For the more fortunate minority of its inhabitants, it was
subtly debasing; for the great majority, it was unlivable.
He stood long and contemplated all that he could see,
196
THE HILLIKIN
and all that he could surmise where the smoke was im-
penetrable. When at last he moved to go, he felt twitches
of the desperate energy he used to enjoy back in his own
hills when the hours that he had been living seemed futile,
and the hours that he meant to live henceforth were bulging
with fruitfulness.
He stopped at a clothing store to look at a suit of clothes
guaranteed to be extra long. The price was outrageous—
"You know they've been having a hell of a war over in
Europe" but he paid it and carried the suit to his room
and put it on before he went out to luncheon. He thought
he could pack his uniform away for a good long time.
In the afternoon he went up to reëngage his room from
the Swedish baker's wife. But she begged to tell him that
he could do nothing of the sort. She was not keeping roomers
any longer. Her husband had made lots of money during
the war-killing required bread, too-and she was just
going to sit and rock till she got good and rested. She knew
a Mrs. Erensberger, though, down in the valley who was
looking for roomers.
"But I don't want to be down in the valley. I must be
up on a hill."
""
"Then try Mrs. Dibblee-three blocks over here, south.
She needs the money. Here, let me look in the telephone
book. Yes, 24; 24 Hillside Avenue."
Giles explained to Mrs. Dibblee that he was just back
from the war. She seemed uninterested. He explained
further that he was a landscape architect and was about to
begin the practice of his profession in what he called a
comprehensive way. She was scarcely more interested. But
by the time he had explained yet further that he was looking
for a large specially quiet room up above things where he
could be let alone with his drafting boards and tables, she
was ready to hear anything else he might have to say.
She led him to a spacious room at the rear of the up-
THE HILLIKIN
197
stairs and said, "Here, isn't this quiet and above things?
And isn't it roomy enough for anybody?"
Giles Dabney walked to the window and looked down at
hundreds of giant corbie steps which the house roofs made
on the steep hillside, and knew at once that here was where
he meant to design the new Steel City.
