CHAPTER V
HEN Giles Dabney began to discover how little
he knew about a city, his one comfort was in the
fact that nobody else knew more. And most
people knew much less. They followed their own little
tunnels in the vast ant-hill without troubling over regions
which their particular tunnels did not penetrate. They
were unable to direct him to streets within a half-dozen
blocks of where they had lived for a decade. They could
not tell him the names of people who lived next door-or
had lived there yesterday if not to-day. The only city
official they were concerned with was their alderman, who
had favors at his disposal, or the policeman on their beat,
who might be a friend in need or a watchful enemy, accord-
ing to circumstances. The Hunky down in the mills knew
as much about the region where his employer lived as if
it were on some other planet; and his employer's family
knew less than that about where the Hunky lived. Giles
had to make so many inquiries that workingmen's wives
began to suspect he was a government representative in
pursuit of Communists, and watchmen in the vicinity of
mills wondered if he were not some incendiary person out
to plant bombs. He never met anybody who knew him;
he never met anybody who knew anybody.
He meant to experience a city. He meant to know what
it was like for everyone who lived in it.
And almost before he knew it, he was learning how a
city feels in summer. He had been in many a one when
the weather was hot, but always he had been in and out
again, and could throw the terror of it off, as one escapes
198
THE HILLIKIN
199
a nightmare by desperate effort. But now he was learning
how it feels to spend the entire summer-perchance every
entire summer for a lifetime-in an inferno, without so much
as a week-end of relief.
When June days suddenly grew blistering hot and June
nights were little cooler, the more fortunate in the city said,
"This town is as hot as hell. To-morrow we go to the
country and stay there till things have cooled off next
September." But the others, who had to keep the mills
running and the stores open, could only say, "This town is
as hot as hell," and then stay on and endure the agony
as if it had been imposed upon them by an Almighty God
who enjoyed making heat.
What if the farmers, who could sleep out of doors after
a sweaty day of work if they chose, were to look in on
Steel City when the night air burned the throat? What if
the more fortunate of the city itself who had ways of escape
were to venture back some fiery night in July or August
when the soft asphalt in the street refused to cool, and
the rains refused to come, and the winds refused to blow
so much as a tantalizing breeze through thin-roofed com-
pany houses and sizzling brick tenements? Sometimes
there was relative coolness on a fourth-story fire escape.
The specially blessed of the tenants could roost there
throughout the evening and on into the night. But most
of them had to sleep in a bedroom. Perchance it had only
one window, and that opened out just above an iron roof
surrounded on three sides by the walls of a building that
had baked in the sun for so many days that it was like a
hot loaf of bread through and through. And the whole
building and hundreds of others were filled with human
beings who tried to get a little sleep: sweaty working-
men, and disheartened wives, and petulant children, and
feverish babes that wailed against the hellish heat into
which they had been born-all crowded promiscuously to-
200
THE HILLIKIN
gether with their heads pressed close to windows and doors
in the futile hope of a breath that was not quite so hot.
They must rest a little before the red sun came up the
next morning to add another roasting day to the ones they
had already suffered.
Giles Dabney remembered August in the country. August
meant warm afternoons under friendly locust trees in hill-
side pastures, and soft bulging white clouds in the blue sky.
But August in Steel City was a hammering, clattering, roar-
ing, dirty hell. Grandfathers and grandmothers who lived
with their sons or daughters in the dingy houses close against
the base of the hills looked helplessly about, said something
about being dizzy, crumpled over, and helped the newspapers
by giving them easy copy for their columns on "Deaths
from the Heat."
"I don't see how the rest of us can hold out," housewives
said across backdoor porches, "but maybe we can endure
till after Labor Day. It always seems to cool off a little
pretty soon after Labor Day is over."
So everybody tried to look forward to Labor Day-and
beyond to October. Even in Steel City, October was worth
waiting for. Men came home in the evening from the mills
where they worked, and cleaned up and lounged comfortably
in front of where they lived, and smoked huge pipes of
pungently odorous tobacco and forgot the summer. Or they
sat through pleasant Saturday and Sunday afternoons on
the smooth, rounded cement curb and watched the children
of the neighborhood play and fight in the streets and dodge
automobiles.
Or they said, "Come on," and took their families to the
nearest little park and saw the last game of baseball or
the first game of football, and then bought peanuts to feed
the polar bear. He, too, was glad that summer was over.
Not that he was so badly off in summer as the human
habitants of Steel City; for the municipal government appro-
THE HILLIKIN
201
priated money for the air conditioning of his cage with
supplies of ice. Still, it was pleasant for him, as for every-
one else who had sweltered, to feel October's breath.
Giles Dabney had reasons of his own for welcoming
October. October told him to go ahead. October filled his
mind with images of his better houses for workers, his wider
streets, his vast civic center, his beautified river and lake,
his open green spaces, his isolated mill districts, and his
smokeless skies. The hills and valleys in the region of
Steel City might be as bright and inviting as those he had
seen in Vermont if only the people were to take thought.
Mrs. Dibblee saw little of him. She thought of him only
as a tall, quiet lodger who seemed to have few friends.
Other women in the neighborhood knew him as "that man
at Mrs. Dibblee's." Children who rushed like the wind
down the steep sidewalks of Hillside Avenue on roller skates
and then when he chanced along begged him to pull them
back up,
knew him as a kind of beneficent locomotive. The
others who filled the streets had no occasion to know him
at all. He was busy with a dream-and they were not.
He spent hours at the window silently contemplating all
that spread out below him. He wore out his shoes tramping
through obscure parts of the city that he wished to know
better. He tramped beyond the city limits-for miles in
every direction to see how the entire region might be ren-
dered logical. If some part of his conception refused to
develop, he "put it to soak" and then tramped again, or
went through photographs and memories of all the cities he
had ever visited, or made journeys to neighboring cities
to see what he could learn there, and then waited, or paced
the floor restlessly, or tried to gaze off with new under-
standing across the city until some happy cross-fertilization
of ideas resulted and he found himself in possession of what
he had struggled for.
One day winter arrived. Winter said, "You had better
202
THE HILLIKIN
take me into account while you are about it. I am quite
as imperious in my requirements as summer.”
That was hard to believe on bright clear winter days.
But after the pale, impotent sun had peered through the
smoke from above the lake and the southern hills for a
short day and then had vanished entirely, and night came
on, and the wind began to sweep in icy gusts round the
granite veneer corners of emptied skyscrapers, and the hard,
stinging snow pelted the face till it burned, and the fortu-
nate ones had gone for the night to warm houses on hill-
tops where all appearance of poverty was prohibited by law,
and the last shop was closed so that women without money
or fuel could no longer keep warm by pretending to be
purchasers, and men without overcoats had to clear out of
nice warm railroad stations into the blast, and midnight
came and went, and nobody on the street knew anybody else,
and nobody cared-then one could see just how much comeli-
ness was left in the city after winter arrived.
While winter raged through long night hours, Giles
prowled into every region where he thought he might gain
knowledge that would be valuable to a city planner. Some-
times he found expert information in what the genteel would
regard as unimportant sources. Tottering but determined
old women with shawls drawn tightly over their heads slipped
cautiously from houses where the weatherboarding rattled
in the wind, and hurried away in the late night to the rail-
road tracks to see if they could not steal a few lumps of
coal. Once Giles stood for a half-hour close against a
telegraph pole and watched an old woman who knew he
was there and feared he might be a policeman. He saw
that she was going to freeze if he stayed much longer, and
walked in the other direction without looking back. Prosti-
tutes who had shivered along the streets until they were half-
frozen and had found no customers besought him, for God's
sake, only to take them where it was warm. They would
THE HILLIKIN
203
forego money, and food, and even drink, if only they could
escape the benumbing cold. What was the pleasure of having
a body if one's blood was full of icicles? "In Christ's name,
ought a woman to freeze to death?"
A man asked him for six cents. "I know where I can get
enough stale rolls to last me for two days for six cents. The
baker is in there now getting the fresh morning bread
ready."
When Giles was on his way home he saw the same man
dodge into the protected side entrance of a church. He
saw Giles, too, and feared that he was to be chased out.
But when he saw that it was the man who had given him
the six cents he said, "I was thinking, buddy, that I could
spend the rest of the night back in that corner. It's kind o'
protected a little from the snow. And I've got four or five
good warm newspapers here."
Toward noon the next day Giles Dabney was going to
Happy Hollow in the willows along the river bottom at
the edge of the city to see how the people down there battled
with winter. He passed the church and thought of his man.
Had he been able to stay there all night? He peeped in.
Aye, he was still there, sitting back in his corner, upright
enough, with the newspapers still wrapped round all of him
except his white face.
Giles could not put him out of mind, even after the
policeman he notified had remarked perfunctorily, "We've
brought in five or six such this morning.”
Why hadn't he given the man money and told him to find
a warm room? Or why hadn't he taken him somewhere?
All the way to Happy Hollow he was troubled.
But as soon as he reached the Hollow, he was able to
turn from cold in the specific to cold in general. Hundreds
of men and women without the rent required in uptown areas
had gathered their children together and had come here
where they were providing a laboratory demonstration of
204
THE HILLIKIN
how goods-boxes and tarred paper and the stolen sides of
abandoned box-cars could cover the very slight margin be-
tween freezing to death and keeping alive.
The hideousness of life here warmed him with rage. Who
could say that such fringes of a city were necessary. Wait
till he proposed his new city! It would be a livable city-
for everybody! That thought kept him from suffering too
much with those all about him who suffered inescapably.
The day was never long enough for his purposes. Always
he had to make use of the night. When he was not out on
some nocturnal journey of exploration, he built a fire in
the open-faced stove which Mrs. Dibblee had placed in his
room to supplement a remote furnace, and looked into the
firelight and saw his city. Or he sat high on his round stool
before the drafting table and worked at maps, and sketches,
and drawings of detail. He was warm through and
through with the feeling that he was making progress.
Sometimes an idea took hold of him as if it were a fever.
He lived in warm agony that kept him from sleeping when
he went to bed; that kept him from eating when he went
to the restaurant; that kept him from seeing anybody when
he walked the streets. He sometimes attempted to throw
it off-in the interest of his own life. But always there
it was again in the forefront of consciousness before he
could give himself up to anything else.
"Just a little more of your time, please!"
Yes, while he was about it, he had better work on. He
had almost everything in clear view now-the whole of the
new city; all, in fact, except a few important parts which
he could not quite visualize. He had better try to press
back the obscurity a little farther-make the circle of clear
vision a little wider.
Then some day the troublesome part grew clearer. He
must get it down while it was easy to see. And in the
warmth of the little stove, he sat for hours-how many he
THE HILLIKIN
205
scarcely knew-and worked easily, swiftly, accurately.
After half a night of that kind, he had to go out and
walk the streets of the dormant city until he had cooled
off enough for sleep.
Long before daylight he would awake with a start and try
to recall whether he had put in an important detail that he
had in mind. He would get up, turn on the light, look at
the drawings, find the detail already there, linger a little to
glance over things in general, smile a cautious smile of
content, and go back to bed to sleep until the dim, smoky
sun was shining in at his southeast window.
By spring he was wondering if he were not already in
complete possession of his major conception. Here was
how he meant to put the railroads through tunnels under
the ridge west of the river and leave the river bank and
the lake front free for parkways. Here was how he meant
to isolate all mills built in the future-and as many that
were already built as could be induced by prospect of great
economies to abandon in-town sites. Here was how he
meant to house the workers away from the mills and
enough away from one another to guarantee fresh air, clean
sunshine, clean rain, and clean snow. And here was how
he would reclaim the section right next door to downtown
that had been turned over in superior fashion to the negroes,
and that had now been half abandoned by them. He meant
to convert the region into a magnificent civic center with
municipal office buildings, art galleries, exhibition halls, read-
ing rooms, a symphony hall, and a municipal theater. If
Montpellier, down in the depths of provincial France, could
have municipal opera and municipal symphonies and mu-
nicipal plays, why could not great, overgrown Steel City,
with ten times as many people and mills? If people gave
their blood daily for the city, should they not receive from
the city in return some of the compensations of civilized
life?
206
THE HILLIKIN
Sometimes he laughed at the city-at the city as it was.
For it was more absurd than any of the other absurdities
with which men had cluttered up their world. It was an
instance of the worst the human race could do when going
it blind. It was absurd fundamentally. That was why it
was fundamentally tragic. It was only a grim battleground
where men who pretended to have the most considerate re-
gard for one another engaged in a desperate catch-as-catch-
can struggle to heave one another over a precipice into
oblivion. Who could look at it and say that it was not
the handiwork of idiots?
Would not the citizens be startled, would not they re-
joice, when they saw what a city-their own city-might
become when somebody took thought?
As months added themselves to months, and his dream
got into his blood, the whole of his life became only a vast
dream. He forgot the hardness of his childhood and youth;
he forgot the dishonest superficialities of President Wiswall
and his college; he forgot the stinking realism of war. These
became unrealities to him, and his dream grew into the only
substantial reality he knew.
Sometimes he caught little unintentioned shreds of poetry
in the city-gray skyscrapers, for instance, rising above a
level blanket of smoke that obscured the streets. Sometimes
when the evening was dripping with rain and snow and mist,
he walked along the waterfront and saw what the painter
sees-Monets and Whistlers and Raffaellis in arrangements
and nocturnes unending. Sometimes he walked through the
din of downtown, and heard nothing of the din, but only
the great inclusive, symphonic something that the musician
hears. The city might well burst into song if only it had a
fair chance.
Yet always these moments were wrested from him by the
city's hardness, its indifference, its inhumanity. Then he
could see only black smokestacks, and great complex masses
THE HILLIKIN
207
of pipes rising above furnaces like the sacred entrails of
some giant that pigmy human beings had lifted up to wor-
ship, and the haunted faces of men and women as they
hurried to a task in which they could not possibly find
interest, or as they hurried away from it in fear that even
that insufficient justification for living might be snatched
from them.
By the end of another winter and perchance another sum-
mer, he would have all the major details of his dream per-
fected, and could lay it before those who were to profit by
it, and let them discover for themselves what their city was
now like, and what it might become.

CHAPTER VI
NE morning in the autumn when all sorts of irregu-
lar rectangles and triangles of frosty lawn lingered
late on the north side of houses, Giles Dabney
walked confidently down into the heart of Steel City. Life,
he still persisted in believing in his youthful, provincial
way, could be made to possess meaning. Not even the
epidemic fatalism of the army had quite jolted that be-
lief out of him. Had he not begun with something as chaotic
as Steel City and ended with an orderly idea? Now he
must find means of bringing his idea to general attention.
He made his way along in the milling crowd down in the
shopping district and wondered who in all that hurrying,
pushing throng would be the first to champion his mag-
nificent conception. From the vantage point of a full six
feet he looked at all the faces and asked himself if any
of them ever enjoyed a great enthusiasm of any kind. He
supposed so, yes; it was reasonable to suppose so, per-
haps. But the evidence which the faces unwittingly re-
vealed was all on the negative. These would have to be
the supporting majority after others had given his plan
initial approbation.
"Pardon me,' ""
a slender, nervous man of thirty or so
with a half-dozen books under his arm inquired, "but aren't
you Dabney?"
"Yes, I'm Dabney, but—”
"You see, I have the advantage.
and easily identified. I'm Gunning.
River one evening-hahahaha!
You are tall and virile
Met you in the Charles
Life consists of a certain
number of chance meetings, they say, don't they? You

THE HILLIKIN

happened along at just the right time to fish me out of the

Can't you go up to our Rive Gauche Club right here round
the corner where we can sit down for ten minutes and
piece things together a little—the last eight or ten years?
You are no busier than I am-you couldn't be."
While they pushed round the corner through the crowd,
Giles glanced down at Gunning. "The years rest lightly,"
he said, speaking like a graduate at a reunion.
"And yours, too," Gunning replied. "You'll always be
a—what shall I say, a husky?—a saver of lives, as you
were when we first met. But tell me, what are you doing
in this town? And how long have you been here?"
By the time they were up to the tenth floor and were
established in some deep chairs with yellow and brown
upholstery in an arty room under a beclouded skylight,
Giles had answered the last question and Gunning was pro-
pounding the first again.
"Oh," Giles answered, "since the war I've been fiddling
around with some ideas of my own. Nothing much."
Gunning accepted the explanation.
"Overseas?"
"Yes. Were you?"
"Yes, eight months."
"And that old pal of yours?"
"Housleton? Hahahaha! Housleton was a major before
the rest of us were anywhere. He was still a major when
his outfit was ready to sail. Then he was nothing. I never
knew just what happened. He contended that it was his too
great advance familiarity with all the well-known French
vintages."
They were silent. Then they smiled frankly at each
other, as if they both knew they were far away from where
they once were.

THE HILLIKIN
"By Jove," Gunning exclaimed with overtones of nice-

how many years did we say, 'eight or ten'? And you've

"Yes."
Giles Dabney struggled for some kind of fruitful lead.
"But you haven't told me much about yourself."
"Oh-was batting around in New York till the war

"To be sure-of course not. Why, I don't laugh-I
make reputations."
Giles looked puzzled.
"Hahahaha! I'm a critic."

"Both. In fact, universal! I'm the Robert Ingraham

"You mean he is here in Steel City, too?"

"Not many-maybe three or four. I've been too busy

"More comprehensive? Why, that sounds exciting. Tell

“Why”—and he talked with the modest frankness of one

out "I'm doing a little city planning. Have just spent

"Marvelous! Magnificent! Why-why I can't half ex-

started you'd go somewhere somewhere in particular. They
emphasized the 'ever got started' pretty heavily. Hahahaha!
But now-well!"
Giles Dabney colored as if he had only yesterday stepped
forth from the wilds of Wiggam's Glory, Ohio.
"Why, it's marvelous-simply marvelous! I've been tell-

New York. But can it be remade? Won't they have to

"That would be rather expensive."
“And your plan-is it inexpensive?”
"Relatively, yes. That is one of its advantages."
"Why, honestly, this is the most exciting thing I've come
upon in ten years. What do they say about it?”
"Nothing-yet. They know nothing about it."

THE HILLIKIN
Gunning pounced upon a happy idea. "Say, you must

Giles Dabney spent the afternoon in trying to decide

Readers know

Quite before seven on the day appointed, Giles was at
the Club. He waited till five minutes past seven. Then
he remembered that Gunning was always late when he
was in college. He waited a full half-hour. Why had he
entrusted his precious plans to anybody's keeping? He
paced impatiently about for another half-hour. Then Gun-

"Oh! Oh!"
He waited to catch his breath.
"It's a knockout! I never wrote anything half so good.
You must go over to my office after we eat, and check up

218
THE HILLIKIN
your plans. But I was going so hot-had been for three

After dinner they went to the quiet of a little office
piled high with books, and decorated with water-colors
and etchings. Giles Dabney sat with a pleasantly burning
face directed toward a pile of books in the corner while
Gunning read what he had written.
The article was somewhat effusive, but it stated the
facts. "And don't you like the title, too?" Gunning asked.
"I am sure it will take hold. It is simple and direct, and
not too revealing: 'A Man with an Idea.' They don't come
along on the street every day, you see. Oh, yes-and the
subtitle applying the idea to Steel City."
Gunning was pleased.
"Everybody who reads the Herald will see it, and Cynthia,
who does features for the Post occasionally will promptly
steal it-rewriting it a little, of course and old Peach-
blossoms is sure to go out of his way to hand you a sprig
or two of sweet spring loveliness, and people will say, 'What
we can't understand is, why somebody hasn't thought of
this before,' and—well, there you are!"
At midnight they walked from the office together like
tried friends.

CHAPTER VII
ILES DABNEY looked beyond Sunday with solid
hope. His plan was sound-he knew that. On
every doubtful point he had constantly tested him-
self with the most critical questioning. And now-despite
a mild contempt that he could not help cherishing for
anyone who was as much of a dilettante as Gunning-he
felt that his labors were to be brought to the attention of
Steel City so publicly that everybody, including those in
authority, would give heed. Was he not entitled to a bit
of confidence?
On Sunday morning he got up with unusual deliberate-
ness. Despite everything that was impending, he felt a
trifle lonely without oddments of drawings and blue-prints
scattered everywhere in his room. He was discovering
somewhat regretfully that his child was grown and was
about to share its life with many another in addition to its
father.
He went to the nearest restaurant for breakfast. Since
he already knew what was in Gunning's article, he pre-
ferred to order some buckwheat cakes and some coffee before
he turned the pages of the Herald to the magazine supple-
ment.
The waitress came with unexampled dispatch and shoved
his order under the newspaper that he held full width like
an inverted house roof in front of his face. She made out
the bill, slipped it under the edge of his plate of cakes,
and hurried away without imagining that he was a person
of enough news value to occupy space of his own in a Sunday
paper.
219
220
THE HILLIKIN
He did not wish to admit to himself that he was hurry-
ing over the pages. But soon he came to the supplement.
There was first a long article, with illustrations, on
how a baby rhinoceros is fed; a full page advertisement of
the kind of soap a distinguished actress had expressed
confidence in; another article on how a New York banker
had brought soil all the way from northern Africa for the
life-sized palm trees in his penthouse apartment; another
on the canning of rattlesnake meat as a coming minor in-
dustry; and still another on how Paris milliners are able
to determine in advance what the women of America will
prefer to wear in a given season.
But he found nothing about a proposed plan for a new
Steel City. Well, it clearly did not belong in the magazine
supplement anyhow. He had better go more carefully
through the rest of the paper.
He did. And he found nothing.
His hands and arms trembled strangely. His foot trem-
bled on the slender steel rest at the base of the stool. The
newspaper was still before his eyes, but he saw nothing.
For how long? Until the waitress asked: "Do you want
me to fetch you some fresh cakes and some hot coffee? I
guess you forgot where you was, didn't you?"
He folded the paper up. "Oh, well," he said to himself,
"they probably had to push the article forward a week.
So why worry? I'm just as well off as I was before-
maybe better-and I was all right then. I still have my
plans, and they are good."
The next day he decided to drop in and see Gunning.
But he met him at a street crossing.
"Hello, there!" Gunning called blithely as if nothing
could possibly be wrong in the world. Then: "I'm awfully
sorry about the story-but you know it is all in the day's
work."
"What happened? I was just coming up to find out."
THE HILLIKIN
221
"The Sunday editor 'killed' it."
They walked along together.
"What reasons did he give?"
"Hahahaha! You are a city planner, all right, but clearly
you are not a newspaper man. Editors don't give reasons
-unless they want to."
Giles Dabney's hurt was so great that he could not thus
dismiss the matter lightly. He felt something that was half
disgust, half anger welling up within him.
"Mightn't he tell me if I were to ask him?"
"Go up and try-if you like. It will do no special harm.
He probably is up there now."
Giles Dabney went up in a clattering old elevator and
walked in at a door that had "Sunday Editor" tacked across

"Are you Mr. Kedsel?" he asked a stout man of forty-
five who sat coatless over a littered desk.
"Yes, sir. Right here. What can I do for you?"
Giles explained.
"Oh-ho! And so you are the man with the idea, are you?"
He was the least bit ironic. "Well, there's nothing wrong
with you except that you're in the wrong town. Haven't
you ever seen those fiery letters in the smoky sky over the
city at night, 'It is forbidden to an idea to enter'?"
He saw Giles Dabney wince, and he had not meant to
hurt him.
"Sit down a minute.”
He picked up a cigar that was lying with the burning
end over the edge of his desk, and puffed away for a time.
"The truth is, I liked Gunning's story. I don't care a
hell of a lot for him personally, but I liked what he had
to say about you. There's something in your proposition;
you've got an idea by the tail, all right. But see where
I'd be if I'd run three columns-or one, even on how
222
THE HILLIKIN
Steel City ought to be rebuilt from bottom to top—that is,

"You get me, don't you? The city engineer and every
damned officeholder from the chief of police down to the
mayor would be on the publisher's back in half an hour.
'What the hell?' they would all want to know. And it
would be up to me to tell them.
"See where that would leave me? I hated to kill the
story; I was young once myself. But I had to or begin
packing up before the paper was off the press. And you
know what that would mean, with two kids in junior high
school and a girl about ready for college."
He looked Giles Dabney over and wondered how this
man in the thirties could still have on his face the believing
look of a boy.
"You weren't in the war, were you?"
"Yes."
"Very long?"
"I saw about all of it-all of our part."
The editor found that difficult to believe.
"See any action?"
"Yes. The Italian front and the Meuse."
"Supplementary service?"
"No. Air."
"I didn't know we had any planes on the Italian front."
"I was with an Italian bombing plane."
The editor went back to city planning. "Well, the only
way to get anything as big as that across is to line up the

Giles tried Perry Settles.

He remembered that it was always easier to get some-

Giles thus found himself so abruptly in the presence of

"But I have in mind an enterprise that I feel sure you'll

The heavy president was unnaturally pink and his hair
and closely cropped mustache were clear white. In his
warm gray tweeds against a dark background of chair he
constituted a perfect portrait of an old man who is both
powerful and distrustful.
"Well, why not just tell me about it now in five minutes.
Then we'll be through. I have to dispose of lots of things
like that every day. I'd be swamped if I didn't completely
snowed under. And as it is, I send most of this Chamber of
Commerce stuff down to Settles. Why didn't you go to him
instead of coming to me?"
"I did. He was-away."
"All right; then go ahead. Just tell me a little-in gen-
eral terms."
Giles Dabney was in no frame of mind to reduce his
plans to general terms. But rather than miss the oppor-

thought were the high points, the points of universal advan-

THE HILLIKIN
225
sion change from distrust to alertness; from alertness to

"Yes, you'd have to consider it as a unit."
"Perfectly crazy! Perfectly crazy! Why, young man,
it would take a hundred million before you got through

"Possibly, before you got through with it. But it wouldn't
all be done in six months or a year. It might take twenty

Mr. Williston smiled in amusement. "Have you ever
made over a city in this fashion anywhere?"
"No-not yet."
"Or did you ever see exactly such a city as you've
sketched?"
"Not exactly. I've tried to plan this one to meet Steel
City's special needs.”
Mr. Williston smiled a more devastating smile. "So
you'd make us a kind of guinea pig to experiment on—is

"No. But every city derives its life from an undupli-
cated environment. You can't make a standard plan that
will fit all cities."
"There might be something in it for a small town, I can
see that. But for a great city like ours that has grown
by leaps and bounds until it is beyond control and is subject
only to economic forces, the idea is simply crazy-that's
the only word."
He looked at Giles Dabney as if he scarcely knew whether

THE HILLIKIN

The fat, pink old man laughed derisively in his great

"But has a sound economic and artistic plan for Steel

Mr. Williston expressed the ferocity of the oldest lion

"Of course it has! Of course it has! Don't you ever do

"One change might not be fatal, but just as soon as you

THE HILLIKIN

CHAPTER VIII
So that was what the important Mr. Williston
thought about it, was it? "Radical," "visionary"—
he had used those words, too. But how much did it matter?
Who was going to be influenced by the ravings of a fat old
Midas who already had hardening of the arteries?
Giles Dabney threw his hat on the bed in his room,
glanced at himself in the mirror, walked to the window and
looked out, dropped into a rocking chair and rocked furi-
ously, got up and ran through the portfolio of maps and
sketches on his drafting-table, felt content with what he saw,
and then spent the entire afternoon and evening enjoying
the experience of feeling bellicose and adequate.
But the next morning the old man's anathema was waiting
for him when he awoke. "A crazy idea!" The words had
made an impression.
He still smiled at all of Mr. Williston's choleric ragings
against change. But his smile was slightly less confident
than it had been the night before. His idea was not crazy—
of course it was not. It was comprehensive; yes, to be sure.
Yes, it was comprehensive, he had to admit. Possibly, too,
it was the very least bit grandiose. But what if it was?
Still, there was old Williston opposing it. He possessed
influence, too—or he could buy it. And there was President
Wiswall-supported by his trustees. And all of these had
friends-influential friends, too, be it remembered. There
they all were, great burly citizens, strongly entrenched, and
standing firmly, closely with hands clasped, surrounding
Steel City like the withed posts of a mighty stockade. And
they were saying: "So? Do you think we are going to let

Some of the deep canals of streets were blue with the

THE HILLIKIN
241
heard and were so convulsed in laughter that Giles had to

Giles thought he did very well for the time at his dis-
posal. He spoke of the advantages, economic, humanitarian,
and æsthetic, that were to be derived from well-planned
cities. The ancients recognized this fact, just as George
Washington did when he engaged Major Charles Pierre
L'Enfant to plan the capital of the United States. If the
politicians who thought they knew more than L'Enfant had
not messed things up in the nineteenth century, the city
of Washington would be the most beautiful city in the world.
Steel City had never been planned. It was a battleground
where conflicting forces left confusion rather than orderliness
and livableness. He was interested in bringing the chaos
of the city into harmony. It would have to be done by
treating the city as a unit. "A city is like a jelly-fish: you
have to lift all of it if you want to lift any of it. If the
railroads were rerouted, and the new mills required to build
farther east away from the residence section, and smoke
prohibited, and the frightful bridges across the river
replaced by beautiful ones, and the chief part of the old city,
now abandoned to the negroes, cleared and developed
as a great civic center-municipal government, reading
rooms, drama, painting, and diverse recreations-the city
might become a kind of model for all cities of the same
class."
Before the thin applause subsided as Giles sat down, some
lusty man called in heroic voice: "There ain't no other cities
of the same class!"
"Hear! Hear!" others called. "Now you've said some-
thing!"
Mrs. Ainslee was baffled, but hoped desperately that
things might yet be brought around to end on the right
note so that she would not seem to be quite so much of a
fool for having had Giles come. So she invited questions.

But Giles preferred to be alone as soon as possible.
He walked along broad avenues where brick walls and
wrought-iron fences enclosed estates that looked cold in the
November moonlight. Then he descended through a blanket
of smoke and frosty vapor into the heart of the city. But
he scarcely saw anything. He was too busy with his own
reflections upon the futility of this particular evening.
He climbed the steep sidewalk of Hillside Avenue where
houses stood so close together that there was just room for
the rain to drip off one of them without dripping on another.
Before he went in he turned to take a look at the confused,
illogical monster that slept there below him, and to compare
it with his dream.
"A crazy idea!"
CHAPTER IX
NOULD it be that the practical men were right? Was
his idea too much detached from the requirements
C
of a city of workaday mood? Should he be content
with something less drastic-with snipping off some spe-
cially horrible ugliness here, some outrageous false economy
there? After all, these people were the city. Perhaps he
ought to try a little more assiduously to see from their
point of view. Perhaps if only he were moderate in his
proposals, he could enlist all sorts of people in his cause.
And certainly if one were ever to remake a city, the people
living in it must be enlisted. Nothing so inclusive could
be done single-handed. Perhaps and his queries and his
doubts multiplied.
Such periods of wondering and inquiry were always fol-
lowed by long hours over his pile of plans. And always
he arrived at the same conclusion: there was no part of his
conception that he could discard. At first he arrived at
this conclusion somewhat apprehensively. He had better
be doubly sure. He had better turn back to make certain
that this or that point was not useless or vulnerable.
When he had satisfied himself, he emerged into a period
of great confidence. There it was as a completed thing—
or as nearly a completed thing as it could be when it dealt
with a growing city. He was ready to defend it even in
its weakest point.
Suppose he were to be called before a committee to
justify himself: here is how he would answer. Wouldn't
he brush the cobwebs from their minds? And wouldn't
his revelation of the strength of his weakest points fill them
with gasping admiration for his entire conception?
243
244
THE HILLIKIN
By the time he had conducted one of these imaginary
cross-examinations of himself and had answered all ques-
tions to the satisfaction of the examiner, he was in such
a state of wild confidence that he cared not a fig whether
old Williston or anybody else approved his idea or not.
He knew he himself—that it was sound, and he did not
mean to mutilate it.
One day while he was in one of his ecstatic moods he
went to call on Mrs. Ainslee. When he was ushered into
one of a procession of vast living-rooms, he found Mrs.
Ainslee drinking tea with an old school friend. At once this
friend began a series of questions designed to reveal to her
why a vigorous-looking tall man should be free of a business
office at this hour of the day. Giles Dabney found it neces-
sary, therefore, to make all the explanations of his profession
yet again, and hear once more all the customary inane
little exclamations of interest.
He did his part with such easy eloquence that quite
incidentally he restored Mrs. Ainslee to a state of con-
fidence.
"So sorry, Grace, that you could not be at our dinner.
You see I had him tell us something about all this then.”
The two sentences were in such fruitful juxtaposition that
Mrs. Ballantine felt moved at once to ask Giles to come

some of her friends who were always looking for something

supported.
Giles Dabney had grave doubts, but he accepted the in-
vitation. He might as well go to tea as do nothing.
He was in one of his dumpy moods when he went. He
wanted to know of himself what was the good of gabbling
for an hour or more with a lot of women who could not

of so many women-some of them not at all difficult to
THE HILLIKIN
245
look at-and the sound of his own authoritative voice speak-

repugnant. Yet he was so concerned with having support

Almost before he knew it he was going to teas and to
dinners without number and to all sorts of much-managed
meetings among the well-to-do and the unoccupied where he
thought his idea might find lodgment. Men were always
in the minority-except at the dinners. So he always had
plenty of women on his hands to listen up into his cheerful,
earnest face, and to wonder why so much naturally waved
hair had to be wasted on a man.
He explained his occupation to white-haired dames living
in that pleasant state of exclusive senility where all ques-
tions are purely academic. He made the same comprehen-
sive explanation to an even greater number of fifty-year-
old women who felt that something ought to be done,
though they hardly knew what. To the forty-year-olds he
spoke the least bit more circumspectly, for some of them still
carried a natural flush on their cheeks and asked irrelevant
questions such as when he thought a woman was most
dangerous. Of course he said, "At about forty," and they
rejoiced at the glad tidings. To the ones of his own age
he spoke more circumspectly still, for when they gathered
about him with a look that combined a degree of sophisti-
cation with the fearlessness of youth, he knew that he had
lied to the women of forty. He was most at ease with
the débutantes and young fiancées, for they were only so
many replicas of his girl students. They needed to hunt
up no subjects of interest, for was not everything inter-
esting-including themselves? They said, "Oh, how thrill-
ing!" and crowded close and pretended to listen and
246
THE HILLIKIN
understand. Some of them did-almost. But all the while
they were jockeying and squirming into fascinating postures
designed to distract a man from landscape architecture and
the confusion of the city-just the least poetic bit.
One day he discovered that spring was in the wind. He
had put in a winter going to teas and talking about cities.
He had lived the season through in daily expectation. And
how much better off was he? He had wanted to be taken
seriously. Was there no one in Steel City who saw that the
fate of the world rested somewhat on the present moment?
He could not answer with certainty. He could only say
that the people he had met were not serious about anything.
They were so far removed from ordinary needs that they
did not take even possessions seriously. "Say, I've got a
good one on Charley. He lost a million and a quarter in
that deal yesterday. Let's call him up and give him a kid-
ding." The hard edges of reality that remind most people
that they are missing something-perhaps pretty nearly
everything did not exist for the Neo-Babylonians. He felt
more alone than before he had met them.
He began to stay in bed later in the morning. Why get
up? He had nothing to do. If he stayed in bed, there
might be some mail on the oak stand in the front hallway.
Anyhow, the day would be shortened by so much. It would
not be so long till to-morrow; and to-morrow some great
thing might reasonably come to pass.
But weeks of to-morrows came and brought no stu-
pendous happening. They did not so much as bring him
mail, save an occasional letter from his mother back in
Wiggam's Glory. She reported that they needed rain, or
that his father had planted the early spring potatoes the
evening before, or that he was selling all the blue vases
he could make at the little pot-shop down in the hollow.
Whenever he received a letter of that kind he bounded
from the house and pressed forth. Where? It did not much
THE HILLIKIN
247
matter. He could not remain inactive. He must bring
something to pass. His father, numbed early by fate, and
tied forever to the hills, would outstrip him yet. Perhaps
he had already done so.
Once when he had thus to flee from his inactivity, he
pushed desperately all the way through town, out past the
mills and the mazes of railroad tracks, and on and on along
the lake shore until he came to a beach where a dozen or two
solemn-faced men and women in tight bathing suits faced
the miniature breakers and pretended to enjoy themselves.
He found a quiet retreat up in the edge of a grove. He
wanted to look off over the blue of the lake.
The lake
was comforting. Close in front of him inland-looking gulls
screeched piteously and flashed silver wings in the sun. He
forgot Steel City; he forgot Wiggam's Glory; he forgot
himself and his sense of futility.
How long had he remained there? It must now be past
noon, for more people were coming to the beach.
They came down from automobiles that they had used
as dressing-rooms, from street cars that circled in a long
loop near the lake front. They shouted, they shrieked.
Were they not gay and free? Yet their faces, even a strange
overtone in their shrieking, belied their voices. They had
rushed out here to get away from something-just as he
had done. They lingered long, just as he lingered, because
there was something in the city they hated to go back to
face. By nature as spirited as the rest of the animal king-
dom, they lived nevertheless in a constant fear, and they
had to find a means of forgetfulness.
He rode back to town on a street car and listened to
their conversation. They talked about the fatnesses of
their bodies; about what they were going to have for sup-
per; about boys and girls up front who pinched each other
and squealed, or cuddled close down in the seat and engaged
in the most energetic spooning; about this and that which
248
THE HILLIKIN
elicited shouts and screams of, "Did you all hear that?"
"Oh, my God!"
They seemed to dream of a new Steel City just about
as much as Mr. Williston and all the younger Neo-Baby-
lonians.
His conviction of inadequacy hurt him until he was physi-
cally sick. Or was he hungry? Well, he ate, anyhow, as
soon as he was off the car, and then made his way up
Hillside Avenue in the late afternoon sun.
How great had been the irony of his life! He had en-
visaged some vast romantic world far from Wiggam's Glory
where he could realize one fair dream after another. And
now with his youth far spent, here he was!
He went up to his room. But he wanted to run away
from it. The walls pressed close and suffocated him. His
drafting table and the stove and the large portfolio on top
of his high square trunk all menaced him with their in-
dubitable presence.
"A crazy idea!"
He broke out in a cold sweat of fear.
But was it "a crazy idea"? Well, now, let's see. Let's
make sure. Let's look at the plans again and settle the
matter.
The plans seemed all right. He leafed through them
again, and his jumpy mind seized only upon the least con-
vincing of his proposals. Even these seemed sound. But he
had better go through them all just once more. Yes, on
paper everything was sound enough. On paper, that was it!
But how about the idea when it was actually expressed on the
ground? Maybe he was impractical. Maybe the plans did
not tell the whole story. He had better go up on College
Hill before it got dark and take just one more look. Then
he would know, and could feel at ease.
When he reached the pergola he wanted to run away
from the city. Yet there it was. It fascinated him with
THE HILLIKIN
249
the charm of a rattlesnake that stirs its coiled body in slight
undulations and says, "Well, I am ready."
He began carefully, critically, and for the hundredth
time went over every major part of his conception. When
he had finished he was amazed, as he had been many a
time before, to see how easily the whole idea might be
carried through, part by part, year by year, to full com-
pletion.
It was a great idea! And yet everybody remained silent
about it and about him. Why did not somebody say some-
thing-anything? If only they would circulate some stu-
pendous lie about him, that would be a start. He could
utter a sweeping denial and let people know that he existed.
He was ready to be run down by an automobile, to be
kidnaped, to be charged with murder, if only some in-
cidental reference should be made to the fact that he was
a city planner and that he had developed plans for a new
Steel City. Why did not Mr. Alexander Williston come
forward with a letter in the Herald or the Star and beg the
attention of the public to the fact that a bolshevik person
with a questionable reputation and no social standing what-
ever was planning a revolution that was to begin under the
guise of rebuilding the city? If only somebody, anybody,
would direct attention to the fact that Giles Dabney, hilli-
kin, lived and dreamed!
He turned to go. A swarthy, stout man with jaws
clamped down on a fat cigar walked toward the pergola.
He looked as if he were surprised to find somebody already

Giles Dabney, in turn, was amazed.

"Yes, I'm Giles Dabney."

Life had become stupendous. Not that Giles
Dabney was wholly unrestrained in his antici-

The next morning at breakfast a specially diminutive

255
256
THE HILLIKIN
What's that? He unfolded the paper and read: "Sidman
Huntt, head of well-known People's Realty Corporation,
tells staff writer of the Star that the time has come when
the citizens can no longer blink the facts."
And what were the facts? Mr. Huntt said that the
city had been too long in the hands of grasping, selfish in-
terests. It was time for a new emphasis. The city had
never been developed methodically; it had been thrown
together hodgepodge by those who were concerned only
with financial profits. It had become utterly confused,
heterogeneous, top-heavy. It was no longer a livable city
in any strict sense of the word. Worse still, it was rapidly
growing more and more unlivable because of its own chronic
blindness. There must be foresight, civic consciousness.
There must be ordered, comprehensive planning for future
developments.
"In the special interview granted to a member of our staff
late yesterday," the Star went on to say, "Mr. Huntt ex-
plained in great detail the dream of a new Steel City that
he had been quietly evolving through several years. His
ideas on the subject had at last assumed the form of a pretty
definite plan which he proposed to ask the city to adopt
'in principle.' It would be necessary, of course, to secure
a certain amount of technical advice, but that would not
be difficult.
"Mr. Huntt proposes a complete, unified development,

the isolation of all new mills that are built so that the

Mr. Williston's interview in the Herald filled a column
on the front page and two or three columns on the inside.
It seems that he, too, had long been considering plans for
a new Steel City. In fact, in his own unpretentious way,
he had been devoting time regularly to the matter day by
day, month by month. He could not, therefore, be accused
of haste by those who might think he expressed himself
on the subject with too great conviction.
Mr. Williston was opposed to Mr. Huntt's plan almost
in toto. What the city most needed was not suburban
development, but the remaking of certain parts of the down-
town district so that people could come there freely to
transact business. The suburbs would take care of them-
selves. Mr. Huntt and other enterprising real estate dealers
would see to that, undoubtedly. The civic center was too
costly to be thought of; taxes were so high already that
a man paid most of his earnings to the government for
the support of something that benefited somebody else.
And telling industrialists where they had to locate their
mills was something unthinkable in free America.
Mr. Williston did not wish to impute bad motives to
anybody, but it looked as if Mr. Huntt had hatched up
this entire scheme as a means of promoting the real estate
business. Or rather he had borrowed the scheme. For
the scheme in many if not most of its aspects was one that
he could state positively had been worked out by a visionary
and discredited college professor. Just why Mr. Huntt, who
had business interests himself at stake, should want to play
into the hands of all the theorists and radicals and com-
munists and rag-tag malcontents who were seeking to under-
260
THE HILLIKIN
mine democratic institutions, was more than he could under-
stand-unless Mr. Huntt was turning radical himself.
Mr. Williston was as much in favor of true progress as
anyone. He always had been. He was ready to admit,
moreover, that certain streets in the city ought to be wid-
ened, if this could be done without damage to private
property. But it ought to be done by some accredited,
practical engineer who sees things as they are, and not by
some visionary who is content with nothing short of wreck-
ing the world.
Sidman Huntt replied in the Star the following day. He
meant to come out into the open. He meant to tell the
people of Steel City how the entire municipal life was
manipulated for the financial profit of a few.
Mr. Williston had accused him of being financially con-
cerned. Well, let the people who read the newspapers go
over the records in the county building and discover who
owned the property down in the heart of the city and then
see if they did not have a new understanding of Mr. Willis-
ton's concern for the congested district and his provision
that no improvements designed to promote the general wel-
fare should encroach on private property. Just how, Mr.
Huntt begged to know, could any improvements whatsoever
be made without encroaching upon somebody's private
property?
Steel City had something to talk about. Every man—at
least every prominent man-who had an ax to grind came
forward with his proposal. It seems that the entire popu-
lation of business men had long been at work on plans for
a new Steel City. If any man's particular selfishness was
not revealed in his proposal itself, it was always revealed
by somebody else the next day. The newspapers were
crowded with accusations, recriminations, and pledges of
high motives and of undying faith in the city and its future.
Giles Dabney rushed about in frenzy and tried to estab-
THE HILLIKIN
261
lish his claim. But who was interested in getting to the
heart of the matter? Who was worrying about giving
credit where credit was due? Even the wives and daughters
and occasional husbands and fathers that he had met when
he was crusading for his idea among the Neo-Babylonians
now said-if they said anything at all-"He probably got
his idea from these people who have been working on the
matter."
They had stolen his idea! And now they were proposing
that it be mutilated according to the requirements of their
own particular selfishnesses.
He tried to cling to the hope that out of it all something
might come to him; that when the warring factions could
not get together, they might give him an opportunity to be
heard. It was his idea. He had the blue-prints, had he
not?
But this desperate hope died a painful death. Whatever
they might say about each other, they were not going to
say anything about him. He was only a crude enthusiast
who for reasons of policy should be kept in the background.
They were united against anybody who was not one of
themselves. They were interested only in practical ideas
that brought results. They were a hundred, a thousand,
crafty opportunists; a hundred, a thousand, bandwagon
enthusiasts who were ready to change front and follow
anybody who promised profits. But they did not mean to
follow any visionaries who were governed by an artistic
conscience.
One day an irresistible doubt crept into his mind. In
some form, it had been there before. But to-day it came
with a new authority. It asked commandingly: "Now hon-
estly, isn't your dream the least bit fantastic? If you were
one of these men, should you favor it yourself? Might it
not be without vital relation to the immediate circumstances
of actual fact?"
262
THE HILLIKIN
This doubt lingered. It bore down heavily, persistently.
It said, "You know, after all, other people have been wrong.
You might be, too, mightn't you?"
For the first time, when he got out his sketches and blue-
prints, his work failed to convince him. He checked up his
specific doubts and proved by logic that they were without
justification. But he could not eradicate them.
He felt the blood leaving his veins. He felt a sweeping
coldness all over his body. He was wrong. He was only a
country bumpkin who had dreamed dreams, and had thought
he could do anything he attempted. He had never grown
up. He had never adapted himself to the workaday needs
of the world about him. He had gone on being a pro-
vincial when he should have adapted himself to the require-
ments of genteel existence.
All other great sufferings in his life were as nothing com-
pared with this first complete admission to himself that he
might be wrong. He walked the floor tremblingly and tried
to say something to somebody, but his shame was so great
that he could not speak. He chanced to see himself in the
mirror, and the frightened look on the reflected face filled
him with a new terror. What was becoming of him? What
would become of him in the future if the sense of rightness
within him was not to be trusted?
Why had he stayed on and on in the city? Clearly, the
city was not where he belonged. It irritated him with its
false logic, its triviality, its absurdity, its tragedy. The
only people who ought to live in a city were the ones who
had been born there and who had never learned, there-
fore, of the existence of anything that was better. Why not
get away now, and let Steel City blunder and bungle along
until it inevitably committed suicide?
He must get away. He had been frightened once when
he went down to the leavings of No. 5 Company Row and
had seen Gustie Malloy and Sockey McPherson in the
THE HILLIKIN
263
maelstrom of a great funnel from which there was no escape
-frightened for Gustie and Sockey. And here he was now,
himself, in the same funnel. It seemed that anybody could
get caught in it-anywhere.
The walls of his room pressed in close upon him. Ter-
rified, he rushed from the house, and then rushed all the way
up the street toward the hilltop. He must get where there
was better air. He must get up the steep side of the fun-
nel before he was suffocated.
He looked with a sweeping glance from the pergola. What
spread before him had never seemed so monstrous.
Yes, he would get away.
To be sure, getting away would look like defeat. But
what of that? He must get away anyhow. There were a
thousand places where he could go; there were a thousand
things that he could do. He could develop a special reputa-
tion in planning gardens. That was not so difficult. He
would get in touch with some of his old friends. He would
bump into some of his former students and-
But he would try not to bump into them too soon. After
he was established-that would be the time. Then he could
meet them unexpectedly and say, "Oh yes, I am here-
have been here for some time," just as casually as that.
"I am building a private avenue just now. Nothing tre-
mendous about it, you see, but I find it very interesting."
He would get away!
And now that he had made the decision, he saw the city
before him with the clarity of a man on the verge of mad-
ness. It had, in truth, been built by idiots. Who else
could have built anything so absurd?
It was a fish flopping and gasping in a pool that was
drying up, and it would come to a slow death-no, a quick
death-if others did not provide it with everything its life
required. A farmer could live indefinitely among his fel-
lows in the country if the city did not exist. But if the
264
THE HILLIKIN
farmers should go on a great concerted strike, the city would
be crying in hunger within a week. If the railroads or
trucks refused for a week to haul milk, the infant mortality
in the city would leap to a new high. Or if the miners
should decide to take a vacation some November, the city
soon would be dying of cold. It had lost all power to
supply its own wants. Of course, if it needed cabbages, it
had to import them. That was taken for granted. It could
not be expected to produce vegetable matter on asphalt
streets. But it had to import everything else. If it needed
a poet-and it did not feel the need very acutely its source
of supply was the hills of New England or Pennsylvania or
Arkansas, or the hot plains of Illinois, or some sunlit moun-
tainside overlooking the Pacific Ocean. If it needed a
soprano, it found her likewise in the provinces or in some
remote region in southern Europe among people who had
not yet lost the power and the will to sing.
It could not live without others. Yet it laughed at them.
It looked at itself and said, "How invincible I am, even in
the slightest details of judgment!" And then it laughed.
It laughed at the farmer because he ate the kind of break-
fast he required, and not the kind it relished; because he
wore the kind of clothes suited to his needs. It could not
live without the coal miner, yet it treated him—if it ever
thought of him at all—as a low-grade being who engaged
in dirty work because dirt was his preference. It could
not live without all these others who humbly gave their
lives that it might live, yet it said, "See how superior and
full of cosmopolitan wisdom I am!" And then it proved
its superiority by driving sharp bargains with everyone
for the very things it could not do without.
It was as cruel to its own, too, as it was to others. A
man could starve to death next door to a restaurant and
nobody would know of his existence. A man could freeze
to death next door to a heating plant, or on a park bench,
THE HILLIKIN
265
or close against the abutment of a busy bridge, and nobody
would go to the trouble to identify him.
The city said: "There is nothing soft or warm or rhythmic
about me. My angles are unyielding, and they are sharp.
If you do not wish to break, then you had better bend;
for I never do."
And all the indiscriminate particles in this monstrous en-
tity were one in refusing heed to his dream. They were
not interested in his regard for his individual self, in his
earnestness of spirit, in his habit of trying to see things
whole. They never knew the fever of a great, desperate
aspiration, a great passion, a great desire. They never
waited long enough to enjoy anything to the full. They
must go right on and on with their own respectable little
lusts, their own respectable great dishonesties, their own
pumped-up superiorities. They must not be diverted in
their efforts to keep up to the minute. Their rapidly chang-
ing vogues were established by no one in particular, by no
one who was a whit more intelligent than they themselves.
Yet they let themselves be swept along by whatever chanced
to be the vogue, like a half-million or million iridescent fish-
flies undulating in a given direction until some other chance
current of vogue caught them and sent them all in another.
The great man among them was not the man with an idea
that called for thoughtful, deliberate consideration and then
adoption or rejection, but the man who could outguess his
fellows on what next season's vogue would be. And one
guess was as good as another.
These were the wise and mighty who asked: "How could
this unproclaimed dreamer possibly know anything that
would be useful to us?"
He was filled with a great loathing. As he turned to
walk away, convulsed in the half-tearful state of early nerv-
ous collapse, he cried out softly to himself the words of
a tragic young Frenchman whom he worshiped: “Oh, come
266
THE HILLIKIN
and preach peace after that! Come and ask men who
suffer to take pity upon what is! Pity, never! Hatred,
that is all! He who does not feel it deeply, this hatred of
the present, cannot really have in him the love of the
future!"
He felt a little less anguish after he had repeated the
young Gaul's brave words.

CHAPTER XI
HEN he came down from the hilltop, a pleasant,
open-faced man of twenty-eight or so sat on the
front porch at Mrs. Dibblee's waiting for him.
"You don't know me," the caller explained as he arose
with so much show of respect that Giles for a moment was
tugged away from his suffering, "but I once had the pleasure
of hearing you speak. I am Graham Ainslee. My mother
had you come to our house a year or so ago. I came in
just when you were speaking, and listened to you from out
in the hallway-round the doorpost. Ever since then I've
had in mind looking you up, but I've been away more or
less. You are not going out?"
Giles assured him that he had no plans whatever for the
evening. No, he was at leisure. But in the grip of his
great hurt, he found words slow in coming.
"You are sure?" young Ainslee asked as if he were a
person of sensitiveness himself. "I don't wish to disarrange
your evening, for I had nothing very specific in mind. I
just thought I'd like to know you better."
Giles looked at him with a sharp glance. Was this some
new breed of creature or was he only a more genteel varia-
tion of Sidman Huntt?
"I'd ask you up to my room, but it is stuffy up there this
evening." Giles shuddered at the thought that he himself
anyhow would have to go up a little later.
After Graham Ainslee's "Oh, this is very pleasant here,"
there was silence again. Giles did not mean to be ungra-
cious, but he could not quickly get his mind away from
where it had been for days.
267
268
THE HILLIKIN
At last he ventured: "You've been away, you say?"
"Yes, way down in West Virginia—in the coal fields. I
got fed up on the blah of this damned town and went down
there and got a job as a coal miner, just to taste a little
reality."
"Did you taste any?"
"Well, I tasted something, all right. When a man is
about two miles back under a hill with the only air he's
going to get being pumped in to him by some measly fan,
that may break down any time; and one meal in his dinner
bucket in case the hill caves in and leaves him back there;
and some tools and some dynamite or powder saying, 'Go
to it'; and a solid black face of coal saying 'Come ahead'-
well, a man knows he's not exactly playing bridge, any-
how."
"I came from a coal region myself," Giles explained
humbly.
"You? No! Why-why I understood you came from
over in the East somewhere-though now when I think of
it I don't believe I remember hearing you say anything
about any 'law-r' of economics or any 'vanilla-r' ice-cream."
"Did you attend Steel City College?"
"No, that is something else I dodged."
He laughed, but stopped abruptly.
"Pardon me, I believe you taught there once, didn't you?
I didn't mean to be personal, of course. I suppose there
are some good people up there-there must be. But I
picked out a little college down the way here on the level
land where they still have professors who aren't afraid to
talk about creating a world that will exalt the human spirit
instead of stultifying it."
Giles Dabney again looked sharply. He could scarcely
trust his own ears.
"You know," Ainslee went on, seemingly pleased to have
such free going, "I've a theory that there are plenty of in-
THE HILLIKIN
269
teresting people in the world-well, maybe not plenty of

Abruptly he asked a question. "You are not by any

They talked about Rathman and medals and college and

"By the way," he said at last, quite casually, "what have

Then Graham Ainslee asked hesitantly: "You don't hap-

270
THE HILLIKIN
top of a drafting table where a man could take a look at

A desperate suspicion slipped into Giles Dabney's head.
"Why, the truth is, they are all rolled up and stuck away

"Oh well, don't bother. Another time will do.
I just
thought that maybe you might be working on them. Maybe
some time when you do have them out― You see, I became

He was fearful that despite Giles's protests, he had been
an awkward intruder. He hoped not. It had been a great
pleasure to come. If it would not be too much of a bore
for Giles, he should like to come again.
As soon as he had gone Giles Dabney's mind was full of
all sorts of regrets and fears and doubts. Possibly young
Ainslee had spoken in utter innocence? What would have
been the harm of showing the plans to him? If he had
stolen something from them, it would have been no loss.
Everybody else had. Ainslee must have thought he was a
cad-or a boor.
So they were going to appoint a committee, were they?
Oh, irony! He had set them going—and they did not know
it. Greater irony still: he had set them going with plans
that were defective. He now knew that they were defective.
Might the committee ever include him and give him a
chance to make some really good plans? He thought not.
He was an outlander. He was not good committee material.
He carried no prestige. Nobody would turn to him-nobody
unless it was Huntt. And now Huntt dare not. Huntt
was out to make money, and nobody ever made money by
stepping forth to champion a visionary and a radical. No,
he was afraid the committee would not include him.
It was just as well, perhaps. Somebody-and he was
frightened at the thought-might bring up the question of
where this movement started, and that would lead to the
THE HILLIKIN
271
revelation that he had made plans that were unworthy of
a good planner.
Yes, he had to admit it, his plans were very imperfect.
He sat bent over, his eyes staring and his lower jaw re-
laxed, and in his fear he let his head sink until at last it
rested on the back of his hands across his knees.
For a long time he sat thus. Then he got up hopelessly,
wearily and made ready to go to bed. He threw himself
down, buried his face in the pillow, and would have sobbed
like a child if he could have done so. That was what he
wanted to do.
He had struggled, and now he was lost. In his youth he
had battled against certain obvious odds and had gained a
little ground. In the war, when the sky was alive with
enemy planes and he had said, "Let's go!" he had not been
without a strange sense of adequacy. But a monstrous,
crafty enemy with a million leering or indifferent faces had
done what no other enemy had been able to do. His dream
was fantastic, crazy, inapplicable. There was something
wrong with it; he could not say just what, but he could now
see that there was something. He thought he had put the
best of himself into it. He thought he had put all of himself
into it. And that all was not sufficient.
The next morning he knew he had slept. He lay in bed
and felt a certain calm in the world and in his own mind.
He was afraid to move, lest he reawaken chaos. He would
just lie very still for a while and think about where he
could best go. After he had gone away, all these people
to whom he had talked with overenthusiasm about his plans
would quickly forget him. That would be pleasant for them
and for him. Time would bring a rich obliviousness.
He would go somewhere and settle down and care for
people's grounds: he had already decided that. And the
years would slip along, and the sun would shine, and the
showers would come, and the green things of the earth
272
THE HILLIKIN
would grow, and while he busied himself with these, the
old hurt would heal a little—perhaps much—and he could
go on caring for things that grew-for he did know how
to do that-and every year a little more of the grayness
of a sense of failure would pass away, and life would be
almost bearable again.
Over and over this vague pathway of escape his mind
traveled. Old Williston would sit down at the table with
President Wiswall when they ate together at the club, and
ask: "Whatever became of that wild-eyed radical you
turned loose on our town?" And they would both laugh,
and Wiswall would answer, "Oh, he just faded out of the
picture. I don't know whether he tried to get in some-
where else or whether he jumped into the river. Anyhow,
I'm glad to be rid of him." And they would smile over their
chicken à la king, and old Williston would say, "I can't
for the life of me quite understand how a college with all of
Harvard's prestige and prominent connections would ever
let such a man graduate."
It would be painful enough, of course, if a man had to
hear it. But he would be away. While they were saying
that, he would be busy with somebody's pleasant grounds
in-well, it did not matter where. It would be somewhere.
He would be out of Steel City.
Mrs. Dibblee with much white hair showing under the
edges of her lacy cleaning-up cap, stepped boldly into the
room.
"Oh, I beg your pardon!" she exclaimed. "I supposed
you had gone out long ago. Are you sick?"
No, he was not sick-just a little under the weather. He
meant to get up almost immediately, since he had several
things to round up in the course of the day.
The sight of a newspaper frightened him. Yet after he
had eaten a bite he sat on a broken bench in the tiny park
across from the restaurant and read both the Herald and
THE HILLIKIN
273
the Star. They carried more or less identical headlines.
Steel City business men who had long been pondering the
need of replanning the city had agreed upon the committee
that was to determine what was to be done. Many interests
were involved. No group ought to be favored at the expense
of any other. It had been decided, therefore, to have each
major interest of the city represented on the committee.
This would guarantee the ideal plan, for it would be an ad-
justment, a compromise, of all the conflicting interests.
Giles read the names of the large committee. Mr. Willis-
ton's name headed the list. Sidman Huntt's came next.
More than a dozen others followed-representatives of mills,
railroads, public utilities, wholesalers, retailers—and at the
very end, President Wiswall, representing education and
culture.
This committee meant to go into every phase of the prob-
lem, and make recommendations at a very early date. The
findings would possess the advantage of having been made
by men who knew the problems of Steel City first-hand.
The citizens of the region could rest assured that they
were to have the services of the best minds the city afforded.
