CHAPTER XII
REAT lassitude displaced great anguish. How much
did he care who constituted the committee, or whose
plan might in the end be adopted? He had put his
life behind him. The committee as it stood suited him
quite as well as any other. These men were certain not to
humiliate him by asking to see any defective plans upon
which he had wasted so much of his time.
He was glad he had occasionally accepted commissions to
design gardens. He was glad he had lived economically.
Now he could choose a destination and go to it without
wondering whether he would have enough money. It would
be well to go to a somewhat distant point. The farther he
went, the easier it would be to forget his absurd dream.
Where was he going? He must decide definitely. Yet
he felt in no hurry. It was easier not to move in any
given direction.
It became easier for him to stay in bed in the morning.
It became easier not to decide what he wanted for break-
fast after he did get up. He waited long before he moved
from his place after he had eaten, for he could not decide
what to do next.
Once he hesitated a week over which of two barbers to
go to for a hair-cut. He needed a shave, too. He had not
bothered with shaving himself for three or four days; he
was too much preoccupied with great matters. If ever he
decided which barber was to cut his hair, why, he could get
a shave at the same time. He was already a sorry figure,
he had to admit. But what difference would one day make?
On the way to the barber shop he met Gunning. Gun-
ning's eyes bulged.
274
THE HILLIKIN
275
"Why, man alive!" he exclaimed. "Aren't you sick? I
hope it is nothing serious."
Gunning was effusive in his solicitude. Yet in his manner
Giles Dabney felt something which he used to feel when he
passed some of the Pekingese in the Harvard Yard and they
deigned to look at him: "Clearly, this fellow is not one of
us."
On the way back he saw Graham Ainslee coming in his
direction. He dodged round the corner and then ran hys-
terically, so that Ainslee would be sure not to discover where
he had gone. He must not meet anybody else he knew until
after he had decided something—and maybe not then.
The days were long. Still they were not long enough to
enable his weary spirit to arrive at decisions. By the time
he had decided when to get up and where to eat and what
to order and which paper to read and where to go for a
walk so that he could think things over quietly and decide
just what he was going to do, it was time to begin thinking
about the next meal or wondering if he could not do a little
packing up that evening. The day usually ended by his
sitting for hours in his room perched high on his stool before
his drafting table with his chin on the palm of his hand,
and looking blankly toward nothing in particular.
Occasionally he became aware of himself. Then he was
frantic. Often these intervals came in the heart of the night
when he could not possibly entertain the idea of getting
up for the next day. What was happening to him, he
wanted to know? And then he broke out in the cold sweat
of the nervously depleted once more, and he heard his pulse
pounding violently in his ears, and he saw all of his past
life and all his future life in the flash of an eye, and he knew
that he had failed.
He had failed! He lay stretched out and limp. He felt
a strange flabbiness of flesh and spirit. It was easy to let
great tears trickle down over his cheeks. He wanted to
276
THE HILLIKIN
talk with somebody who would understand him without
unnecessary words, to explain something, to admit having
done something discreditable, to confess to a priest. What
would he confess? He was not at all sure. He wanted to
say, "Don't you see, it was like this?" He was slipping, he
was aware of that, and it would be pleasant to feel the grip
of a hand stronger than his own-the grip of any hand
whatsoever.
The tears would then come faster, and he made no effort
to restrain them. He was giving way, he was letting go, he
was going down-how far he could not say. If going com-
pletely under were no worse than this, he was sure he meant
not to resist.
After two or three hours in this affrighted state, he had
used up so much of his dwindling nervous reserve that he
had not even the will to sleep. Then daylight came and he
had to begin wearily making decisions again. Had he better
have breakfast dishes late and call them luncheon, or lunch-
eon dishes early and call them breakfast? It was hard to
decide.
Where had the summer gone? Would somebody tell him
that? And autumn-had autumn gone too? Was the sharp-
ness of the wind the sharpness of an aging November? Had
he lived through enough days to accumulate all that pile
of newspaper clippings about what the Committee of Fif-
teen had in mind?
One morning at breakfast he read in the newspapers what
the Committee of Fifteen proposed. He laughed so noisily,
so hysterically, that everybody in the restaurant turned to
look. Let them look! Who cared? He had read great
news. He had read that by the time the Committee of
Fifteen had abandoned everything objected to by real estate
dealers, public utilities companies, transportation companies,
manufacturers, and wholesalers and retailers, there was
nothing left to be planned-nothing except the widening of
THE HILLIKIN
277
two or three arterial streets, and a closer coöperation between
suburban promoters and public utilities companies.
He had to laugh. He walked nervously along the quietest
streets he could find and slapped the side of his leg with
his folded newspaper and laughed and let the tears run down
his cheeks as they would. His plans were defective. Like
a repentant sinner he enjoyed making the confession. But
what about the committee's? They were no plans at all.
"The services of the best minds the city afforded!" Could
he ever stop laughing?
One morning he did not get up at all. At noon Mrs.
Dibblee came with a tray of food. "You are not well," she
said; "I know you are not. You had better stay in bed
and keep warm. I brought you your mail, too, and a news-
paper. And if there's anything else you need, I'll get that."
She treated him as if he were a great man who neverthe-
less needed a bit of motherly attention.
"Now eat that broth while it is hot. You need to have
your stomach warmed through and through. You have not
been digesting your food. You are thinner than you used
to be, and there are lines around your mouth. I never did
think restaurant food was fit to eat. It's a wonder it hasn't
killed more people than it has. And when you are through,
just turn over and go to sleep again and I'll see that nobody
disturbs you."
It felt good to be commanded by a firm will. It was
pleasant, too, to have somebody speak about such work-
aday matters as food just as if they were important. Giles
picked up the soup spoon and tasted the broth before Mrs.
Dibblee had got to the door.
But as soon as she was out, he turned to the mail. There
was a letter from Wiggam's Glory. The broth could wait.
What was in the letter? He was in such a state of
nervousness that every moment of suspense filled him with
terror. And in this case, the handwriting was his father's.
278
THE HILLIKIN
What could that mean? His father did not write unless the
occasion was unusual.
He thrust his fingers under the flap of the envelope and
ripped it loose. A train of wild fears crashed through his
brain while he was awkwardly getting the tight letter out.
A bit of something blue dropped to the bedclothes, but he
plunged into the letter.
"Dear Son:
"I could never quite forget that thousand dollars we
were meaning to have for you when the coal company
left us so high and dry. You managed to squeeze along
without it, and I know now that you don't need it, with
all the money you are making in your profession. But
I always thought I'd feel better myself if I was to give
it to you some time, and here it is. Maybe you won't
mind having a little extra for something just at Xmas.
"As your mother may have told you, I've been able to
sell all the ware I make. I make only vases. Mr. Dwyer
says he can handle as many as I send him. He seems
to like them.
"Your affectionate father,
"LUKE DABNEY."
Giles picked up the check and opened it. It was drawn
on a Centerville bank. His father had a bank account!
With the check and letter gripped in his hand, he dropped
back on the pillow and let the easy tears come.
His father, back there in the hills, was tasting life's satis-
factions. After he had been banged about for years in a
coal mine, and then had struggled through other years in
a pot-shop when the tin-can age was coming and taking
away his market for fruit jars, he had at last been able to
unite the business of making a livelihood with his own
stunted artistic aspirations.
THE HILLIKIN
279
How calm his father's letter was!
How free from all the
nervousness induced by a city full of people who are wildly
trying to get credit for being the last word in this or that!
He sat up and ate his broth and the other sick-room deli-
cacies that Mrs. Dibblee had brought to him. Then he
got up and dressed. He thought he must have been negli-
gent of his finger nails for a day or two. He thought he
must have been more or less generally negligent for a much
longer time. He must get out of this. He would take the
check to the bank. He had better deposit it at once. Some-
thing might happen.
The sun was doing its best to penetrate the smoke that
hung over the city and tell everybody to be cheerful. But
instead, everybody was frantic. Cards in every shop win-
dow announced: "Only six more shopping days before
Christmas. You had better not wait." And nobody seemed
to be waiting.
Giles Dabney wormed his way through the crowded cor-
ridor of the bank and placed his thousand dollars on de-
posit. Everybody else seemed to be drawing money out.
He watched for a moment, and then pushed back into the
milling crowd outside.
He enjoyed a sense of great ease as he allowed himself
to be swept along. He was going in the same direction as
everybody else. His legs seemed to have few muscles in
them, yet enough for the business of keeping him going.
His arms, so long and so often in the way, now were no
encumbrance at all.
The crowd diminished steadily as it passed certain doors,
and just as steadily augmented as it passed others. The
result, if nobody considered individuals—and nobody did—
was precisely the same as if nobody were lost and nobody
added. But ahead he could see a clear diminution as the on-
rushing stream passed a great shop painted in lurid colors.
280
THE HILLIKIN
"Nothing over a dollar," it announced, “and most things not
over ten cents."
Giles Dabney allowed himself to be swept inside. Acres
of women were clutching and grabbing over counters,
stretching over the crowd for parcels that salesgirls were
trying to hand to them, buying cheap candy, cheap jewelry,
cheap linen, cheap ribbons-cheap, cheap, cheap! Did
Jesus the Christ know that his birth was to result in this?
Girls banged out a few notes of cheap songs on tinny
pianos for customers who would have nothing short of the
latest word in anything. Pompous salesmen who tried to
look like bank presidents gravely wound up music boxes for
the benefit of juvenile inquirers. Clattering music, tinkling
music, a mélange of music!
From somewhere back in the phonograph-record depart-
ment came abruptly the comforting contralto words, "He
shall feed His flock."
Giles Dabney listened. Fear, joy, and the comfort of a
great release pursued each other through him. He meant to
be self-contained; he was aware of the people all about him.
But his breaking nerves could not hold back the tears even
when he willed. Yes, yes! Let the comforting words sing
on.
He moved in the direction of the music, wiping his eyes
with a flourish of his handkerchief so that he would not be
too much of a spectacle.
Did "He" feed "His" flock? Giles hoped so. "His"
flock needed feeding. They were hungry enough-hungry
enough for something-though they seemed not to know
what.
The music almost overwhelmed him. He felt as if all the
ten million interwoven little threads that customarily held
him together and made of him an integrated being were
snapping or melting away and leaving him an insubstantial
THE HILLIKIN
281
something that would fall into complete nothingness if it
were jostled a little.
The girl in the booth was saying, "Or maybe you'd like
this. I'll play it, too. By Schumann-or no, Schubert; I
always get those two names mixed."
The notes rose to enrapturing heights and then came down
in graceful sweeps like the swallows over the meadow. Giles
enjoyed coming down with them more than going up. It was
easier. He leaned against a massive imitation marble pillar
and half closed his eyes.
Just what was happening to him, he did not know. He
only knew that it was pleasant to offer the world no re-
sistance.
He believed he had better get out in the air. And the
crash of the first notes in a new fox-trot helped him to make
the decision definite.
The air was brisk. He had better take a walk and pull
himself together. He did not wish to see any more of the
crowd. He did not wish to see the city. He craved quiet
by-streets where houses were close and intimate and di-
verting.
Why hadn't he gone somewhere? Here he had remained
in a state of passivity for months. He had better make up
his mind.
Yet he could not. Daily he wandered aimlessly about,
relieved always to see no one who knew him.
One day in a library a book cried out to him. It spoke
of the glory of self-control. He read it. It did him no good.
He read a dozen others. They were equally impotent. Yet
he kept reading. He read everything he came upon. A
book was something to clutch at, to open, to look into for
strength. He read the magazines. He read the newspapers.
Just why he went over every page of the newspapers two
or three times he could not say. He found nothing that
282
THE HILLIKIN
interested him. Still, he might. It would be well to keep
on looking.
He drifted late on Sunday mornings into comfortable
churches to see what they offered, and then let his mind
wander over the face of the earth while some minister with
whooped-up radiance lighting his face preached an unprick-
ing sermon designed to meet the special requirements of a
Steel City congregation. Giles Dabney thought it better
to be out in the openness of the sky and sunshine.
One night he went to hear a guest orchestra, and its pro-
gram was Tschaikowsky's Symphonie Pathétique. All the
while he listened, he saw not what was before his eyes, but
the compact crowd of professors and their wives and daugh-
ters in Sanders Theater, and the scores of Harvard and
Radcliffe students looking down from their high perch above
the stage, as he had seen them when he first heard this
symphony.
He was exhausted for days. A man should have good
nerves when he listened to a symphony-especially this one.
It had taken everything out of him. And yet it kept ring-
ing in his ears just as if the orchestra were still before him.
The cymbals hammered in his head. The liquid, fading
violins left him constantly on an incline that stretched far
down to some half-inviting abyss. And the deep basses and
the kettle-drums kept on shuddering some impending ter-
rible doom.
All of this had come to him from too much dreaming about
a livable city. And nothing had come of the dream. It
was worse than useless. It was "a crazy idea." He would
get that bundle of blue-prints and maps and drawings out of
his trunk and burn them up without giving them one look.
Then they would be gone. Then nothing would hold him.
He could pack up and go.
W
CHAPTER XIII
AS it the motion of the train that had set his mind
flying, or was it only the exhilaration of having
at last made a decision? He wanted to see every-
thing the little towns which the train crashed through
without pausing, the rivers and creeks swollen to lakes by
the late winter rains, the promise of a return to life that
every sunny hill held forth.
Yet he could not immediately escape thought of the city
he had left behind. Giant steam shovels here in the hills
that ripped the earth open brought to him a vision of the
Steel City mills down by the lake front. The shovels were
destroying the hills in the process of getting at the coal
beneath. Who bothered about the beauty of the hills? Steel
City must have cheap coal-at any cost.
That was why Wiggam's Glory had risen to tawdry
affluence and then had faded away entirely. Dingy, dirty
Wiggam's Glory, abandoned when it had been bled of all
that Steel City desired, was Steel City's necessary con-
comitant. Yes, the two went right along together. Wig-
gam's Glory had given its blood in constant transfusion in
order that Steel City might have red corpuscles. Wiggam's
Glory had long ago been sucked dry of its last drop of
blood. There were still thousands of other Wiggam's
Glories, but these, too, were in the process of being sucked
dry. Man was on the way toward realizing his dream of
subduing the earth.
Who had said that man was made in the image of God? If
he was, then God must be a demon. For this thing made
in His image had become the arch enemy of everything on
283
284
THE HILLIKIN
earth. What one animal did the rabbit and the bobwhite
and the pheasant and the deer fear most? What one animal,
when armed with all his specially devised implements of
destruction, filled the entire woodland with tremblings? Nor
was this all. The beasts of the forest that warred on their
fellow beasts at least stopped short of warring on their own
kind. In recognition of the esteem in which they held
themselves, they treated their own species with a certain
degree of tolerance. But man was as destructive of his own
kind as he was of any other. He slaughtered whatever got
in his way, even when he did not so much as need it for
food or raiment.
Giles's hot brain covered time and space without hin-
drance. He saw how the earth would look in ten thousand
years after this creature made in the image of God had had
a fair chance at it. It would be one vast expression of
cavernous desolation as hopeless-looking as the parched,
thin-earthed Out-Back regions of Australia where men are
driven mad by the grayness and the silence.
He caught a glimpse of Wiggam's Glory across the deep
hollows. Immediately he had a new hatred for the spirit
of Steel City. For Wiggam's Glory, when let alone, could
recover from all that Steel City had done to it. The in-
tegrating forces of the earth were already at work covering
up the scarred hills. The hills were healing themselves.
They would keep right on healing themselves.
He felt a flash of hope. Perhaps after the ten thousand
years of destruction that man seemed determined to wreak
upon the earth, the healing process would begin and bring
everything once again back to a modest fruition that would
satisfy the meek.
It was impossible for him to discuss his plight with his
father and mother. He only said that he was weary-too
weary, in fact, to talk. If they did not mind, he thought
THE HILLIKIN
285
he would just stick around for a time and try to sleep and
get rested up.
It was only a degree less impossible for him to accept all
the attentions that his father and mother heaped upon him.
He was their son. But he was also a great man. Could
they not get him special foods? Did he not wish more
fire in the fireplace in the cool of the spring evenings? Did
he not wish to sleep later than he customarily did when he
was at home? By their manner they apologized for all the
little that they had at their disposal when he must be accus-
tomed to having so much. He could only receive their
attentions with a half-serious protest. It was less painful
to them and to him to leave them with their illusions than
to take them away.
Before many days had passed, the novelty of being at
home had worn off. Old questions began to push them-
selves forward in his mind. Just where was he now? That
was what he had to make out. And he was nowhere. He
was just in a state of abeyance-caught in midair without
anything to seize upon for support. He must hurry and
get rested. He must attach himself to something and feel
secure.
He wandered over fields that he once called his own, and
was soothed by the familiar sights and sounds and odors.
He threw himself on the dry leaves that the winds had piled
along the edge of the woods, and let the spring sun bore into
his skin until he felt limp with the warmth. One day when
the sun and the droning bees suggested summer, he tried
stretching out on the new grass in the field. He experienced
some strange new intimacy with the earth. It touched him
as if he might yesterday have sprung directly from it. It
filled him with the pulsations of its own life-pulsations that
expressed themselves in green grass and gray buds and
microscopic pink flowers, and soon would express themselves
more obviously.
286
THE HILLIKIN
He came back to the spot the next day, and for a week.
The mothering earth! He lay with his face close against the
grass and wept without fear. Then when late April showers
had passed he came again. But he was no longer constrained
to weep. He felt the eternally clean earth restoring him.
One day he ventured beyond his own fields. He walked
as far as St. Bernard's. The church spire appeared only
half as high as it was when he was a boy. Just the same,
it was a good spire. The entire church, for that matter,
was a credit to its architect. He walked over to the corner-
stone and looked for the man's name. It was not there;
only the date, 1839.
Why did people quit building good-looking churches like
this? Was it because the spirit of Steel City had got too
much into their blood? Some day, if ever he felt rested,
he meant to think further into the matter.
He wandered like a melancholy poet into the churchyard
-down past all the additions of twenty years to a grassy
spot where an epitaph on a bit of very white marble read:
"Hope, daughter of Denis and Mary Fealty, aged nine."
But strangely he did not feel as he had feared he might.
He was not overwhelmed by any great sadness. All the
anguish that he had suffered as a child when he saw her
dying before his brimming eyes, and all the fear of his own
immediate death when he had heard that she was dead, now
seemed only so much historical incident that was unbe-
lievably beautiful.
He thought he must be getting better.
A few days later he ventured farther still-as far as the
Lookout where he used to stand like a solitary Indian in
the brightness of the morning. The brightness of the morn-
ing had faded somewhat. It was not quite so full of piercing
shafts of light and rosy gleams and golden blue mists.
Still, he felt ready to look off at the hills and reflect upon
THE HILLIKIN
287
what he saw. Why not sit down and go more deliberately
about it?
But his mind refused to limit itself to the Wiggam's Glory
area. It plunged ahead like a Valkyrie leaping from peak
to peak above a battlefield. It demanded something con-
temporary, something resistant to busy itself with. It
plunged to this and that, and before he knew it, brought
him up abruptly in front of a drafting table in a modest
room of Mrs. Dibblee's on the hillside in Steel City.
"What about those crazy plans you made?"
Why had he not burned those plans as he had meant to
do? Here was another evil fruitage of his weariness and
inability to make decisions. They existed. And their mere
existence over there in the trunk right in the room where
he had been sleeping filled him with fright that scorched
like a flame.
But he was able to catch himself up. "Now! Now!
Now!" he counseled himself. "Just wait a minute. Don't
get panicky. Your destiny to that extent is in your own
hands. If you want to destroy the plans, you know you
can. Nobody can prevent you."
Very well, he would burn them. That would be easy
enough. As soon as there was another cool, wet morning
and his mother needed a fire in the fireplace, he would
start it for her.
He was getting his will back. At least he knew once
more that he had a will. He would just put the plans
out of mind.
Why not walk down through the woods among the oaks
and poplars? That would be on his way home. And since
he was tracking down his youth more or less, why not see
the maples, too, and the old gate where he had first met
Beulah Lettinger, and where he had always after found it
easy to meet her.
It was still easy to think about Beulah. She possessed

The old doctor turned on him. "God Almighty, boy, do
you mean to spoil this visit with me? I don't need any
three hundred dollars. Why, I've got almost as many
patients as ever. I can't always go to see them, but I've

diseases get well of themselves, if you give them a good
chance. Plenty of hot water and a little pepsin and some

scription!"
He laughed longer than usual at his own depreciation of
his adored calling.
"And, you know, old man Skinner still sends for me. In

That's God's honest truth, Giles. When I work for a man

you go back, you just tell some of 'em to come out and

"When you go back," Pewee had said.

All the while, his father, stooped slightly and iron gray,
yet with something reminiscent of the slender young god in
his movements, worked at his vases in the little pot-shop in
the hollow. And his mother, straight, heavy-browed, clear
white, and still serene, moved about in the kitchen and

One morning he came downstairs with decision and an-

for Cambridge. He would write later and tell them where
to send his trunk.
They did not reveal the pain of a good-by that hurt more
than most. They did not so much as suggest that they
would feel a little more at ease perhaps, if they knew just
why he was going, or when he might be back. A great
son such as theirs must be expected to have ways of

wished to see something done that was more fundamental

So his crazy idea was still a little in their blood, was it?
Or was it a crazy idea?
Wherever he went, his dream was throbbing in his head
feverishly: "Are we crazy plans? You don't expect to do
much else, do you, until after you have disposed of that
question?"
One day while he walked he suddenly saw the full pre-
cariousness of his state. He was on the way to madness.
He must do something that would bring him a quiet mind—
jump off a high-level bridge into a calm river, or anything
else that might be required.
He sat by the roadside and did the closest, coolest think-
ing he had done in months. He could not stay in the country
there where he was. He could not go back to Steel City.
Where, then, did he mean to go? For he had either to
escape that hellish question or or have it answered.
But if he could not escape it, who could answer it for
him so that his mind would be at rest? And did he wish
to know what the answer was? If, for instance, he should
go back to Cambridge and present his amazing case to
Professor Tillinghast, might he hope for an unprejudiced
answer? But did he wish to go to Professor Tillinghast?
Professor Tillinghast wanted students to bring him long re-
ports of what they had done to save the world from ugliness.
Could he, then, go back and report that he feared he had
done nothing at all, and have Professor Tillinghast confirm
the report? Not only that.
Not only that. If he should tell Professor
Tillinghast that he had come to doubt the validity of his
own spirit, might not Professor Tillinghast henceforth doubt
it, too?

Yet if he did not go back to Professor Tillinghast-
All the while, his father, stooped slightly and iron gray,
yet with something reminiscent of the slender young god in
his movements, worked at his vases in the little pot-shop in
the hollow. And his mother, straight, heavy-browed, clear
white, and still serene, moved about in the kitchen and
among her thriving flowers with an attitude of "Oh, well!
I guess if it is anything we ought to know, he'll tell us."
One morning he came downstairs with decision and an-
nounced that he was all packed up and starting for Boston-
for Cambridge. He would write later and tell them where
to send his trunk.
They did not reveal the pain of a good-by that hurt more
than most. They did not so much as suggest that they
would feel a little more at ease perhaps, if they knew just
why he was going, or when he might be back. A great
son such as theirs must be expected to have ways of
his own.

PART THREE

CHAPTER I
E was very humble, consequently very sensitive.
Nothing that he saw or heard was insignificant.
When he entered the sleeping-car he felt an im-
mediate calm in the presence of so many genteel people and
so much subdued green upholstery. He was nobody much,
but here he was, moving about as other people moved, and
perhaps like them waiting to have some momentous issue
adjudicated. When a tiny five-year-old girl came from the
section across the aisle, leaned her elbows upon his knee,
looked up into his face as if she detected sadness there, and
then asked in gentle confidence, "Won't you tell me who
you are?" he was for a moment filled with some strange
great belief. Before he died he might yet see sweeping
silvery vistas again.
He was still in this state of sensitivity the next day when
he walked out of the South Station. There was no odor in
any street of any other city in the world like that of Federal
Street, Boston-some precious blending of the saltiness of
sea air, the tang of fresh leather, and the aroma of spices
and roasting coffee. When he walked into the old Parker
House to register, he received the same kind of clear-
edged impression. How could these swaggering traveling
salesmen with cigars stuck too far round in their mouths
move so jauntily about just as if they were in some new
hotel filled with over-ornamented iron "woodwork," and
rattling with much steel?
A cocky black boy in smart livery carried his luggage
to his room on the second floor, back past the library
that Dickens used when he visited Boston, down two steps,
301
302
THE HILLIKIN
round a corner, up three steps, then to the right, then to

When he took the subway to Harvard Square, he grew

The cool odor of the subway! It was not unlike the odor
of fresh clay in the pot-shop. The tarry odor of Harvard
Square! It filled him with the memory of whatever little
joy he had experienced in college, and nothing of the pain.
What would he not give-ten years of his life, half of his
life-if he could be coming back carefree, buoyant, con-

The gates! He had walked through those gates himself

wide portfolio under his arm, and that probably was of no
worth. The thought still frightened him. But soon he

"Would it be possible for me to see Professor Tillinghast

Yes, Giles Dabney thought his errand important enough

He tramped about in the Yard and was less fearful. No-

At the thought, he felt the cold chill and then the hys-

He wandered over toward the great new library that had

304
THE HILLIKIN
studied the Corinthian columns and thought they might

It suddenly rained-a fresh, thunderous June shower-

He lingered about the Yard all afternoon. Then in the

The fresh, cool air suggested that the sea must not be
far away. The thin-bodied young elms that had been trans-
planted late in life to take the place of the old ones killed
by the moths dripped splattering heavy drops when the
wind stirred them, and suggested further freshness of life.
Young men they seemed very young-hurried in quiet,
civilized fashion along paths toward the Square, toward
their rooms, toward the library, toward social engagements
of every imaginable sort.
They seemed to possess much gentility. They had been
painstakingly brought up. Were they too civilized? Did
they suffer from mental anemia? Had they been too much
influenced by the Pekingese who constituted such a socially
important minority when he was in college? He liked
students with gusto who dreamed wild, magnificent dreams.
Still, just now, he might trade a little of the provincial basis
of gusto for some of their ease in the presence of the world.
Most of them had come from families where life had been
well rounded as long as they had lived, where a successful
THE HILLIKIN
305
and respectable career for the deserving had always been
taken for granted, where doubts about their past were few,
and therefore still fewer about their future. They had
never suffered from the too great awareness that one is a
hillikin. They had never suffered the experience of being
alone with a great uncertainty.
He sat on the high steps with his back comfortably against

ing peace that he made no effort to understand. No one
had yet answered for him the question planted ineradicably
in his mind by the jeering of Steel City. Yet he was at ease.
It was his alma mater that he had returned to, and she had
received him unperturbed. She was a strange old mother.
Her sons went from her doorstep with heads full of as many
different kinds of gifts from her as there were heads. Some
went burdened with much dead knowledge and many still-
born ideas. Some went restless with ferment and rebellions
and hungerings and aspirations and convictions and hatreds
and loves. She said, "Take what you will. The dead
accumulations are more obvious than the spirit that forever
struggles to free itself. But as you like." She stood with
a sublime indifference to what men found in her.
It soothed his fevered head and singing nerves to snuggle
up close beside such a mother. She heaped no ridicule upon
him. It was not her practice to heap ridicule upon any of
her children. If they wished, they might ridicule one an-
other-and often enough they did. But so far as she was
concerned, she bore with them. They might go their way.
They might fight whatever battles they liked or none at
all. They might come back to her honored or victorious
or shamed and defeated. She would be there when they
came.

and say that he was one of life's incapables.
For a moment he lost himself utterly in his great dread.
When he looked up Mickey's eyes were waiting, and he felt
in them a skepticism for his own fears that made him twist
a little in his chair and smile into her face ever so feebly.
"They are good plans," she assured him again. "I haven't
seen them, of course. But you see I've spent so much time
alone that I've discovered what kind of people may be ex-
pected to make good plans-or good anything else."
He was the least bit comforted. He still sat bent over
and looked downward abstractedly, but a glint of confidence
showed in his face.
He moved as if to go.
"Listen," Mickey commanded him. "You must promise
me one thing. Will you?"
"What?"
"That whatever he says, you'll bring all your sketches
and drawings and things and let me see them-to-night, be-
fore you leave. I want to see them. One of my class-
mates married a city planner. But he is just starting—isn't
half so far along as you are."
318
THE HILLIKIN
She reached for her cane and stood up to accompany him
to the door.
"You see," she said, when she noticed that he could not
keep from watching her efforts, "I can walk."
She kicked along across the floor slowly, and Giles could
think of nothing appropriate to say.
"So," she said at the door, "I'll see you again this eve-
ning." She spoke hearteningly.
He smiled the smile of a boy who has just agreed to stop
crying.
"Good-by!" he said.
"But you haven't promised yet."
"Oh, I'll try to."
"But you must. I want to see them. So hurry along
and have it over with as soon as possible."
All the way down the street he marveled. What kind of
an experience was this he had wandered into? She just
sat there and comprehended everything without being told!
It had been less an experience than a fantastic dream. Yet
it was real.
Between darting flashes of fear he was still marveling
when he reached Professor Tillinghast's office.
"Come in!" the professor called, cheerily but still some-
what non-committally.
"Sit down."
The portfolio was lying unopened on Professor Tilling-
hast's wide desk. He sat for a moment without saying a
word, without making a movement. At last he lifted his
half-open hand in a doubtful little gesture to the top of
the desk and raised his fingers and let them fall in a manner
that expressed still more bewilderment.
"You say you have not been in Steel City for three or
four months?"
"No."
THE HILLIKIN
319
"And you have not been in touch with anyone there—
recently, I mean?"
"No. The only news of any kind I've had for weeks was
a bundle of newspaper clippings."
The professor rapped his hand lightly on the desk a half-
dozen times while he reflected.
"Well, I think I'll just tell you the whole story, then."
He turned squarely to Giles as if in fact it was not his
intention to conceal anything.
"It was like this. A committee of citizens from Steel
City came on here recently to tell me their woes. They
had blood in their eyes. They meant to bring something
to pass. I was busy, but they insisted that I go back with
them and look things over-in a general way. I hadn't the
time; but I was through with my examinations, which
chanced to fall early this year, so I went. I've been out
there for a week.
"Well, the upshot of it was that they asked me to take
leave of absence next year-and I am entitled to a year-
and prepare comprehensive plans for the future develop-
ment of the city. I scarcely took the matter seriously at
first. It was going to mean a lot of work, and a man on
leave ought to relax a little instead of loading up with some-
thing heavier. But they guaranteed me more money than
I'll ever receive for teaching a year if I live to be a million,
I suppose, and they stood ready to pay half of it in advance.
They promised me absolute backing, and all the assistants
and office help I might need. I could not keep from seeing
that it was going to be a mighty interesting chore for the
man who undertook it.
"I wanted to think it over, although I was favorably in-
clined before I left. I told them so. I promised them that
either I'd do it myself or find a man who could do the job
better than I could.
"By the time I reached home this morning I had decided
320
THE HILLIKIN
to accept provided Harvard offered no objection to the
arrangement. You can see, then, how your walking in here
with plans of your own almost before I was in the office
somewhat puzzled me."
He did not know the hell that Giles was suffering. He
smiled, and reflected a moment.
"I scarcely knew whether I ought to look at your plans
if I meant to accept the commission myself. You may have
noticed that I was playing a little for time this morning
when you were here. But I decided. I half decided before
you left. Well, I shut myself up in here, told my secretary
to fight off all intruders, and went to work. I finished not
ten minutes ago. Didn't so much as go out for lunch."
He smiled. "You see I wanted to learn without delay
whether what you had done was any good."
He looked away. Then he looked back into Giles's face
the least bit maliciously.
"Now what I'd like to know, and what I want you to
tell me honestly is this: Did you think the plans were much
good yourself? Just as a matter of history."
Giles tried to make his voice firm. "Oh, the truth is,
I've thought everything. When I was making them I
thought they might be pretty good. But since then so many
things have happened that I've not been at all sure—”
The professor chuckled; he beamed; in every other pos-
sible manner he expressed content, as if the plans were his.
"It's the finest piece of work I've ever seen on paper.
If I'd work on their city for ten years, I could never do
so well. You had something to carry to the job that I could
never have developed: the right combination of experience
in the back of your head; the right proportioning of Harvard,
let us say, and-where was it, Wiggam's Glory? You didn't
draw too heavily on the wisdom of the ages, as I might well
enough have done. You had the requisite audacity."
He lifted up the cover of the portfolio contentedly and
THE HILLIKIN
321
let it fall-a half-dozen times or more. "Their excellence
is simply marvelous."
Giles trembled with the greatest happiness he had ever
known. Professor Tillinghast would take him in as an asso-
ciate on the commission.
But the professor had something else in mind. "Now
here is what I have decided to do. I shall send the com-
mittee a telegram and say that, everything considered, I
cannot undertake the enterprise, but that I have already
found the man who will do a better job."
He broke off abruptly and asked: "Of course you came
on for commencement; that is, you'll be here for a few
days?"
Giles confessed that he had not had commencement in
mind when he came, and that his schedule for the next few
days was a little vague.
"Yes, you must stay. I mean to tell them that the man
is here, and that he must be engaged at once. You see, they
must come on, so that I can help them to see what they've
got. They came here to me in the first place, they said,
because they wanted to get far enough away from Steel City
to escape all local prejudices. I wish to make it clear to
them that they have. It will hurt nothing, anyhow, to bring
them on and have them receive the announcement with a
little burning of incense.
"But aren't you hungry? You look hungry. And I am
ready for some food myself-or will be as soon as we walk
over to the Square and write the telegram. I'll ring up my
wife and tell her I'll not be there-which will be nothing
new for her—and we'll go over to the Club and begin early
and eat as long as we like. Then we can come back here.
I want to talk over the points that I think are exceptionally
good, and when we have done that we can visit a little and
get acquainted again.”

CHAPTER III
T was ten o'clock when Professor Tillinghast and Giles
Dabney came out into the evening tranquillity of the
Yard. Giles was letting Professor Tillinghast do all
the talking. Often, in the isolation of Mrs. Dibblee's top
floor, he had tried to imagine what it would be like to be
accepted among the discriminating. But never had his
lively, provincial imagination been able to create anything
half so sweet as the actual sound of Professor Tillinghast's
voice.
When they parted for the night Giles turned away rap-
turously without thought of where he might be going. He
required no immediate destination. He only wished to walk
wherever he could keep within the brightness that enveloped
him, wherever he could savor the eternity of each moment.
He had wanted to believe in himself. And in his greatest
agony in Steel City some tiny spark of him-the spark, he
now knew, that had eventually guided him back to Professor
Tillinghast never allowed itself to be completely smoth-
ered. That spark, he had wanted always to believe, was
the essential part of himself. But between the wildest,
most confident wanting to believe and the joy of actually
knowing that one is justified in believing, there was an
unimaginable distance. Now he believed. His own inner
self had remained, after all, the infallible self-disciplining
oracle.
With his blood pressing warmly through him, every flash-
ing thought in his mind was significant, every object an
object of interest. He stopped to look at one of the white
wooden fountains, which resounded when he kicked it, like
322
THE HILLIKIN
323
a box factory. He looked at the electric wiring that hung
low overhead awaiting the Japanese lanterns in the morn-
ing; at the newly transplanted trees themselves and their
guy-wire supports, at the paths and the buildings. All these
he had seen only three hours before, but at the moment they
had in their familiar look a strange, stark freshness of out-
line as if he were looking at them for the first time.
They were glorious old buildings! He strode about and
looked at them affectionately. Hollis and Stoughton were
supposed to be alike, but Hollis was the better of the two.
Stoughton was only some builder's imitation of Hollis-of
that he felt sure. The Adamses and the Emersons and a
few of the other great ones had once roomed up there. He
tried to see them moving about. He tried to imagine Emer-
son gingerly breaking the ice in the wash basin when the
mornings were cold, and John Quincy Adams contemplating
a bath on like days without such incidental accessories as a
tub or a shower. But somehow they both had managed to
get along.
He might as well be making his way down to the Square
and over to town. He was still too enraptured for sleep,
but he could walk some more after he got over into the
city, or go up and spend some quieting hours in the hotel
library, and get himself ready for sleep sooner or later. He
was in no mood to waste unnecessary hours in unconscious-
ness.
Mickey! He should have called her up to say that he
was not coming!
The hurt of his having forgotten did not penetrate very
far into his ecstasy. Still he felt regret. She had been sit-
ting there all evening, there in the library, waiting for him
to come. Perhaps she had decided that he was not coming;
that the verdict of Professor Tillinghast had been unfavor-
able, and that he had no wish to come. Or she might be
sitting there waiting still.

THE HILLIKIN

Professor Tillinghast, Giles insisted, had not said much

Mickey amplified each of his simple statements until it

326
THE HILLIKIN
asked so many questions that soon he was giving her a full

He sat in a low, deep chair that thrust his knees high

enabled them to operate without showering all their dirt

civic center that was to be the city's testimony to its own
comeliness of spirit and power of self-discipline.
So long had he been alone in the depths that now when

in his own mind. She was helping him to catch the fuller
significance of the very thing he himself had already created!
The room was filled with the warm ambience that had en-

additional.
"And nobody helped you very much to do it," she com-

"No. Nobody. But how did you know that?"
"Oh, I can see that it all came out of the quiet some-
where."
He sat wondrously amazed.
"Didn't it? Didn't you work at it a long time without

"Well, yes; that is, I did work at it a good while."
"Up in somebody's attic or up in the top of some sky-

"Something like that, yes."

THE HILLIKIN
327
nothing else while people rushed by without thinking of it

Giles glanced up and saw her sitting there in her wing

become easy and natural. Yes, he had to admit that he
had been hounded more or less by those who never thought,
or by those who thought only of themselves.
He was still near the terrifying experience. It was com-
forting to express it to somebody and have support in de-
nouncing its absurd hideousness. So he told her about his
encounter with the newspapers-a subdued recital, it must

the beginnings of his first doubts; and then how he could
see it clearly enough now-the doubts grew and multiplied
until they assumed a more terrifying aspect than death;
how everywhere he went, the disdainful, jeering, economi-
cally entrenched were ever in the foreground ahead of him,
and the bland, uncomprehending herd that was being rapidly
enlarged by the processes of the city's life was ever in the
background; how he had gone to the hills for rest and
support, only to find the city's wreckage there; how,
wherever he turned, he was confronted by the city until he
imagined he was on the verge of madness.
They sat in utter self-forgetfulness-he a great lounging
outlander doubled over intently in his low chair, with his
eyes much of the time directed toward the floor, and she a
chastened young woman whose face was bright with a can-
dent warmth, and whose entire person expressed animation
save where her benumbed feet seemed to hold her like a
plant to the earth.
At last she said as if she had long ago made the discovery,
"They have never confronted the real. Or, if they have,
328
THE HILLIKIN
they have refused to look at it. They can't trust themselves
in the quiet-that is the trouble. They are afraid to rely
upon decisions they make in their own solitariness of spirit.
The only way they have of feeling sure that they must be
right is in doing what they see everybody else doing."
They sat for a time. The circle of Mickey's psychic life
included Giles, enveloped him. He was at ease. He felt
the subtle atmosphere of the room and thought he under-

slightly crooked position of the portrait of the queer old

"You have discovered," she asked eventually without
seeming to intrude, "haven't you, how wonderful every-
thing is when you keep in the quiet long enough?"
"How terrible also, sometimes!"
"That is because you yet fear somebody. That is because
you have not yet been enough alone. When you are enough
alone, there is nobody to fear. Then you begin to discover
your own resources, how life has a way of taking care of
itself if you give it a chance, and how impossible it is for
anybody else ever to do you injury.
"Everybody talked about my great misfortune. When-
ever I was in their minds, they talked of nothing else. They
took so much pity on me that I almost took pity on my-
self.
But soon I saw that that would never do; and as
soon as I saw that, I discovered I was not half so badly
off as anybody thought. It is worth the price of one pair of
feet to have learned how to deal with the fears that most
people I know are pursued by. They fear most of all what
THE HILLIKIN
329
other people will think. These people whose opinions they
fear do not think any more than they do themselves; but

"When the family go off to some bridge party or dance

going because of my feet. But I don't go because I prefer
to come in here. I like to.
I know why I come. I come
in here to wait. And then I begin to live.
"Isn't it marvelous to live? I don't mean just to breathe
fresh air and eat good food and sleep in a comfortable bed
and get up and look out the next morning on blooming
flowers or anything of that kind; but to have a mental life—
to feel one's spirit always tugging away, pulling one along?
Can you imagine anything I could get by going somewhere
that I would accept in a trade for that?
"When I sit here long enough, the books in the shelves
all come to life. The people who wrote them and the people
who are in them peer round the corners of the covers and

They bow to one another in the costumes of their different
centuries, or their different personal choices, and engage in
all sorts of pleasantries and philosophizings. Don't you
think it would be great fun to sit and listen to Molière and
Bernard Shaw pacing back and forth across the room dis-
cussing the theater? I wanted to hear more of Molière's
voice, but he had to stop and cough at times, and Shaw
seized upon this opportunity to do most of the talking. I
liked Chaucer and Marie Antoinette, too, when they went
bowing and curtsying by. I couldn't understand what they
were saying, but they were saying it with a lot of adorn-
ment. And Queen Victoria one night came waddling along
and met Henri de Navarre-right over there by the table.
Can you imagine their conversation? She did not care for
that long, shiny, evil-looking feather that adorned the side
330
THE HILLIKIN
of his hat. And on another night when Emily Dickinson
was scampering across under the elms there by the fireplace
to see Sister Sue, she encountered Walt Whitman strolling
around with no coat on and his wide-brimmed hat pushed
quite back on his white head as if he were about to break
forth into a lusty pagan song of himself. Emily only looked
at him and turned her head and hurried on. But when she
got over to Sister Sue's she made more interesting remarks
about his ego than any he ever published in his books.
After Whistler and Mark Twain had spent an evening to-
gether, it took a whole week to get the atmosphere quiet
again. Oh, I have perfect revels!
"And once I saw Christina Rossetti and her mother go
by quite gravely, and I could scarcely tell one from the
other; and once, Robert Louis Stevenson, astride his little
donkey Modestine, who kicked and rattled the gravels with
her tiny hard hoofs, and drowsed along while he talked to
her and to the world at large; and once, Tiny Tim and the
Hunchback of Notre Dame and Jane Eyre; and once, Tess
of the D'Urbervilles, all by herself.
"But the people who come out of the books are only a
part of it. If you keep quiet long enough, you discover
your attachments. You begin to know which friends you
love, when you have deliberately kept away from them for
a time. And it is not just people, either; it is everything.
The things of the earth just come up to me through my
feet and remind me that, after all, I am only a part of them;
that I have sprung from the universe and therefore ought to
be at home in it. That is the greatest discovery of all. That
is what lets you know that you are secure."
Giles Dabney listened and wondered. Must one be tor-
tured yet go on living, having one's feet nailed irremediably
to the earth, in order to understand what is the birthright
of every son and daughter of man?
The family came in, and by their chatter and bustle, by
THE HILLIKIN
331
their mere presence, dispelled the magic hour and reminded

Down in the subway at Harvard Square while he waited

and studied the construction of the tunnel. The faint rumble
in the distance and then the roar and screech of the train
as it took the sharp curve at the very entrance to the station
were satisfying sounds. That was how things were rush-
ing along now. He must speed the day to an end, and get
ready for another and another and another.
CHAPTER IV
UMBLY he heard-or felt-the telephone ringing.
N
He was not quite certain whether he was in France
or in Steel City. But Professor Tillinghast's voice
quickly dispelled all uncertainty.
"Just had their telegram. They were quite jolted, I
guess; not certain they can get here, right away, at least.
If they can, they'll let me know some time yet to-day."
He felt himself sinking through the side of the bed where
he sat with the telephone in his hands.
What a fool he had been to let himself get excited before
he knew for a dead certainty that everything was settled!
What a greater fool he had been to talk with anybody until
the committee had signed a contract! The time when he
could put his plans into stone and steel and concrete and
green grasses might yet never come. Yesterday faded as if
it had never been.
Was there no strong support to which he could cling?
Could he find no justification for his day of rejoicing?
Why, to be sure! He had plenty to hold him up. They
were good plans. Professor Tillinghast had said so. Wasn't
that something? Wasn't that enough?
He smiled profoundly and felt his fear giving way. They
were good plans. That could not be gainsaid. That he
now knew. He ought to be content with that, even if the
committee never came.
If Steel City did not care to be made over-well, there
were plenty of other cities. If he could make one set of
plans that Professor Tillinghast called exceptional, he could
make another. He had learned how.
332
THE HILLIKIN
333
He sat on the edge of the bed and relived those glowing
hours in Professor Tillinghast's office. "Their excellence is
simply marvelous!"
Who cared what the committee decided to do? He al-
most hoped they would not come. Some day when Steel
City had grown to be the laughing-stock of every other city
in the country, he would publish his treatise on city plan-
ning and use his plans for Steel City as a model, so that
anybody who wished could see just who had been stupid.
He would take the plans to Mickey as he had promised.
She would see that they possessed merit. If a quarter-
million or a half-million or a million people from Steel City,
ten millions from anywhere on the face of the earth, should
march by now and with jeering faces cry out to him that
they were crazy plans, he would only smile and let them
cry out till they had had their fill.
He reached over and looked at his watch. Oh, it was
noon, was it? Well, he felt as if he had slept a good long
time. He would dress, and snatch a bite somewhere, and
get over to Cambridge and show Mickey the plans.
He decided that it would be pleasanter to eat the bite in
Cambridge. He wished to know what a Class Day was like
when one did not have to sling hash.
He had no trouble in learning. He sat for a half-hour
before a waiter came to take his order, and then another
half-hour before the food arrived. Yes, it was Class Day.
He secured his ticket and walked into the Yard through
the gate which bears the inscription: "Enter to grow in
wisdom." Then he skirted the crowd and hurried to Pro-
fessor Tillinghast's office. Just before he reached the steps
of the building, Professor Tillinghast came alongside.
Giles explained that he wished to show the plans to some
one.
"Any of my colleagues?"
"No, just a personal friend. She doesn't get about quite
334
THE HILLIKIN
so easily as the rest of us, and I promised to bring the
plans to her."
"Oh, so that's it! Well, just so you get them back before
dinner time. You see, I've invited two or three of my col-
leagues and two or three of the men who are receiving
degrees to come to the Club to meet you and to see the
plans-if you don't mind. I tried to reach you nearly an
hour ago, but had no luck."
A telegram hung limply in the letter slot of the door.
Professor Tillinghast slipped it out, unlocked the door,
swung it open for Giles to enter, and then tore the colored
envelope open and read.
"Bully!" he said, and handed the half-folded sheet to
Giles.
"Will arrive at 9.30 Wednesday morning," it read.
So that was settled.
Giles took his portfolio and hurried through the throng
that was filling the Yard-past one bandstand that emitted
a snatch of cornet solo, past another that roared out "Up
the Street," past one of the fountains that played in ele-
phantine fashion, past thousands and other thousands who
stood and talked together or bowed to each other in intro-
ductions, or sat on the varnished folding chairs and listened
and looked. He had felt insignificant at the sight of these
crowds when he was a student. It had not been possible for
him to hold off an inexplicable sense of deficiency when he
had to work in a restaurant while important people in
flannels and holiday dresses took Class Day as a matter of
course in the routine of their exalted lives. But this after-
noon he felt not at all insignificant. How many of them,
with all their nonchalance, had ever dreamed a great dream?
He sat the afternoon through with Mickey and explained
details or listened to her exclamations of approval and
understanding, or watched her intent, lighted face as she
turned back from time to time to look at something again.
THE HILLIKIN
335
"Oh, Hillikin," she asked when at last he was saying some-
thing about having to go or keep Professor Tillinghast wait-
ing, "do women ever become city planners?"
Giles saw no reason why not. The thinking that city
planning required might be done by anybody who could
think; the imagining might be done by anybody who had
imagination; and the drafting could be done by anybody
who could draw.
She reached for her cane and lifted herself up to accom-
pany him to the door. He hoped she would take his arm
as a matter of course, and she did.
He never had felt so sturdy. He wanted her to pull
heavily on his arm and see how he was strong enough to sup-
port a dozen who had to walk with canes. Her face, he knew,
as she was obliged to concentrate somewhat on the busi-
ness of kicking her braced foot along, was the loveliest face
he had ever seen. And at the door when she pressed his
hand fervidly like a very old acquaintance and said in
quiet rapture, yet with a trace of resignation in her voice,
that she had always known he was destined to do fine
things and that he must never think of coming to Cam-
bridge without bringing to her a record of everything he
had done to date, he wanted to throw his arms around her
and tell her that he would rather sit in that rambling old
New England house and report to her, and hear her speak
beautifully and wisely, than go to all the dinners that all
the Professor Tillinghasts in the world might ever arrange
in his honor. But instead he promised simply to come,
thanked her for doing so much to help him out of the depths,
and hurried away.
His head was so full of memories of the afternoon that
he had not yet freed himself from them when Professor Til-
linghast was presenting his younger colleagues and his stu-
dents, and expressing the belief that they might as well go
on in and do their talking while they ate.

THE HILLIKIN
When the evening was over and the men had said their

And this committee probably will wish to cross-examine you

The next morning Professor Tillinghast immediately pro-

to receive the committee alone. We'll go over the matter

after I've prepared their minds so that they can think of

Giles leafed through journals on architecture and city

at the designs made by men who were to receive degrees.
The committee's minds seemed difficult to prepare.
Just before noon Professor Tillinghast came out and told

The man at the end of the table nearest the door rose

"Why-hello there, Rathman. Are you on this com-

THE HILLIKIN
337
When everybody was seated, Mr. Hamill, a sturdy, some-

well guess. They were favorably disposed, but they wished

"We have no axes to grind, Mr. Dabney—that has been
the curse of our city government for years and we do
not mean to have any entanglements with anybody who has
-that is, if we know it. So far as the plans themselves
are concerned, we must rely in large measure upon pro-
fessional counsel. We'd have to do that altogether if we
were to engage somebody who had not yet made plans. I
am an engineer and Mr. Barnhart is an architect, and just
from glancing through some of your conception here, we
think your idea is perfectly bully. So that part of the case
may be regarded as somewhat settled.
"But this is what we want to know: Just what relations

put two and two together and made five and decided that

"None whatever!"

THE HILLIKIN
story of his new Steel City. Naturally I have not seen

After Mrs. Frederick, who was on the board of education

It was such a pleasant luncheon, and it induced so many
inquiries which logically belonged in the afternoon session

Tillinghast and accept the man he named. They had wished

In fact, they felt that his activity in behalf of the city
quite justified them in saying that he had been their adviser.
Since he was to have received one-half of his fee when
he had a sketch of his general plan ready, it seemed only
reasonable that since Giles Dabney had already done so
much in such completeness of detail, he should receive half
of his fee at once.
Several thousand dollars now, yes; that was reasonable
—a little working capital, so to speak; and the rest of the
initial amount as soon as the members of the committee
deemed the plans as complete as they could be made before
work was actually begun. Beyond that, of course, the com-
mittee could not go. It seemed only reasonable to assume,
however, that the municipality would hardly let the matter
drop after it had advanced the preliminary sum to a com-
mittee that had been appointed to serve for three years.
Giles found it possible to suppress his joy after the meet-

339
sweltering interior, Giles Dabney and Rathman decided to

They knocked. They waited. They knocked again, and

They ate some dinner and walked in the Yard and re-

At two minutes past nine they were back.
"You are late!" he declared when he came to the door.

"No, sir, not on your life! Something designed to re-

They all went to the hotel together. It took them so long

CHAPTER V
EE that lighthouse in the sun!" the quiet engineer
exclaimed with the enthusiasm of a shy boy. "By
Jove, how she shows up! I know the man who
built that. He's a great fellow."
Giles gazed at the white spot miles out at the head of a
narrow point of land where the low sun accentuated any-
thing that possessed height. Then he let his eyes follow
the shore line back toward the speeding train they traveled
on, and saw the houses becoming distinct in detail and the
gulls low over the water assuming almost life size.
It was pleasant to sit with a man who said little. For
Giles, as the day drew to an end, was troubled with a strange
regret, a progressive nostalgia, that was allayed somewhat
by undisturbed contemplation of the sea.
He almost wished he had not come away from Cambridge
just yet. He wanted to go on living those two or three days
of triumph. Still, it was probably just as well that he had
come away as he had. He knew how dead the Yard could
be just after the commencement festivities were over. He
would have to content himself with those days in retrospect.
It would be something to keep in touch with Professor
Tillinghast, as he would have to do.
The next day when the quiet engineer took him to see
the tunnel, Giles was fascinated. Yet not even his inter-
est in the tunnel could sweep his spirit clean of melancholy.
A tunnel was too cold, too inhuman, to stir his imagination
as he wanted it to be stirred. He was seeing much that
would be useful to him some day in Steel City, without
doubt. But his mind only received individual impressions.
It did nothing with them after it had them.

THE HILLIKIN
In the afternoon they went to the top of a tower build-
ing to get a bird's-eye view of some of the city's colossal
projects in planning. The sight of the limitless agglomera-
tion which completely overshadowed the individuals who had
constructed it and who supposedly made it serve their in-

He imagined that the quiet engineer noticed his dejection.
He must talk himself into a happier frame of spirit.
"But New York, you see, is not Steel City. New York is
more diversified, hence less dependent—that is, better able
to look out for itself, to make its own way."
The quiet engineer smiled. "More diversified, yes; but
not less dependent. In fact, we are more dependent than
any of the others-for the simple reason that the teakettle
we stew in is so much larger. It is more difficult for any-
body to learn anything. Everybody says, 'Just look at us.
Aren't we the biggest thing you ever saw? There is nothing
like us.
At least we never saw it.' And of course that
proves that they know more than anybody else."
He made a sweeping gesture that included the harbor,
the railway approaches through New Jersey, through the
Hudson valley, through the hills of New England.
"No, we have to live by desperate importation, by artificial
respiration, just the same as any other city. We import
editors and columnists and staff reporters and architects
and sculptors and musicians and painters and inventors and
actors and playwrights and musicians and surgeons and
ministers and priests and—and engineers. So that's about
everything, you see, except politicians—and a good many
of them come from a certain well-known island on the other
side of the Atlantic."
"But you yourself—you are not an importation."
"Of course I am. My father was a modest toiler in Puri-
tan New England.
"Oh, yes, the only way we can live is by having plenty
THE HILLIKIN
345
of other places that people want frightfully to get away

and Oshkosh, Wisconsin, and Ladoga, Indiana, and Sterling,

Yet neither Giles's own effort at conversation nor the
quiet engineer's effort at humor was enough to dispel Giles's
strange feeling that the best of everything was past. Now
that he was at last to savor the great days about which he
had so long dreamed, they were turning insipid.
In the evening he remembered that he might be suffering
from weariness. Did not a person let down a little after
so long a period of uncertainty? He would go early to bed.
But he had slept only an hour when the voice of the city
far below awakened him. He got up, pulled a comfortable
chair over in front of the window, slumped down into it,
and looked idly at the spectacle the greatest collection on
earth of man's glittering playhouses and ante-mortem obe-
lisks. The sight of the scintillating maze, however, was no
more effective than anything else had been in dislodging
from his mind the feeling that whatever days might be
ahead for him, there would be nothing among them to
match the four or five he had just lived. They had been
keyed so high, they had been brought to an end in such
full harmony, that it hurt merely to think of them as
something in the past. Nor could he think without pain
of the simplicity of mind with which he had gone to Pro-
fessor Tillinghast-or with which he had confessed to
Mickey the great uncertainty that had made life hell. He
could never be like that again. Only three or four days
had passed, yet if the same situation were now to confront
him, he probably would try to be a little more self-contained.
346
THE HILLIKIN
Still he was glad he had been ingenuous. He had felt
lost, and he did not regret having been honest about the
matter, either to Professor Tillinghast or to Mickey. When
there was no concealment of any kind, there was no preju-
dice.
And had his mood not been somewhat that of the humbled
approaching a confessional, Mickey might not have said
more than a few ordinary cheerful words. And not to have
heard Mickey speak of her own resources in fantasy and
reflection, and of his hurt and his achieved dream, would
have left his experience in Cambridge unfinished and out of
balance.
"Mickey!"
He pronounced the name to himself and almost wished
he had not done so. In his present mood, it seemed sacri-
lege to label such a brave, chastened creature with such a
name. Or did it? It depended, he supposed, on how you
thought of her.
Just how he thought of her he could hardly say. He had
known her in so many stages of her life.
He sat there before the window in the tower of the hotel
completely oblivious of all that was before his eyes. He
saw Mickey as a spindle-legged schoolgirl exulting in sport
and irreverence. He saw her as a rebellious youngster who
lived only to get even with her older sisters. He saw her
as the painted young miss who wanted to outdo Mrs.
Aldrich by going to the commencement exercises, and who
had said, “Oh, Hillikin, don't you want Aggie? She
and Perce have had an awful bust-up." He saw all of
these Mickeys very, very clearly. Yet they served only
as a composite in modifying somewhat the image of the
beauteous, thoughtful woman who sat alone in the library
and waited for the people to come from within the book
covers and walk together in her unobserved presence, or who
reflected and thereby discovered her many resources.
THE HILLIKIN
347
Fate had broken her a bit-to her hurt, but not to
her disadvantage. If she had been treated less sternly, she
might have been only another nicely smoothed-off college
girl who early surrendered the joy of thinking, married a
man of good family, and settled down to the steady occu-
pation of wondering if her marriage had been a success.
But through suffering she had become not only resourceful
but beautiful. Was that the way human beings were made
into something better than average? Well, it was the way
Mickey had been made into something better than average
-very much better.
He tried to think of the attitude of the young men of
her generation toward her. They had said, "Isn't she
charming? Isn't she wonderful? She is the loveliest woman
I have ever met." When she had stood up with the aid
of her cane they had glanced at her alluring body from
head to foot and had said, "What a pity that she has to be
hampered like that for life!" Then they had married girls
who danced, and were active in a thousand unimportant
social affairs, and were not meaning to have their dulcet
lives upset by the contemplation of anything that was
fundamental.
Eventually he tried sleeping again, but without great
success. The next morning he went down late to the break-
fast room on the sixteenth floor. He ate deliberately and
watched jaded women and flabby men with heavy rings
under their eyes come in and eat as if life were too great a
burden to be bothered with much longer. After he had
wearied of watching them he tried to piece together the
program he had made for the day-developments in city
planning that the quiet engineer thought he might care to
go into a little further while he was at hand.
There was no reason why a woman could not be a city
planner if she wished. But some women ought not to be—
Mickey, for instance. She ought to write imaginary con-
348
THE HILLIKIN
versations. She ought to write about the spiritual resources
of those whose feet are a hindrance, and make every woman
with good feet ashamed of herself. She ought to tell the
fearful to be less afraid.
Developments in city planning did not interest him to-
day. It made no difference, he thought, whether he went
out to see anything or not. With plenty of money in his
pocket, as he soon would have, he could come to New York
whenever he chose.
He could go back to Cambridge sometimes, too—just run
over and see Professor Tillinghast and Professor MacLaurin
and a few others, and enjoy the quiet of the Yard in the
evening. When he came to New York he could run on over
to Boston and Cambridge.
He welcomed a wild resolution. He was going back to
Cambridge to-day. The quiet engineer had baited him away
early. There must be other matters that he ought to talk
over with Professor Tillinghast. Just what they were, he
did not feel sure. But he would go up to his room and
call Mickey and tell her that he meant to be back in town
for a few hours and might drop in for a few minutes.
Would that be the best means of letting her know? She
might say, "I am just as sorry as I can be, Hillikin, but we
are leaving for Maine early in the morning and shan't be
back till September."
It would be better to send her a telegram-with no op-
portunity for reply. That would be better. Then if she had
gone to Maine, he could run on down there or up, or over,
whichever it was. He could say, "I had to come back to
Boston, and since I have been living a little strenuously,
I thought two or three days-" Yes, he could say some-
thing like that, and give her no opportunity to forestall him.
"Address, Parker House." Just why he put that at the
end of his telegram he was not certain, since there was no
special reason why she should address him at all.

CHAPTER VI
E was on the Knickerbocker long before one o'clock,
the hour of its departure. When at last the train
started with a dull little thud and then later came
out into the open where he saw the city whirl by for the
better part of an hour, the problems of urban life troubled
him not in the least. When for two hours afterward he saw
high tide pushing into every inlet along the Connecticut
shore, he troubled even less about that.
Before the train reached Providence he was admitting to
himself quite openly that the only person he had in mind
seeing in Cambridge was Mickey.
He sat deep in his chair and reviewed the long procession
of girls and women who had passed his way in the course
of a dozen years. Certain of them had attracted his notice.
Certain of them had set him tempestuously ablaze. But
after they had been out of his sight for a time, after his
great dream had once again claimed him, they faded until
they were too uninteresting even to be recalled to mind.
They had never lifted him to any sublime ecstasy.
But Mickey! He wanted only to sit and enjoy the rap-
ture of her total beauteousness. He was not interested in
cataloguing the individual qualities that constituted her.
Yet forthwith he thought of her individual qualities one by
one. And he was sure they were what he had always hoped
to see in harmonious combination in some one woman, but
had never found.
As the train sped through the open country north of
Providence and then crashed into the outskirts of Boston,
his old great doubt, now completely divorced from his
349
350
THE HILLIKIN
dream of Steel City, and therefore free to roam at will
through his mind and attach itself to anything approach-
able, crept up close to his new dream.
"But you possess so few of the facts," it whispered.
"How do you know that she is not about to marry some
other man whom she loves profoundly? Perhaps when you
imagined that the men of her family's social set were inter-
ested in her only with a pitying interest, you were but
pleasing your own ego. Perhaps her interest in you was
but that of a much-beloved fiancée, who because of her own
great happiness was full of sympathy for anyone less for-
tunate.”
But he swung round in his chair with a swift movement
expressive of strong will, and brushed all floating doubts
aside. It made no difference if she were going to marry
some other man the next day, he meant to see her and tell
her that she was the most beautiful woman he had ever
known, and that he always would be devoted to her with a
great devotion. He would have at least the satisfaction of
carrying with him for a lifetime the image of her while he
had told her.
At the hotel he cautioned the bellboy to be sure to get the
right bags and the wide portfolio, and then hurried to the
wicket to ask if there were a telegram for him.
The girl leafed carefully through the D's a second time.
"No, nothing; nothing whatever."
Up in his room he decided against telephoning. He had
developed faith in meeting people face to face. He would
have some clothes pressed, and eat some dinner, and then
go to Cambridge in person.
When he felt reasonably sure that he would not be too
early, he took a taxicab directly from the hotel. He wished
to cover the miles without delay.
The Charles River Basin looked austere and right. He
hoped nobody would ever undertake to improve it. Harvard
THE HILLIKIN
351
Square was even more deserted than he had thought it would

His heart leaped with bumping, warming pulsations when

was lighted from top to bottom. What could so many lights
mean?
He hurried circumspectly up the walk and pulled the sil-

"Is Miss Priscilla at home?" he asked the gray-haired

"Yes," she replied, with an inclination to smile at seeing

Soon Giles heard her returning from somewhere in the

with complete matter-of-factness, "You know, I believe,

But no one was in the room. He stood nervously for a
