that envelops a woman when she sees that the man before
her loves her. She looked up into his face comprehendingly
without uttering a word.
"I came back," he stammered ardently, and threw his
arms around her and drew her close.
"I could think of nothing but you, Mickey—and I had
to come back. I've waited all my life to see the woman I
loved, and I never saw her until I was here the other day
and saw you. I love you! I love you! I love you!"
He covered her lips, her cheeks, her eyes with kisses be-
fore she could offer protest.
At last she was able to say, "Oh, Hillikin, but you must
wait-wait till I can tell you."
This time she looked up into his face with a great pity.
He was frightened. She was going to tell him that he was
too late. Instead, she made him speechless with joy by
adding: "Don't you see, Hillikin, how I would always be a
burden to any man? You have not thought of that."
"Yes, I have! That is, I know what you mean." And
again he covered her face with kisses. "What difference do
you suppose that would make to me? You are the most
beautiful woman I have ever known, and it would be just
the same if you could not walk a single step. I'd carry
you!"
There was a noisy clattering of many small feet on the
piazza, and the voices of children.
"They'll have to be going in order to get the kiddies to
bed on time," Mickey explained, "and they are coming in
to say good-by to me."
Giles had to bow in introduction to everybody. Elizabeth
Ann's husband looked at him with mild contempt in his
friendliness. Aggie's husband treated him as if he were a
sturdy variant who might blunderingly do something im-
portant some day.
When the families were ready to go, and Mr. and Mrs.
THE HILLIKIN
353
Morrison had accompanied them to the door, Mickey said:
"We must go outside. I want you to see."
They walked at her slow cadence toward some chairs and
seats that were mottled by the moonlight. Giles imagined
that she clung to him with reliance, with tenderness. She
clutched his arm more firmly still as she sat down.
He was persistent. "If you were to try, Mickey, if you
were to begin right now, don't you think you could learn to
love me? I'm not much, but—”
"No," she answered, with just enough levity to startle
him, "I could not."
"Why?"
"Because I have always loved you. Only I never dared
let myself think of it. When I used to see you over in Mrs.
Aldrich's garden, I often said to myself, 'I'd like to marry
him when I am ready for a husband, but he will be the
husband of somebody else by that time.' And don't you
remember on your Commencement Day when I begged you
to marry Aggie? It was because I loved you myself-
though I don't know just why I made that suggestion. I
only know I was glad when the idea struck you as pre-
posterous. And when you were here the other day I loved
you more than ever, but, you see, long before that this other
obstacle had interposed itself."
"You mean to say, then, that you will go with me to
Steel City?"
"But I would be a great hindrance."
"No!" he protested. "No! Don't think of that. I never
shall. And you must not."
Her insistence that she would be a handicap moved him
to enlarge upon deficiencies of his own. "I am the one who
really ought to fear being a burden. You will have me on
your hands, and I have never lived much in your world-
in the intellectual crispness of Harvard Square. You are a
New Englander and I am from the hills."

"Yes, I know."
"And still you are not afraid?"
"No-that is, not of that."
"The whole problem of the Great Divide-East and West
-it doesn't frighten you?"
“No.”
She smiled brightly, then became serious. "I may as
well confess to you. I dared not think you would come
back, but I wanted you to. I pushed the thought out of my
mind, at least out of the front of my mind, because I feared
I should be too great a burden. When I first saw you this
evening, I thought you had come for some other reason-
that the committee had faltered, or something—but only
for an instant. So don't worry about the Great Divide.
I never was quite an orthodox New Englander, anyhow-
don't you remember? It might even be a relief, I've thought
sometimes, to get away from the scintillating intellectuality
of Harvard Square and live with people who toil with their
hard hands, and sweat, and suffer, and die a heroic death.”
"Sure you'll not get too much of the sweat and suffering?
It is one thing, you know, to look upon it as a spectacle, and
quite another to live with it, to participate in it, endlessly."
"I've thought that all out, too. I was not certain that
any man would come along and offer to bear such a burden
as I am. But often enough I've dreamed of just the life
I'd live if the choice were left to me."

THE HILLIKIN
355
"They have suffered?"
"Yes."
"Then if they love me and have suffered, we shall under-
stand each other."
He sketched to her the life they had been forced to live;
how they had transferred their own dreams to him and had
staked everything on keeping him out of the mines and
getting him away to some less hurtful existence.
She tried to visualize them; to see herself going up the
walk to be greeted by them in some country front yard
such as she had seen in Maine. The sight of herself going
so slowly while they hesitated to come to her touched her
with fear.
"But Hillikin! After all, are you certain? Have you
thought of everything-about me, I mean. I am just a left-
over, a 'second' that no man has wanted. I've never had
opportunity to be in the thick of things. I'm but a solitary
observer-mostly from my easy chair."
He clutched her close as if he meant to smother her be-
yond speech. "You mustn't, my sweet, my darling. Why,
I am the happiest man in the world." And he kissed her
and caressed her with the irresistible caresses that convince
a woman when words are inadequate.
They sat long without speech, without movement.
"You can't think of anything else deprecatory to say about
yourself?" he asked with the humor of one who knows he
has not lost.
"But Hillikin, the fact remains that I can't walk very
fast."
"Then I shan't have to worry about your running away!"
They started back toward the house very slowly. "You
see," she said, "it will be like this."
"Or this!" Giles cried exuberantly, and caught her
his arms and ran with her all the way to the piazza.
up
in
356
THE HILLIKIN
"Are you always going to be just a great big boy?" she
asked as he put her down.
"I don't know," he answered, out of breath. "Maybe I'll
grow up when I get married."
But he saw nothing save indefinite youth ahead, with
always a song in the world as there was now. The mature
experiences of his life-being a variant professor in a college,
fighting in a war, planning a city-now seemed only hollow
projections of his real self.
Yes, he was still young. As he walked down the street
that night knowing for a certainty that he was to see Mickey
again not only to-morrow, but on and on into the future
without imaginable limits, the song of youth was still with
him. The historic old houses along Brattle Street joined
in the singing. The college took it up in deep, harmonious
melody, like that of the Commencement Day psalm. And
the quiet river sang, and the lovers in the parkway along
the waterfront.
Over in the city, the late crowds still milling along the
streets seemed more pitiable than he had ever known them,
and more deserving of love.

CHAPTER I
AN you fight?" Mr. Hamill asked Giles Dabney
as soon as Giles reported in Steel City.
"Yes. Why?"
"It looks as if we'd have to."
"Opposition?" Giles asked casually. He felt equal to any
opposition that might develop.
"Something like that," Mr. Hamill answered in customary
understatement. Then he added, "Opposition plus lethargy.
A great combination."
"What does the opposition say is wrong?"
"Everything! Everything from start to finish! The
wrong people are on the committee; they engaged the wrong
city planner-I may as well be frank; it is the wrong time
to undertake the enterprise; it is going to cost too much
money; and it won't be what we want when we get it.
How's that for a start?"
"Not bad!"
"Well, we'll let them know they've been through some-
thing, anyhow."
He smiled as he fumbled with a brass skyscraper on his
desk. Giles studied his active, sturdy face, his closely
cropped head, his decisive posture, and thought that if there
was to be a fight, Mr. Hamill was the man to lead it.
"Of course they'll bring up all sorts of charges against
Barnhart and me. That was to be expected. We have
agreed, naturally, not to accept a commission of any kind
in engineering or architecture until after this election is over
in November. That, of course, won't satisfy the crooked;
they will only think that the two of us are preparing to
359
360
THE HILLIKIN
cash in when the election is over. But the honest will
understand, and we'll have to be content with that. And
I'll be left free with all of my time for the fight."
Giles Dabney enjoyed the warm sustenance of support,
even if it were the support of only a small minority.
"When do we begin-and where?"
"Right away; and with the real people always. They
never figure up so very many to the city block, but there is
always a low percentage of them sticking around—and you
can count on them. They will back us because they can
see at once that the idea is sound, and that our motives
are not low. After them, we'll begin on the opposition.
Some of those who oppose us because of their honest con-
victions will come over to our side as soon as we have made
our position clear enough. A few will not, for the simple
reason that their convictions are about two-thirds prejudice.
We'll have to forget them. That still leaves the great body
of the opposition untouched. The only way we can deal
with them is to show them up.
"We have already begun preparing a list, to be used as
occasion may require, of all the people who have axes to
grind. We are not going to deal in abstractions. We are
getting concrete instances of the way each one of them ex-
pects to grind his particular ax by keeping things as they
are.
"When we have these three movements well under way,
we'll have to begin on the apathetic. They are our biggest
job. It's hard to get people excited about some far-off idyllic
day, when the problem they face is how to get through to-
morrow morning without starving to death or freezing.
We'll have to show them how they will begin to derive prac-
tical benefits just as soon as the planning goes into effect."
The committee and the city planner constituted a board of
strategy that was more or less in perpetual session. Every
THE HILLIKIN
361
day some member was in the office early, every day some
other member was there till midnight or after.
One morning Mr. Hamill burst into the headquarters with
the energy of a bear. "Say," he asked Giles Dabney while
he mopped trickling beads of sweat from his stout face,
"whatever have you done to this man Wiswall up at the
college?"
"Why?"
"Why? Why, he thinks you are a long-haired, bloody-
eyed bolshevik. I had taken it for granted that I could
count on him-with a department of landscape architecture
in his own institution, and his idealism and all that-but I
ran into a stone wall. I wanted to go at him with a sledge
hammer, but I decided it might be better to see you first."
Giles tried to summarize President Wiswall's philosophy.
"He's just make-believe. He cares nothing for realities in
themselves; he only wants to get credit for having them."
"Just about what I decided. But this was the first time
I ever ran into him personally. I didn't care for him. I
soon felt that he was not one of the real people. Just the
same, he exercises a lot of influence, and we'd better deal
with him. I'll have to tell him that, after all, you conceived
the idea while you were in his faculty; that he must forget
incidentals, anyhow, and look only at larger aspects and rela-
tionships. Ha! Doesn't he love to mouth over those big
meaningless words: 'larger aspects,' 'relationships,' 'educa-
tional commitments.' What in God's name is an 'educa-
tional commitment' anyhow, putting somebody in prison?
Well, we'll point out to him that the larger aspects and
relationships are that the idea is a great idea and that it
originated in his institution-so to speak. That ought to
get him. He probably will keep still for a time-which won't
hurt us especially-and if he fears that we are going to
lose, he'll just keep on keeping still. But if he suspects
that the child is going to distinguish itself, he'll come for-
362
THE HILLIKIN
ward and announce that he is its father and that it affords
him the greatest pleasure imaginable to give it support."
Soon the real people began to emerge; soon also, the pig-
gish, who were more numerous and more powerful. It be-
came clear, too, which newspapers were certain to oppose
the committee's project, though it was not so certain which
ones would give it active support. It became clear, also,
which ministers of influence were to be counted as a part
of the opposition. They were calling frequently for the
singing of
"Faith of our fathers, holy faith,
We will be true to thee till death,"
and preaching valiantly about the all-importance of a rever-
ent spirit, and magnifying the pricelessness of our heritage.
"Say," Mr. Hamill wished to know on another day, "what
is the trouble with this man Brother Ellington in the big
red Presbyterian church?"
"Oh, that's easy," Giles explained. "My arterial boule-
vard to the northwest would cut off about thirty feet of the
back end of his church lot. It extends clear back to the
street that we must widen into the boulevard at that point."
"I see! I see!" All right, put him down with the rest
of them in the 'Axes-to-Grind' book. We may have to pub-
lish it in two volumes before we are through.
"And put the Star and the new Globe both down definitely
in the book of real people. I have the word: they are going
to work for us actively. They will print anything, too, that
we send them.”
The next morning he came early and was much elated.
"Say, can you write slogans? The two papers want to run
something at the top of the first page every day. We've
got to ginger things up for the apathetic."
He fished in his pocket. "Here is one that Barnhart and
THE HILLIKIN
363
I have evolved from the smoke commission's report for
last year: 'Do you know how many thousand tons of soot the
citizens of Steel City helped to bear up last year? Or what
the soot-fall for the year was for every man, woman, and
child in the city? One hundred and sixty-two pounds!
Don't you want a clean city? You can have it if you want
it. Vote yes for the permanent planning commission'—and
so forth and so forth."
Soon readers were looking for the slogans and were read-
ing: "Let's be an example of cleanness, not of dirt.” “Why
wash your window hangings to pieces and then always have
them dingy?" "Give the children a place to play. It's
better to build parks than reformatories." "Let's make the
river and the lake beautiful, as they once were."
But the Herald was crafty. It inquired first in a tone
of great fairness if it were generally understood that the
project would cost money. Then it asked if taxes were not
already high enough in Steel City. Finally it wished to
know who was going to profit by all this hullabaloo except
the committee and the city planner. The Herald, it was
pleased to say, had gone into the matter. It had canvassed
the substantial citizens of the city, and almost to a man they
were opposed to the committee's idea. More than that, it
had canvassed the working districts, and when the working-
men and their wives learned that the project would certainly
mean reduced wages, and possibly mean the removal of
many of the largest mills to other cities where business was
not throttled, they too were against it.
"I don't believe it!" Giles declared. "That is, I suppose
the Herald canvassers did scare them into saying something.
But I don't believe five per cent of the people who work
prefer to live in dirt, or roast in the accentuated heat of
crowded areas, or live without the fundamentals of privacy."
He spent days among the working classes. Then he pro-
posed that the Star and the Globe invite housewives and
364
THE HILLIKIN
•
children to send in letters which would set forth their reasons
for favoring the new Steel City.
The newspapers were flooded with letters of every degree
of illiteracy and every degree of enthusiasm.
"It would be nice," a Lithuanian woman wrote, "to have
a clean place where I could hang the clothes when I do
the washing. They are as dirty from soot before they're dry
as they were in the first place." "If the new housing plan
would give me a porch of my own," wrote another house-
wife, "I'd be in favor of it. I've always wanted one, but
had about given up." "It would be fine to have a nice cool
park right outside your door somewhere," argued an Irish
woman, "where you could go and rest a little in the evening
after you've put the supper things away." "Why not have
the river front cleaned up," an Italian workman wanted to
know, "and have concerts there on the soft green grass of
Sunday afternoons? I favor that."
"Piffle!" the Herald screamed. "The committee probably
arranged to have these letters written in return for special
favors promised. Why should an important question like
this be dragged down to the level of precinct politics?”—that
was what the Herald wanted to know.
The children's letters troubled the Herald, too. "I think
it would be great to have a park down where we live where
we could play and not be chased out by somebody all the
time," wrote one boy. "If we had a nice clean swimming
pool in the park, it wouldn't be so bad to get along without
a bathtub at home," wrote a little girl in careful, rigid pen-
manship. "I hope we can have one of the nice clean school-
houses with gravel to play on and with nice grass and
flowers out in front," still another eleven-year-old wrote,
"instead of the ugly old thing we're in now."
"Sentimentalism!" the Herald screamed in still taller
headlines. "The more or less self-appointed committee has
failed in sound reasoning and even in precinct politics; so
THE HILLIKIN
365
The
it has had to resort to mawkish sentimentalism."
Herald thought it was time to get down to brass tacks.
"Who," it wanted to know, "is better able to say what the
city's needs are, some Lithuanian housewife, some ten-or-
eleven-year-old schoolboy or schoolgirl, or the substantial
citizens of the town who naturally have always had the city's
interests at heart?"
Mr. Hamill read the Herald with great interest. "If they
will only keep on asking questions like that, we'll win this
election yet," he observed, and then sat and smiled. He
gave a brusque little laugh. "They're insulting all the
business men who favor the plan, as well as all the working-
men and their families."
Giles Dabney walked in the clouds. His idea was a good
one he could rest assured of that. And it had a chance to
win right in the very place where it had been despised.
The chance might not be a very good one, yet it was a
chance. But whether he won Steel City or not, he was no
longer alone.
One evening when he was ready to leave the office,
Gunning walked in. "I had to keep quiet as long as I
was a part of the Herald force. But the News has been
picking up wonderfully in the past few months, and has
offered me an increase. So now I can have my say, since
the publisher has told me he means to come out for your
plan in a few days."
Before the end of the week the News carried a front
page editorial announcement of the fact that Mr. Sylvain
Gunning, the distinguished critic, had left the Herald and
joined the News because he favored the committee's new
Steel City and wished not to feel throttled in any views he
might incidentally express. The News had guaranteed to
Mr. Gunning absolute freedom of opinion and speech.
Mr. Hamill walked into the office with the confident
stride of Napoleon. "You know a campaign is a strange
366
THE HILLIKIN
phenomenon. It goes just about so far, and then without
any new tangible elements in it, it begins to get into the
air like a fog or gossamer, and you just feel it. I can feel
sentiment growing in our favor. I heard what your man
Gunning said when he was in here. I suppose he did go
chiefly because of more money-but not altogether. He
began to feel just what I sense whenever I walk through
the streets. There'll be others pretty soon who are stirred
by motives high or low to come over to us."
He was still in this hopeful mood the next afternoon.
"About day after to-morrow I figure it would be safe to
send Mrs. Garwood out to interview the good Dr. Wiswall.
She'd better play up that park and natural amphitheater
over back of the campus-call it the Grove of Helicon or
the Shades of Academe, or something like that. And let
him know that it won't cost money-everything's there if
you just don't touch it and spoil it."
The Star carried the interview. President Wiswall had
been deeply impressed by the high-mindedness of the com-
mittee in choosing a city planner. They had chosen what
you might almost call one of Steel City's own boys. Not
enough people, President Wiswall felt, had remembered that
fact. He himself took no little pride in the matter per-
sonally, since it was he who had brought the present city
planner to Steel City in the first place, as a pioneer in
landscape architecture and city planning. Steel City College
was one of the very first in the country, he was happy to
say, to establish a department of this kind.
President Wiswall had become reminiscent and had sup-
plied the Star's representative with interesting sidelights on
the genesis of the idea. "The very day Professor Dabney
came to town ten years ago, I took him out to the pergola
on the campus to let him see the city down below. I be-
lieve I am safe in saying that the marvelous dream of a
city that is destined to become a shining example for the
THE HILLIKIN
367
entire country had its origin definitely in that incident. It
could not be known to anybody but myself and Mr. Dabney,
and he may have forgotten the circumstances himself. But
I have not. I was greatly impressed at the time. Until he
left us to enter the World War as the first volunteer, I
believe, from Steel City, he was constantly making use
of his plans of the new city in his classroom work. Natu-
rally the college is proud to have had so large a share in
an idea of such magnitude and to have contributed to the
city proper a man of such vivid personality and vision."
Much surplus blood crowded into Giles Dabney's cheeks
and ears while he read.
"He pretty nearly did the whole thing, didn't he?" he
asked Mr. Hamill.
"Sure! He did do the whole thing. You were just a
kind of ouija board that put down what was in his mind.
Didn't you know that?"
He laughed a quiet laugh of deep gratification.
"You don't mind, do you? Let him think that if he wants
to at least till after the election is over. His friends may
be on to him a little themselves and see what he's up to.
Anyhow, let him vote with us. Let anybody vote with us
who chooses. But we must keep our own skirts clean, and
we must keep voters remembering that. It requires vigi-
lance. The sight of an honest man rather startles them.
We must help them to get adjusted to seeing him around."
Much of the effect of Dr. Wiswall's interview was de-
stroyed, however, by a longer one in the next issue of the
Herald. The Herald was pleased to publish an exclusive
interview with Mr. Cleveland Mulloney, the city treasurer,
on the probable actual cost of the new project. Mr. Mul-
loney gave an estimate, "based upon a careful study of
the facts," of the increase in the tax-rate that the com-
mittee's fantastic dream would shoulder upon the citizens
of the city. The increase would be so great that it would
368
THE HILLIKIN
simply be unbearable. Mr. Mulloney hesitated to impute
motives, but it looked to him as if somebody-perhaps
somebody whose name had never been mentioned-was ex-
pecting to get a rake-off at the city's expense.
"Backfire!" Mr. Hamill proclaimed when he read it.
"Hog-wash! He suspects we are after him. And we are.
Judge Whittlesly will be ready to tell us something sooner
or later."
Sooner or later the judge did have a wise smile and a
cautious word or two to communicate to the rest of the
committee. "It is too early to say anything yet to any-
body. But the prosecuting attorney has been conducting
an investigation of Mulloney's career as treasurer, and he
may have something to say. Mulloney might not even serve
out his term."
"Couldn't the prosecutor speed up his work?" somebody
wanted to know. "If the big event is going to come, why
not cash in on it?"
Well, anyhow the fluctuating tide seemed to be rising.
How much higher it would have to rise, they did not know.
"We've got to do more to wake up the lethargic," Mr.
Hamill declared. "We've got the professional men-virtu-
ally all of them. We've got a share of the business men.
And we've got some enthusiastic support among the workers.
But we must have more of the workers. Too many of
them have no interest-no interest in anything. Their in-
terests have been so long handled by somebody else that
they've forgotten how to have any for themselves. If the
ones who are for nobody stay at home, and the ones who
fall for Mulloney and old Williston and all their crew go
to the polls and they will-we're lost, and the sooner we
admit it the better. If they don't know how to think and
act for themselves, we've got to think for them-and then
see if they'll act. If they act often enough, they may get
to thinking again."

369
"Hear! Hear!" young Graham Ainslee called.
"It is perfectly simple," Mr. Hamill went on, "if you
approve. I've taken an option on seven halls for the last
two evenings of the week before the election. We're going
to have music, Salvation Army style. Is that agreed? Do
you approve? All right, then: the reading of letters writ-
ten by women and children in that particular neighborhood.
We'll announce that the names of the writers will be given
out at the time. And we've got the greatest collection of
photographs of the town as it is that anybody ever saw-
dirty streets, unsanitary housing, palls of smoke, congested
downtown sections, regions disfigured by mills. We're going
to show slides from these, and then drawings by the city
planner of what the town is to be some day-if the honest
people turn out and vote. We're going to show them, too,
why they must not listen to the arguments of the people
who have axes to grind. Then we'll show them that we
have none to grind ourselves, and that the time has come
when nobody who works for the city must be permitted to
have any. And then to finish off, we are going to show
them just how tremendously they in their particular com-
munity will profit by the planning; just how the region
will be made over, and how the numerous economies that
the plan will effect will offset most of the new costs. By
the end of October, we'll be fighting on even terms-I hon-
estly believe that-and by the time we are through with
these mass meetings, we may have the whole opposition on
the run.
"But do you approve?"

AREFUL, lady!" the porter importuned. "It's a

Back in the crowded corridor of the sleeping-car a ruddy
salesman begged impatiently to know what the hell was
wrong outside there that prevented people from getting off.
But when the woman just ahead of him said a bit reprov-
ingly, "Oh, it's that young crippled woman getting off," he
remembered that he had seen her in the car the night before,
and gallantly subsided into utter silence. Perhaps his cus-
tomers were not yet in their offices, anyhow.
While the passengers on the platform watched, Giles and
Mickey crossed to a cab, told the driver which luggage was
theirs, and asked to be taken to a garage that rented cars.
When they were quickly there, Giles Dabney told the
proprietor-manager what he required: "A small one-about
the smallest you've got, perhaps but with good tires and a
good engine. We'll be going over some roads that are not
much traveled."
The proprietor had just the car they were looking for,
he was sure. "New tires all around-and a rumble seat, too,
if you should have need of one."
"How much, for a week?"
"For a week?" The proprietor told him.
"And if I should decide to buy it, you'll apply the rent
to the purchase?"
"Sure. Glad to."

They were soon out of town and in a countryside bright
with mid-morning October sun.
Mickey was strangely silent. She was in a new world,
and it claimed the whole of her thought. At the sight of
hills ahead, Giles, too, was silent for a time. He was filled
with an overpowering sense of belonging here and nowhere
else. A man, after all, he thought, is closer to the soil on
which he has grown up than he can ever be to anything
else.
He gave the engine more gasoline, and the car shot ahead
with a pleasing lunge into a straight level stretch of road.
"I know the way now, you see," he remarked. He was be-
ginning to feel her silence.
She had heard much from Giles about the grim world
they were now ready to enter, and she was all eyes.
"But the wonderful maples!" she exclaimed. "And in
every pasture field! Why-they are as beautiful as the
ones in New Hampshire!"
"Of course they are!"
"Why-"
"But you had better suspend judgment until you see a
little more of what is ahead."
They climbed a long winding road to the top of a sharp
ridge, and Giles stopped the car. "I was not referring to
this," he said; "but look back. See Centerville? Here is
where I decided that some day I was going to build a city.
Old Doc Seabright whom I've told you about brought me
to town with him once. Just as we came up the hill-going
the other way, of course I saw it. It was bigger than
Rome or Paris or New York or London. See the court-
house? It was more of a dome than St. Peter's. We
climbed to the top of it, and I saw more than I did when
I went to the top of Eiffel Tower."
372
THE HILLIKIN
Mickey's face expressed interest in this as in everything
else she was seeing, but she troubled little for words. She
was too busy learning what kind of world the man she
had married had sprung from.
They drove on-down a gentle slope in a quiet road com-
pletely arched over by yellow and scarlet maples and bronze

"Oh, this is just what I've been waiting for-something
absolutely different from Harvard Square."
"Well, I guess this must be it, then. See the tipple—
where they dump the coal? And the black hole in the hill
over there where they go in for it? And that long line

let down one by one as they have need."
"But all those rows of little cabins, like garages-"
Giles could not resist the temptation to smile in superior

"You don't mean to tell me that people live in those

"Two people! You had better say a half-dozen. They

They moved on very slowly. Before they had gone far
they passed a great blue signboard bearing the words: "The
New Spirit at Work. Drive in if you like and see the latest
word in mining. The Steel City Coal Company."
They did not need to drive in. Far up the hollow they
could see a giant power-shovel ripping the hill to pieces in
order to get at the coal. Acres of hideous ridges and hillocks
of black earth and bone coal that defied vegetation spread
out in all directions on the shovel's left; and on its right a
hill that had been half gutted away stood helpless before
its implacable march. At the top of the sheer declivity
the main roots of toppling maple trees stood out in mid-air.
"Why!" Mickey stopped with the one word. She was
overwhelmed at the sight of anything so monstrous as the
shovel, anything so desolate as the earth when the shovel
was through with it. "Why, somebody-why nobody ought
to be allowed to do that to the hills."
"Of course not. It makes the shell craters of Verdun
look like mere gopher mounds. And that is the reason, too,
there is nobody living back yonder in the Company Row.
The coal is not all gone, but it takes only a half-dozen men
or so when they do it this way."
They drove on down the road that had once been yellow
but was now piked with slate and bone coal.
"I may know the people in Steel City who are doing
that," Giles reflected. "The fact is, I do. I met the Steel
City Coal Company one night at a dinner where I didn't
belong. He is rolling in wealth."
The ugliness had come as something hard and inhuman
in their idyllic pilgrimage and had interrupted its continuity.
Mickey had wanted to see the worst. But when she saw
it, it was more hideous than anything she had been able to

387
Luke answered authoritatively. He discussed this instance

"Why, it's a battle!" Mickey declared at the end of the

"That's just what it is!" Luke replied, full of conviction

lose, sooner or later-or pretty nearly always."

"Something like that, yes," Giles replied.
The next day there was much mail. Old Alexander
Williston of the First National Bank had come forward
with the opinion that the present city planner, if the evi-
dence could be unearthed, was in the employ of Sidman
Huntt. Men were asking him every day, he said, why people
in Steel City could not put two and two together and see
that Huntt was favoring the plan because the planner had
agreed to run some of the new boulevards past Huntt's
suburban holdings. Mr. Williston was careful to say that
he himself was not making the charge. He only asked the
citizens of Steel City not to take any long chances when
they went to the polls to vote. He could say, positively,
however, that mill owners who paid enormous sums in taxes
to the city meant to abandon the entire region and go where
they would not be throttled by public interference, if the
388
THE HILLIKIN
proposed plan carried at the polls. That simply meant, in
blunt words, that the city would be bankrupt.
The city was beginning to call once more.
thought he had better be getting back.

Yes, Giles
W
CHAPTER III
INTER passed; spring came; and yet another
spring. Then one afternoon Giles Dabney and
his wife stood on a rocky bluff below their house
and watched a vast, expectant assemblage directly beneath
them on the hillside. The crowd pressed eagerly, almost
worshipfully, round a stocky power-shovel which stood ready
to gouge a wide notch from the river valley along the
slope to the top of the hill and the surrounding plateau for
an arterial boulevard. The platform of the shovel had been
extended for the occasion, and a speaker's desk and micro-
phones had been set up.
The mayor of Steel City arose to speak. "It is a mo-
mentous hour for us all," he said, "for this afternoon marks
the beginning of a great realization-the realization of a
dream long cherished by every forward-looking citizen of
Steel City. Perhaps no one could say whose mind first
stirred to life the shadowy beginnings of this mighty dream.
It has always been one of the great mysteries of life to me
that ideas should be full-fledged in the minds of so many
people before anybody has given much thought to them.
They seem just to spring up from the general consciousness.
They are in the air, and we all seem to possess them at more
or less the same time.
"That has been true of this idea for a new Steel City.
For the past two or three years it has been in the mind of
us all-the men in the street and office, the women in their
homes and clubs. I think, then, that it is only fair to say
that what we are about to do is the result of the entire
city's concentrated effort-industrial, commercial, and cul-
tural.
389
390
THE HILLIKIN
"Now that we are about to break ground for the first of
the many projects that are to constitute this great civic
enterprise, I am happy to present to this splendid assemblage
of citizens some of the men who have been working to make
this dream possible. It was necessary to have funds for
the vast amount of preliminary work that had to be carried
on by the Planning Commission while the money from taxes
voted by the people was still unavailable. Some of these
men expressed their confidence in the city by lending us
whatever we needed. Others have been at work in other
ways in our behalf. Men of standing in every line of activity
represented in this region have united in this effort to make
our city the finest in America.
I
"But I must not take too much of your time myself.
find the greatest possible pleasure in presenting to you three
representatives of the city's life. First of all, I am happy
to introduce a man whom you all know, at least by reputa-
tion, as the president of one of our chief railroads, who will
speak for all the railroads entering the city."
The president arose, fingered the edge of the speaker's
desk, and assured the audience repeatedly that he was a
man of few words. He only wished to say that it had been
the policy of his road-and while he was not authorized
so to say, he felt sure, of the other roads as well-to look
far into the future to discern the needs of the people whom
railroads existed to serve. For a considerable time one of
his pet dreams had been a double tunnel under the hill
where they now stood, so that the circuitous lake front
could be left free for beautification. He doubted whether
most people fully appreciated the value of such æsthetic
influences. Now that this tunnel was included in the city's
comprehensive plan, he was happy to say that he and his
colleagues felt at liberty to undertake the building of it as
soon as funds were available.
The mayor next introduced the most important mill owner
THE HILLIKIN
391
in the city, who spoke for the industries. He wondered how
many of the people present knew that a new spirit pervaded
industrial policy. For a long time, he said, industrialists
had been agreed that the more directly an industry con-
tributes to the welfare of the people, the better it is for that
industry. For instance, some industries create dirt. These,
it ought to be logical enough, should concentrate in a region
somewhat away from where everybody has to live. He was
pleased to say, further, that as a result of trained men's
investigations it had been scientifically determined that such
a practice of concentration was more economical. But in
any event, it contributed to the happiness of the people,
which, after all, was the thing uppermost in everybody's
mind.
And then President Wiswall of Steel City College was
introduced to represent culture. He said that he was prouder
on this occasion than any railroad president or distinguished
industrialist dared be. He believed he had the right. For
he was the person who was responsible for the first vague
stirrings of this idea to which the mayor had made reference.
He did not shrink on this occasion from boasting a little of
the fact, since it was all a matter of printed record in the
college catalogues for ten years back, and could not be
successfully controverted.
He then told how he had struggled along more or less
unobserved, more or less without financial or moral support,
in maintaining a department of landscape architecture and
city planning before there was any appreciation of the
subject "I say it without bitterness"-in the city. In fact,
his department was one of the first of its kind in the
country. From that modest beginning had emerged the
germ idea that had become the thought of everyone, as the
mayor had said, and that now was to take on material form.
And, quite incidentally, he wished to remind them that his
college had contributed bodily the planner who had been
392
THE HILLIKIN
employed by the city to shape up the technical details of
the magnificent civic dream.
The mayor brought the brief meeting to a close by telling
his fellow citizens what a privilege it had been to serve a
city where such unanimity of opinion in great matters
prevailed.
He then announced that he was sure they would all wish
to stay and see the new power-shovel-itself a product of
Steel City-try its teeth on the stony hillside.
What had seemed to be only two or three high humps in
the surface of the throng suddenly disintegrated and re-
vealed trucks waiting to be loaded. As soon as the entire
crowd had oozed away a little out of danger, the shovel
sputtered, and then like a giant bulldog thrust its sharp new
teeth irresistibly into the hill. As it swung aloft two or
three cubic yards of earth and dropped it with a terrific
bump into a truck, the people roared their admiration.
Mickey broke a long silence. "How absurd!" she said.
"They do homage to everybody and everything except the
man who originated it all. Why didn't they ask you to say
something—or at least mention your name?"
"Of course it is absurd," Giles replied, ignoring her ques-
tion. "In masses people are always absurd. They develop
their absurdity, their impotence, by living on whooped-up,
dishonest emotion. Even a city planner can see that. When
a man with fresh energy comes along and they are unable
to kill him off or make him like themselves, they suck in as
much of his idea as they can use, and then imagine that it
has always been their own, and live on it.
"But who cares? I am having my chance. I know that I
am planning the city. So why worry if they find so much
pleasure in kidding themselves?"
As soon as he and Mickey were back at the house, he
rushed away to his office to spend the rest of the afternoon
on certain details of the next project to be taken up.
THE HILLIKIN
393
Every day he worked-joyously, but like a demon. He
had got a late start. He must catch up. And every evening
he hurried home to carry good news and to enjoy a calm
which unfailingly heartened him.
He was humorous; he was fantastic; he was ironic. He
had to laugh at the absurdities of the ones who had sought
to make him out absurd. Sometimes he laughed with sheer
gusto, sometimes with a trace of something that was half
contempt, half pity. The last of his hysterical fear had
gone from him. In the name of all the divine swashbucklers
and fighters of windmills and builders of utopias who had
lived before him, he pursued his way.
But who could believe that it was ever more glorious to
fight when there was no hope of winning?—that was what
he wished to have somebody tell him whenever he walked
abroad and saw one part of his dream after another be-
ginning to assume material form.
Thus with the promise of unnumbered full days ahead,
he gave himself without stint to the city, like the candle that
consumes itself while lighting the world.
THE END
